### *To our Most Holy Lord*

# POPE BENEDICT XIV.

## ANGELO MARIA, OF THE TITLE OF SAINT MARK

### *CARDINAL PRIEST QUERINI*

->*Bishop of Brescia, and Librarian of the Apostolic See.*<-

---

**T**wo years have already passed since **YOUR HOLINESS** most graciously deigned to receive the second Syriac-Latin volume <note>This refers to a "polyglot" edition where the original Syriac text is printed alongside a Latin translation for scholars</note> of the Works of **SAINT EPHREM** <note>Saint Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373 AD) was a prolific deacon, hymn-writer, and theologian from Upper Mesopotamia</note> which I offered into your hands; and now, in the same way, this other Greek-Latin volume of these same Works awaits the protection of your Divine Authority <note>original: "Numinis Tui." While this word was used for Roman gods, in this Renaissance Latin context, it is a highly stylized and respectful way to address the Pope's sacred office</note>.

<summary>This page contains a formal dedication by Cardinal Angelo Maria Querini to Pope Benedict XIV, presenting the second Greek-Latin volume of the collected works of Saint Ephrem the Syrian.</summary>
<keywords>Pope Benedict XIV, Cardinal Angelo Maria Querini, Saint Ephrem the Syrian, Vatican Library, Brescia, Syriac-Latin edition, Greek-Latin edition, 18th-century dedication</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory front matter of the work, specifically the Dedicatory Letter to the Pope. It establishes a historical link between the current editor's work and the first Latin translations of St. Ephrem by the Renaissance humanist Ambrogio Traversari.</meta>

->viii<-
->### DEDICATORY LETTER.<-

*I have many reasons to hope for the best regarding this work, especially your provident care—most worthy of a Roman Pontiff—in fostering literary labors of every kind, as well as your singular kindness toward me. But there is one reason besides, which these very days in which I write most opportunely present to me. Embracing this reason most gladly above others—which usually fill up every page in dedicatory letters of this kind, often to the point of wearying patrons <note>The original Latin refers to "Maecenatum," invoking Maecenas, the famous Roman patron of the arts</note>—I shall immediately explain what it is.*

*He who first of all admitted* **ST. EPHRAEM** <note>Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373 AD), a prolific Syriac-language hymnwriter and theologian</note> *within the borders of Latium <note>A region of central Italy, here meaning the Latin-speaking world or Rome specifically</note> was Ambrose, a Camaldolese Monk <note>Ambrogio Traversari (1386–1439), a famous scholar and General of the Camaldolese Order who translated Greek patristic works into Latin</note>, a man of great name and General Superior of his entire Order. He explains the matter, just as it happened, in these words in a letter to the most celebrated Cosimo de' Medici <note>original: "Cosmam Medicum," the de facto ruler of Florence and a great patron of learning</note>:*

> *I recently encountered a traveler, arrived (as they said) from Syria to us; he was advanced in age and tall in stature, but now bowed with years, with a peaceful and comely countenance. In his very appearance and dress, he displayed the marks of sanctity, his eyes filled with tears: but in such a way that no dignity seemed taken from his face, and much authority and grace seemed added to it.*

*Afterwards he adds that, having found such a man and being captured by such grace and dignity of face, he approached him more closely and noticed that he was skilled in the Greek tongue <note>original: "Graecae locutionis gnarum"</note>. Then indeed, with greater constancy, he threw himself into his embrace and shared holy kisses; and taking his right hand, not with many pray-* <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "cibus," which completes the word "precibus" or "prayers" on the following page</note>

<meta>catchword: prayers</meta>

<summary>The author justifies his dedication to the Pope by citing the historical precedent of Ambrogio Traversari, who first introduced St. Ephrem's writings to Italy. It features a vivid description of an encounter with a holy man from Syria who represented the spirit of Ephrem.</summary>
<keywords>St. Ephrem the Syrian, Ambrogio Traversari, Cosimo de' Medici, Camaldolese Order, Syria, Latin translation, Church Fathers, patronage</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dedicatory preface, likely written by a 16th or 17th-century editor of the works of St. Ephrem the Syrian. The previous page presumably introduced the scholar Ambrogio Traversari (Ambrosius) and his encounter with Ephrem's writings.</meta>

<page-num>ix</page-num>
->### DEDICATORY EPISTLE<-

<meta>catchword: from</meta>

*From whom he <note>Referring to Ambrogio Traversari, a famous 15th-century humanist and monk</note> obtained, not against his will, that he <note>The spirit or works of Ephrem the Syrian</note> should visit his small cell and enjoy his hospitality for some time; and finally, he led him to learn to speak in Latin as well, for the convenience and comfort of our people. "Good God!" (he said) "what joy, what comfort, and how much profit I received from the mouth of such an extraordinary guest, when he addressed me for many days and nights in continuous speech! Nothing can be said or imagined more pleasing than his appearance, more wholesome than his teaching, or dearer, more pleasant, or more joyful than his company." Ambrosius pursues these matters more extensively in that most elegant Letter, and finally commends that guest of his to Cosmas <note>Cosimo de' Medici (1389–1464), the de facto ruler of Florence and a great patron of learning</note>, so that he might receive him gratefully and reverently, and hold him in honor, and attend to him with every duty and veneration.*

*You surely see by now, **MOST BLESSED FATHER** <note>Addressing the Pope</note>, where these things lead: namely, that I feel I can be led into the most justified hope that the marks of venerable pilgrimage—which first won for **EPHREM** <note>St. Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373 AD), a prolific Syriac-language hymnwriter and theologian</note> coming from Mesopotamia to Florence the benevolence and reverence of Abbot Ambrosius, then of the most illustrious Cosmas, and finally of all Latins—might themselves obtain from **YOUR HOLINESS** signs of Pontifical favor for the Volume that I now offer to You, which encompasses the Works of such an extraordinary and divine Writer. You will also easily understand by what right these things that occurred in these days—or those which You Yourself performed the day before yesterday <note>original: "nudius tertius"</note> toward another illustrious pilgrim—instill this confidence in me in a greater measure.*

<summary>The author compares his new edition of St. Ephrem’s works to a visiting dignitary, recalling how Ambrogio Traversari previously introduced Ephrem to Cosimo de' Medici. He asks the Pope for his official blessing and patronage of the volume.</summary>

<keywords>St. Ephrem the Syrian, Ambrogio Traversari, Cosimo de' Medici, Mesopotamia, Florence, Papal patronage, Latin translation, Church Fathers</keywords>

# DEDICATORY EPISTLE

->...from equally distant Eastern regions to Rome, so that he might present himself at Your feet and show the obedience owed to the Supreme Head of the Catholic Church. I speak of the Patriarch of the Armenians, whom you—having previously honored and refreshed him with many signs of your paternal charity—decided should be received most honorably in the Consistory of your Brothers, the Cardinals of the Holy Roman Church <term>S. R. E.: Sanctae Romanae Ecclesiae, the Holy Roman Church</term>. You did this so that a lasting monument of Pontifical benevolence and esteem toward the Eastern Nations who profess Catholic dogmas might be passed down for the memory of future generations. These are the things, MOST BLESSED FATHER <note>A formal title for the Pope</note>, which give rightful cause for hope to the most holy elder EPHREM <note>Saint Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373 AD), a prolific Syriac-language hymnwriter and theologian</note> as he approaches You. As you know, Ephrem is celebrated as a citizen of Nisibis, a Deacon of Edessa, and the "Prophet of the Syrians." Even Greece, though deeply devoted to its own teachers, boasts of him as a Master. Basil the Great and his brother, Saint Gregory <note>Saint Gregory of Nyssa</note>, raised him to the heavens with the highest praise. Finally, Jerome <note>Saint Jerome, the primary translator of the Latin Vulgate Bible</note> testifies that his works, having been translated into the Greek language, were customarily read in the churches immediately after the reading of the Holy Scriptures.<-

->Finally, MOST BLESSED FATHER, even your own birthplace, which you share with Pope Gregory XIII <note>Ugo Boncompagni, Pope from 1572–1585; both he and the recipient of this letter, presumably Benedict XIV or Clement XII, were from Bologna</note>, is a reason for EPHREM to present himself to You with even greater confidence. For it was during that Gregory’s papacy—and whether by his persuasion or his command—that Gerard Vossius, the Provost of Tongeren, devoted extraordinary effort to translating Ephrem's works from the Greek into the Latin language, from which there emerged...<-

<header>DEDICATORY LETTER.</header>

## DEDICATORY LETTER.

...finished edition of these same works, none of which until this Vatican edition of ours has been either more complete or more distinguished. Therefore, moved also by the example of that most illustrious citizen and your most holy predecessor, may **YOUR HOLINESS** deign to grant your patronage to this present volume, which I humbly request. At the same time, I shall strive to imitate the example of two Librarians of the Holy Roman Church <note>original: "S. R. E." standing for Sanctae Romanae Ecclesiae</note>, namely Sirleto and Carafa, whom Vossius <note>Gerardus Vossius (1577–1649), a famous scholar who edited the works of the Church Fathers</note> found to be the best of patrons when he undertook that labor. I also follow the example of my fellow citizen Agostino Valier, Cardinal of San Marco, whose outstanding devotion toward the writings of so great a Father is shown by several ancient editions of them dedicated to him. I take up this care in a manner fitting for the Bishop of Brescia <note>The Latin "Brixiano Antistiti" refers to the Bishop of Brescia, a city in Lombardy</note>, since it was the pride of that city’s printing houses to have been, if not the very first as some have thought, then at least among the first to publish those divine writings. Therefore, let those learned men who manage the Vatican Library under my presidency know for certain that—whether I am present in Rome or residing in Brescia—they will not lack the encouragement to bring the new edition to completion more quickly than they have done thus far. To this end, although the power of the Apostolic Librarian <term>Apostolic Librarian: The head of the Vatican Library, one of the most prestigious scholarly positions in the Catholic Church</term> over any of the keepers, writers, and ministers of that same library is great, I nevertheless also implore the help of **YOUR HOLINESS**, while most humbly kissing your feet.

->Rome, November 27, 1742.<- <note>original: "V. Kal. Decembr. M. D CC XLII." Using the Roman calendar, the 5th day before the Kalends of December is November 27th.</note>

<summary>In this concluding part of the dedication, the author invokes the history of the Vatican Library and the printing tradition of Brescia to request the Pope's formal patronage for a new edition of sacred texts. He pledges to use his authority as Librarian to ensure the project's swift completion.</summary>
<keywords>Vatican Library, Pope Benedict XIV, Brescia, printing history, Guglielmo Sirleto, Antonio Carafa, Agostino Valier, 1742, Apostolic Librarian, patristics</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page serves as an introductory or frontispiece element, likely for an 18th-century edition of the works of St. Ephrem the Syrian, such as the one printed in Rome by Giovanni Maria Salvioni (1732–1746).</meta>

<note>This text is an excerpt from the "Encomium on Saint Ephrem" written by Saint Gregory of Nyssa (c. 335–395 AD), one of the Cappadocian Fathers. It celebrates the legacy of Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373 AD), a prominent Syriac Christian deacon, hymnwriter, and theologian.</note>

That Ephrem <note>original: Ἐφραῒμ ἐκεῖνον</note>, who is carried upon the lips of all Christians. I speak of Ephrem the Syrian. For I am not ashamed of his race, in whose ways I take pride; Ephrem, the radiance of whose life and contemplation <note>term: contemplation (θεωρίας); in a theological context, this refers to the spiritual vision or insight into divine mysteries</note> has shone forth into all the earth; who is known by almost everyone under the sun, and recognized by as many as know that great luminary of the Church, Basil <note>Referring to Saint Basil the Great, a contemporary of Ephrem</note>. Ephrem, the truly spiritual Euphrates <note>A wordplay in the original Greek: the name Ephrem and the river Euphrates share a root suggesting "fruitfulness"</note> of the Church, from whom the fullness of the faithful is watered, yielding a hundredfold the seed of faith. Ephrem, the fruitful vine of God, who like sweet clusters of grapes has blossomed with the fruits of teaching, gladdening the children of the Church with the abundance of divine love. Ephrem, the good and faithful steward <note>term: steward (οἰκονόμος); an official responsible for managing a household or estate, used here as a metaphor for a teacher of divine grace</note> of grace, who distributes the words of virtue in due proportion to his fellow servants, and manages his Master's household excellently.

->From the Encomium of Saint Gregory of Nyssa, Bishop, regarding SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN,<-
->wherefrom the Syrian nation may learn to faithfully guard the deposit entrusted to it by the Holy Doctor.<-

---

->**GUARD THE DEPOSIT**<-
->*To Timothy, Book 1, Chapter 6* <note>Referring to 1 Timothy 6:20: "O Timothy, guard the deposit entrusted to you"</note><-

Rome, from the Press of Giovanni Maria Salvioni &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; C. Fery engraved [this]

<meta>The transcription on this page follows the typical front matter of an 18th-century scholarly publication—the official ecclesiastical approvals (Approbationes) required for printing. These texts verify that the edition of Saint Ephrem the Syrian’s works is both doctrinally sound and scientifically rigorous. This page follows the title or dedication pages (likely marked with previous signatures) and precedes the main body of the work.</meta>

# APPROVAL

**S**aint Ephrem the Syrian’s <note>original: "Sancti Ephræmi Syri"</note> writings have flourished in the Catholic Church with such great fame from the earliest centuries even until our own times, and have been (and are now) extolled with such high praise by everyone, that it seems entirely unnecessary to add my own testimony or recommendation, of which they have no need at all. Rather, one must praise the care and concern of the Most Eminent Librarian of the Holy Roman Church in promoting their publication, and the zeal and learning of the Most Illustrious Prelate and my most beloved colleague, Joseph Simon Assemani <note>A famous Lebanese Maronite scholar (1687–1768) who served as the head of the Vatican Library and was instrumental in bringing Eastern Christian texts to Europe.</note>, in preparing this edition and enriching it with various scholarly and truly laborious studies. Therefore, it is entirely fitting that a work so distinguished—so greatly desired by learned men, yet never before hoped for—should be brought to light, since there is nothing in it that is contrary to our Religion and holy morals.

->From the Vatican Library, March 7, in the Year of Salvation 1743.<-
->*Giovanni Bottari.*<- <note>Bottari (1689–1775) was a prominent Italian scholar and counselor to the Pope.</note>

---

# APPROVAL

**O**beying the commands of the Most Reverend Father Luigi Ridolfi, Master of the Sacred Apostolic Palace, I have reviewed the second volume of the Works of Saint Ephrem the Syrian. In this volume, many works of the same Holy Man are brought to light in Greek based on the Vatican Codices <note>Handwritten manuscript books.</note>, and have been translated into Latin by the most distinguished Prelate Joseph Simon Assemani, first Custodian of the Vatican Library. Prefixed to these are Assemani’s Introductions <term>Prolegomena</term> <note>Lengthy scholarly introductions that provide historical and critical context.</note>, in which he refutes—with as much eloquence as vigor—the slanders of those who accuse Ephrem’s Latin translators of both unreliable faith and laziness. Indeed, some have even dared to characterize several of Ephrem’s Sermons as counterfeit and fabricated. For a long time, almost all learned men have not only desired but even demanded that Ephrem's works—some of which had previously appeared only in Latin—should be made available to the public in Greek as well, recovered from the Vatican Codices. There is reason, therefore, to give thanks first to Cardinal Angelo Maria Quirini, Librarian of the Holy Roman Church, under whose primary authority and leadership this much-longed-for edition of Ephrem was managed; and then to the most distinguished Assemani, who undertook the preparation of this edition with great diligence and labor. For who does not know the incomparable zeal of the most honorable Cardinal Quirini in enriching the "Republic of Letters" <note>The international community of intellectuals and scholars.</note> almost daily, partly through his own genius and partly through the monuments of the ancient Fathers, which he has remarkably illustrated? Thus, those who read Ephrem will not only feel themselves more inflamed toward piety, but will also gradually perceive many things pertaining to sacred dogmas, ancient discipline, and the institutions of the Monastics <term>Coenobites</term> <note>From the Greek 'koinos bios' (common life), referring to monks who live in a community rather than as hermits.</note>. Those who carefully consult Assemani’s studies will understand that Ephrem has not only been most abundantly clarified, but also tirelessly defended from slanders. I have, therefore, no reason to doubt that the individual Treatises <term>Syntagmata</term> <note>A Greek term used here for systematic collections of writings.</note> of Ephrem contained in this volume, and the learned introductions by Assemani, should be printed and safely read, because I found nothing in them that opposes the Catholic faith and the integrity of morals; on the contrary, new arguments are drawn from them to support the dogmas of the faith and the discipline of morals.

->Given at Rome, March 13, in the Year of Our Lord 1743.<-
->*Domenico Giorgi.*<- <note>Giorgi (1690–1747) was an Italian priest, antiquarian, and scholar.</note>

<summary>This page contains two official ecclesiastical approvals for the publication of Saint Ephrem the Syrian's works, certifying the book's moral fitness and scholarly merit while praising the editors, Joseph Simon Assemani and Cardinal Quirini.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Ephrem the Syrian, Joseph Simon Assemani, Vatican Library, Cardinal Angelo Maria Quirini, ecclesiastical approval, Greek manuscripts, Latin translation, monasticism, 18th-century scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page serves as the formal opening to the second volume of the works of St. Ephrem the Syrian, following the first volume published under Pope Clement XII. The author, Assemani, establishes his goal to address scholarly criticisms and expand the catalog of Ephrem’s writings.</meta>

ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHRAEM THE SYRIAN.
<a>page i</a>
<meta>Volume II.</meta>
<meta>catchword: CA-</meta>

# JOSEPH SIMON ASSEMANI
### FIRST CUSTODIAN OF THE VATICAN LIBRARY
## ON THE SECOND VOLUME
## GREEK AND LATIN
# OF SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN
## P R E F A C E.

When in the first volume—which appeared under the auspices of Pope Clement XII <note>Clement XII (r. 1730–1740) was a patron of the arts and sciences who oversaw the expansion of the Vatican Library's collection.</note>—I first reviewed the Latin, Greek, and Syriac works of **SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN** which had been published in print up to that day; and then when I cited the Greek manuscript Codices preserved in the most famous libraries of Europe, from which I believe I presented an accurate catalog of nearly all Ephrem’s Sermons: I thought nothing else remained for preparing and completing this edition than to vigorously apply my hand to translating into Latin <note>original: "Latio donandis"</note> those works which remained in the Greek Oxford edition. <note>This refers to the 1709 edition of Ephrem's Greek works published at Oxford by Edward Thwaites.</note> With these translations having already been completed in this Second Volume, by God’s favor, I intended to pursue the rest in the Third and final volume—those which had never before appeared in Greek, or the greatest part of which had escaped the diligence of Ambrogio <note>Ambrogio Traversari (1386–1439), a famous humanist translator of Greek patristic texts.</note>, Zino, and Vossius <note>Gerardus Vossius (1577–1649), a prolific scholar who edited Ephrem's works in Latin.</note>, who were otherwise most studious translators of Ephrem.

But as soon as I finished the work I had begun and consulted the writings of certain more recent authors—in which they set forth their opinions on the aforementioned translators and on various Greek and Latin Ephremic writings—I truly realized that I had neither included everything that is known from printed records to exist in libraries as commentaries of this most holy Syrian Father; nor had I sufficiently defended those same translators, or the Greek or Latin manuscripts <term>Codices</term> <note>Codices are ancient or medieval manuscript books, as opposed to scrolls or printed books.</note> which both they and I used, from undeserved criticism <note>original: "censura"</note> by providing the testimonies of libraries and learned men. Therefore, I think I will be doing something consistent with our purpose if I undertake to clear the reputation of those who have served the Republic of Letters so well by translating Ephrem’s Greek sermons into Latin, wiping away every stain of alleged falsehood or negligence; and if I establish the reliability of the Greek and Latin manuscripts; and finally, if I report one by one from the most trustworthy writers whatever other works of the Holy Father I have learned are preserved elsewhere.

<summary>In this preface, Joseph Assemani introduces the second volume of St. Ephrem the Syrian's works, outlining his plan to translate remaining Greek texts into Latin and defend earlier scholars from academic criticism.</summary>
<keywords>Joseph Simon Assemani, St. Ephrem the Syrian, Vatican Library, Pope Clement XII, patristics, Greek manuscripts, Latin translations, Ambrogio Traversari, Gerardus Vossius</keywords>

II
A 2
<meta>Catchword: wretched</meta>

# CHAPTER I.
## SERMONS OF SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN,
### Which exist in Greek in the Libraries of Saint Mark in Venice, Passionea, Vallicellana, and Moscow.

---

## I. CATALOGUE OF THE SERMONS OF SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN,
### Which exist in the Greek Manuscript Books <term>Codicibus Mss. (Codices Manuscripti) are hand-written books rather than printed ones.</term> of the Library of Saint Mark in Venice.

**C**odex LI [51]. In folio <term>folio refers to a large book size made by folding sheets of paper or parchment only once.</term>, made of parchment <term>membranaceus</term>, consisting of 411 leaves. From approximately the 10th century. *Published in Venice in the year 1740, at the shop of Simon Occhi, Bookseller*. Page 32.
Works of Saint Ephrem the Syrian.
1. Letter to John the Monk concerning patience, etc. Beginning: <note>original Greek: "πολλὰ παρανέσαντες τὸν εὐλαβέστατον Θεόδωρον"</note> Having exhorted the most devout Theodore many times...
2. 4 Exhortations on virtue to a novice monk. <note>original Greek: "ὁ Κύριος ἐπὶ σωτηρίᾳ τῶν ἀνθρώπων ἐπιδημήσας τῷ κόσμῳ"</note> The Lord, having visited the world for the salvation of mankind...
3. 10 Chapters on Virtue. The first chapter begins: <note>original Greek: "μακαρία ἡ πόλις ἡ ὑπὸ εὐσεβῶν βασιλευομένη"</note> Blessed is the city ruled by the godly.
4. On that passage of the Gospel: "Take heed to yourself," 12 Chapters. The prologue begins: <note>original Greek: "ὀφειλέται ἐσμὲν, ἀγαπητοὶ, νουθετεῖν κ᾿ παρακαλεῖν εἰς τὸν ἕνα"</note> We are debtors, beloved, to admonish and encourage one another.
5. That one should not laugh, nor boast, but should mourn and weep for oneself. <note>original Greek: "Ἀρχὴ καταστροφῆς ψυχῆς μοναχοῦ"</note> The beginning of the destruction of a monk's soul.
6. Advice on the spiritual life to a novice monk, 93 Chapters. <note>original Greek: "ἐπήνεσά σου τὴν πίστιν καὶ τὴν σπουδήν"</note> I have praised your faith and your diligence.
<margin>A</margin> 7. 89 Chapters on the Upright Life. (In the Latin edition of Gerard Vossius <note>Gerardus Vossius (1577–1649) was a Dutch scholar who produced a famous edition of Ephrem's works.</note>, 91 Chapters are counted). Beginning: <note>original Greek: "ὀρθῷ βίῳ ὀρέγῃ· ταπεινοφροσύνην ἄσκησον"</note> You desire an upright life; practice humility.
8. Beatitudines <term>Latin for "Blessings," modeled after the "Blessed are..." sayings of Jesus.</term>. <note>original Greek: "μακάριος ὃς γέγονεν ὅλος ἐλεύθερος ἐν κυρίῳ"</note> Blessed is he who has become entirely free in the Lord.
9. Other beatitudes. <note>original Greek: "μακάριος ὃς μισήσας κατέλιπε τῷ βίῳ τούτῳ"</note> Blessed is he who, having hated this life, has left it.
10. 49 Exhortations to monks, <margin>B</margin> numbered in the margin: (In the Vossius edition, 50). <note>original Greek: "δόξα σοι ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν"</note> Glory to You, our God.
11. 100 Chapters on the way to acquire humility. <note>original Greek: "ἀρχὴ καρποφορίας ἄνθος"</note> The beginning of fruitfulness is the flower.
12. On the perfection which is fitting for a monk to possess. <note>original Greek: "πέρυσί μοι προσῆλθόν τινες αἰτοῦντες"</note> Last year some people came to me asking...
13. On divine grace. <margin>C</margin> <note>original Greek: "σπεῦδε ἔχειν θείαν χάριν"</note> Hasten to possess divine grace.
14. On the disease of the tongue, and other vices. <note>original Greek: "ἐν παροιμίαις εἰσὶν αἱ ἀναπαύσεις πᾶσαι"</note> In proverbs are all resting-places <note>The Greek term "anapauseis" here likely refers to rhetorical pauses or spiritual comforts found in the text.</note>.
15. Sermons on virtues and vices. Beginning of the prologue: <note>original Greek: "μακαρίζω τὴν ὑμῶν ζωήν"</note> I bless your life.
16. On the fear of God. <note>original Greek: "μακάριος ὁ ἄνθρωπος ἐκεῖνος ὁ ἔχων ἀγάπην"</note> Blessed is that man who has love. (Two chapters are missing: see on him who does not have the fear of God, and on charity, or love).
17. On him who does not have charity <term>In this context, 'charity' refers to the theological concept of selfless love (caritas).</term>.

<summary>This page provides a detailed catalog of 17 Greek sermons attributed to Saint Ephrem the Syrian, as found in a 10th-century parchment manuscript (Codex 51) held at the Library of Saint Mark in Venice. The list covers various ascetic topics including humility, the spiritual life of monks, and the nature of virtue and vice.</summary>

<page-num>III</page-num>

-># ON THE WORKS OF SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN.<-

<margin>A</margin> ...wretched and miserable is he who is far from love. <note>original: "ἄθλιος καὶ ταλαίπωρος ὁ τῆς ἀγάπης μακρὰν ὑπάρχων." This appears to be the conclusion of a previous entry regarding Christian love.</note>

18. **On long-suffering.** Truly blessed is that man who has acquired long-suffering. <note>original: "De longanimitate. μακάριος ἀληθῶς ὁ ἄνθρωπος ἐκεῖνος ὅστις μακροθυμίαν ἐκτήσατο."</note>

19. **On him who does not have long-suffering.** He who does not have long-suffering...

20. **On patience.** Blessed, brothers, is he who has acquired patience. <note>The term used here is <term>hypomonē</term> (endurance/fortitude), distinct from the "long-suffering" in section 18.</note>

<margin>B</margin>
21. **On him who does not have patience.** Wretched and miserable is he who has not acquired patience.

22. **On kindness.** Blessed is that man who is not easily provoked to anger.

23. **On irascibility.** But he who is always mastered by a sharp temper, both frequently and quickly...

<margin>C</margin>
24. **On wickedness.** It is necessary then, brothers, to lament and mourn over those who do not have gentleness <gloss>missing [text]: on gentleness; "blessed etc. is he who has gentleness."</gloss> <note>The editor notes a missing section (lacuna) in the manuscript regarding the virtue of gentleness.</note>

25. **On truth.** Blessed is he who has ordered his life in truth, and has not been caught in any lie.

26. **On falsehood.** Wretched and miserable is he who persists in every lie because from the beginning... <note>Likely a reference to the devil being the "father of lies" from the beginning.</note>

<margin>D</margin>
27. **On obedience.** Blessed is he who has submitted and maintained true obedience.

28. **On disobedience and grumbling.** Cursed and miserable is he who has not acquired obedience.

29. **On him who is free from envy and jealousy.** Blessed is he who has not been subjected to envy and jealousy.

<margin>E</margin>
30. **On envy and jealousy.** But he who wounds himself in these things...

31. **On him who is a stranger to curses and insults.** Blessed and thrice-blessed is he who [guards] his tongue...

32. **On those who are slanderous and insulting.** How evident it is that he who delights in the slandering of others...

<margin>F</margin>
33. **On temperance.** Truly blessed and thrice-blessed is he who has guarded self-control. <note>The Greek term <term>enkrateia</term> refers to the mastery of one's desires and appetites.</note>

34. **On intemperance.** But he who is incontinent and does not possess self-control...

35. **Self-reproach and confession.** Brothers, have compassion for me, having tender mercies...

---

36. **Toward the overthrow of pride.** Vain is all ascetic practice, all self-control, all submission [without humility].

37. **A Discourse on compunction of soul.** Come, beloved, come my fathers and brothers, the chosen flock of the Father. <note>The editor notes: "This is Discourse IV on this subject in the Vossius edition," referring to Gerardus Vossius's 1589 publication of Ephrem's works.</note>

38. **An ascetic discourse (on the religious and monastic life).** The labor of speaking compels [me].

39. **In imitation of the Proverbs. The Prologue begins.** He who believes in the Son of God...

40. **On the fear of God.** Fear the Lord.

41. **For the correction of those who live wickedly and seek honors.** I am in anguish, my brothers, and I shudder.

42. **Self-reproach and confession.** Seeming to be useful to many of you, brothers...

43. **On the passions of the soul.** I wish to speak before your glory, Christ Savior.

44. **On compunction.** Giver of all good things. <note>This is noted as Discourse III on <term>compunction</term> (the spiritual sorrow or "pricking of the heart" for one's sins).</note>

45. **On compunction of soul.** One of these days at dawn, having risen, I was walking with two brothers outside the city... <note>Noted as Discourse I.</note>

45. <note>Note: The original text repeats the number 45.</note> **On compunction.** Be pricked in heart, my soul. <note>Noted as Discourse II.</note>

47. **An exhortatory discourse on the text: "Give heed to yourself."** Give heed to yourself, O youth.

48. **On the holy Fathers, who were perfected in their own times.** My heart is in pain; have compassion, brothers.

49. **Another on the holy Fathers, who having died at that time, had found rest in holy peace.** On this first day...

50. **From the same Discourse, a four-syllable Prayer.** The wicked devil having been utterly defeated... <note>The term "tetrasyllaba" refers to a specific rhythmic meter used in the Greek text.</note>

51. **A seven-syllable Discourse.** Knowing then, brothers, our own weakness, let us take heed to ourselves. <note>Referencing "heptasyllabus" meter.</note>

52. **On the fear of the soul.** I, Ephrem the sinner, am weak and lazy.

53. **A prayer from the teaching of Saint Ephrem.** I have sinned against heaven and before you, Lord God.

54. **An admonitory prayer,** Remind...

<sig>a 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: ...ing [original Greek: κόν.]</meta>
<meta>Tom. II. [Volume 2] at bottom left</meta>

->PROLEGOMENA<-
<note>Introductory remarks</note>

...[concerning] the spiritual. Opening words <note>original: "Incipit"</note>: At every hour I have in mind the <unclear>uncreatedness</unclear>.

55. How the soul must return to God with tears in time of temptation. Opening: A distressed soul comes to you.

56. On the Judgment. Come all brothers, hear the advice of me, the sinful and unlearned Ephrem.

57. On patience. He who wishes to please God.

58. On blessings and miseries. Blessed are those who have loved God.

59. Confession or prayer to God. Heal me, Lord, and I shall be healed.

60. On the second coming of our Lord Jesus Christ. Approach and come, sons of light.

61. On faith. The Lord in the Gospel [taught] his own disciples.

**Codex LII.** in folio <note>a large format where the paper is folded once</note>, vellum <note>original: "membranaceus"; animal skin parchment</note>, 218 leaves. Circa the 10th century. Page 34.

The works of St. Ephrem the Syrian <note>a prominent 4th-century Christian theologian and hymnodist</note>, preceded by his *Life*, which is attributed to an Anonymous author in the Preface <note>original: "Prolegomenis"</note> of the most excellent Roman Edition of 1732. In this Codex it ends with these words: "Ephrem the Syrian": in the same Roman Edition many more things follow.

1. On the fear of the soul. Opening: I, Ephrem, a sinner, unintelligent, lazy, and always sluggish, introducing...

2. 48 Exhortations to monks. Glory to you, O God, and again I say, glory to you.

3. 100 Chapters on the way to acquire humility. The beginning of fruit-bearing is the flower.

4. An ascetic <note>relating to spiritual self-discipline</note> and hortatory sermon to the brothers. Brothers, let us long for blessings, that we may inherit blessings.

5. On judgment and compunction <note>remorse or spiritual sorrow</note>. Come all brothers, hear the advice of me, the sinful and unlearned Ephrem.

6. An ascetic sermon, useful for the ascetic life. Opening: Those who wish in truth to give themselves to the Lord for the future...

7. On patience. He who wishes to please
<insert>A</insert> God and to become an heir of Christ through faith.

8. Blessings. Blessed is the man who, having hated...

9. On blessings and miseries. Blessed are those who have loved God, and because of that love...

10. That we should not laugh, nor boast, but mourn and weep for ourselves. <insert>B</insert> The beginning of the destruction of a monk's soul is laughter and excessive boldness.

11. On those who sin daily, and daily do penance. How long, O friend, will you tolerate the enemy...

12. On the (second) coming of the Lord. Approach and come, sons of light.

13. On the second coming of the Lord, and On the Judgment. <insert>C</insert> Come all my brothers, hear the advice of me, the sinner.

14. On virginity. Paul the Apostle...

15. On repentance and patience. Who then considered the present life to be a fleeing thing?

16. On repentance. <insert>D</insert> He who descended from the bosom of the Father and emptied himself for us.

17. For the correction of those who live wickedly and seek honors. I am in agony, my brothers, and I shudder.

18. * Encomium <note>a speech or piece of writing that highly praises someone</note> on those living in a monastery. A paradise full of the fruits of joy and various flowers.

19. On humility and kindness of response. <insert>E</insert> Like marrow and honeycomb in the mouth. This belongs to the Admonitions <note>original: "Parænefes"</note> to monks.

20. On worldly cares and business. Like a bond and a chain to an eagle, so is the care of the world to the monk. Found among the Admonitions to monks.

21. On the proud mind, and envy of works, and jealousy, and about moving from one place to another. <insert>F</insert> Just as a tall tree with a beautiful... Found among the Admonitions to monks.

22. That Christians must abstain from frivolous things. That Christians should not play or trifle is something we all know.

23. On the Virtues. Prologue. I call your life blessed, O lovers of Christ.

24. On the fear of God. Blessed is the man...

<page-num>VI</page-num>
<header>PREFACE</header>

# PREFACE

<margin>A</margin>
...then, we received as a loan from the Sforza Library <note>original: "Sfortiana Bibliotheca"; the library of the noble Sforza family in Italy</note>, where it is found under number 1. We have now transcribed it in its entirety and compared it with several Vatican manuscripts.

1. *The Letter of our Holy Father Ephrem to John the monk regarding patience, etc.*
**Beginning.** Having inquired much of the most devout Theodore... <note>original Greek: "πολλὰ πυνθάνεσαντες τὴν εὐλαβέστατον Θεόδωρον"</note> Page 1.

<margin>B</margin>
2. *Four Exhortations* <note>original Latin: "Adhortationes"</note> *on virtue for a novice monk.* The Lord, for the salvation of mankind... <note>original Greek: "ὁ Κύριος ἐπὶ σωτηρίᾳ τῶν ἀνθρώπων"</note> page 6.

3. *Ten Chapters on Virtue.* I considered it necessary... <note>original Greek: "ἀναγκαῖον ἡγησάμην"</note> Chapter I. Blessed is the city. <note>original Greek: "μακαρία πόλις"</note> page 21.

<margin>C</margin>
4. *Twelve Chapters on the text: "Attend to yourself."* Prologue. We are debtors. <note>original Greek: "ὀφειλέται ἐσμέν"</note> Chapter I. Listen, beloved. <note>original Greek: "Ἄκουε ἀγαπητέ"</note> page 34.

5. *That one ought not to laugh or be puffed up, but rather to weep and mourn.* The beginning of the destruction of a monk's soul... <note>original Greek: "ἀρχὴ καταστροφῆς ψυχῆς μοναχῷ"</note> page 58.

6. *Counsel on the spiritual life for a novice monk, in ninety-two chapters.* I have praised your faith... <note>original Greek: "ἐπαινέσα σου τὴν πίστιν"</note> page 62.

<margin>D</margin>
7. *On the upright life, in eighty-nine chapters.* Do you long for the upright life? <note>original Greek: "ὀρθῷ βίῳ ὀρέγῃ;"</note> page 79.

8. *The Beatitudes.* Blessed is he who has become entirely free. <note>original Greek: "μακάριος ὃς γέγονεν ὅλος ἐλεύθερος"</note> page 89.

9. *Other Beatitudes.* Blessed is he who, having hated this life, left it. <note>original Greek: "μακάριος ὃς μισήσας κατέλιπε τὸν βίον τοῦτον"</note> page 95. These continue from the previous ones.

<margin>E</margin>
10. *Fifty-one <term>Paraeneses</term> <note>A Paraenesis is a formal moral exhortation or piece of advice.</note> or exhortations to monks,* the first of which begins: Glory to you, O God. <note>original Greek: "δόξα σοι ὁ Θεός"</note> page 100.

11. *Exhortation II.* Beloved, you are a Christian through the grace of God... <note>original Greek: "ἀγαπητέ, Χριστιανὸς εἶ διὰ τῆς χάριτος τοῦ Θεοῦ"</note> page 101.

* 12. *Exhortation III.* Brother, if you enter to struggle... <note>original Greek: "ἀδελφέ, ἐὰν εἰσέλθῃς ἀγωνίσασθαι"</note> page 102.

13. *Exhortation IV.* Beloved, having renounced the world... <note>original Greek: "ἀγαπητέ, ἀποταξάμενος τῷ βίῳ"</note> page 103.

<margin>F</margin>
14. *Exhortation V.* Beloved, behold I set before you... <note>original Greek: "ἀγαπητέ, ἰδοὺ ἀπατίθημί σοι"</note> page 105.

15. *Exhortation V (repeated numbering):* If someone from a great reputation... <note>original Greek: "ἐάν τις ἀπὸ μεγάλης ἀπολήψεως"</note> page 106. From here, the first Exhortation seems to take the place of a Prologue.

16. *Exhortation VI.* Beloved, if you cast... <note>original Greek: "ἀγαπητέ, ἐὰν βάλλῃς"</note> page 106.

17. *Exhortation VII. And concerning the work of one’s hands.* <note>original Greek: "καὶ περὶ τοῦ ἐργοχείρου"</note> page 107.

18. *Exhortation VIII. Concerning the scandals that occur.* <note>original Greek: "περὶ τῶν συμβαινόντων σκανδάλων"</note> page 108.

19. *Exhortation IX. And concerning time.* <note>original Greek: "καὶ περὶ τοῦ χρόνου"</note> page 109.

20. *Exhortation X.* Brother, do not be disheartened by the thoughts that come upon you. <note>original Greek: "ἀδελφέ, μὴ ἐκλύου ὑπὸ ἐπερχομένων λογισμῶν"</note> page 110.

21. *Exhortation XI.* Brother, struggle like a good soldier. <note>original Greek: "ἀδελφέ, ἀγωνίζου ὡς καλὸς στρατιώτης"</note> page 111.

22. *Exhortation XII.* Brother, if having renounced the vain life... <note>original Greek: "ἀδελφέ, ἐὰν ἀποταξάμενος τῷ ματαίῳ βίῳ"</note> page 112.

23. *Exhortation XIII.* It is necessary also for those from a wretched and miserable life... <note>original Greek: "δεῖ καὶ τοὺς ἐξ οἰκτροῦ καὶ μοχθηροῦ βίου"</note> page 113.

24. *Exhortation XIV.* Brother, will you set your soul...? <note>original Greek: "ἀδελφέ, ἐπιστήσῃς ψυχήν;"</note> page 113.

25. *Exhortation XV.* A city is built part by part. <note>original Greek: "καταμέρος οἰκοδομεῖται πόλις"</note> page 115.

26. *Exhortation XVI.* Brother, you have been deemed worthy of the holy garment. <note>original Greek: "ἀδελφέ, ἠξιώθης τοῦ ἁγίου ἐνδύματος"</note> page 117.

27. *Exhortation XVII.* Brother, why do you wander about being acted upon...? <note>original Greek: "ἀδελφέ, τί πλανᾶσαι ἐνεργούμενος"</note> page 119.

28. *Exhortation XVIII.* Brother, when a brother calls out by night. <note>original Greek: "ἀδελφέ, ὅταν κράξῃ ἀδελφὸς νυκτός"</note> page 120.

29. *Exhortation XIX.* Let us consider, beloved, the last things. <note>original Greek: "ἐννοηθῶμεν ἀγαπητοὶ τοὺς ἐσχάτους"</note> page 121.

30. *Exhortation XX.* As I reckon, brothers, that there are three... <note>original Greek: "ὡς λογίζομαι, ἀδελφοί, ὅτι τρεῖς εἰσιν"</note> page 122.

31. *Exhortation XXI.* If a brother comes. <note>original Greek: "ἐὰν ἔλθῃ ἀδελφός"</note> page 124.

32. *Exhortation XXII. To the younger ones in the <term>cenobiums</term>.* <note>A cenobium is a community of monks living together, as opposed to hermits.</note> <note>original Greek: "τοῖς ἐν κοινοβίοις νεωτέροις"</note> page 126.

33. *Exhortation XXIII.* They are foolishly attacked. <note>original Greek: "ἀφρόνως πολεμοῦνται"</note> page 128.

34. *Exhortation XXIV.* The thought casts off. <note>original Greek: "ἀποβάλλει ὁ λογισμός"</note> page 133.

35. *Exhortation XXV.* Faith is the mother of every deed. <note>original Greek: "ἡ πίστις μήτηρ ἐστὶ παντὸς ἔργου"</note> page 134.

36. *Exhortation XXVI.* If it happens that a brother of the monastery... <note>original Greek: "ἐὰν συμβῇ ἀδελφὸν τοῦ κοινοβίου"</note> page 138.

37. *Exhortation XXVII.* A certain brother recounted to me. <note>original Greek: "ἀδελφός τις διηγήσατό μοι"</note> page 140.

38. *Exhortation XXVIII.* It is written: "To him who has, it shall be given." <note>original Greek: "γέγραπται, τῷ ἔχοντι δοθήσεται"</note> page 142.

39. *Exhortation XXIX.* Brothers, if one of the more devout... <note>original Greek: "ἀδελφοί, ἐάν τις τῶν εὐλαβεστέρων"</note> page 142.

40. *Exhortation XXX.* Beloved brother, if you happen to make progress. <note>original Greek: "ἀγαπητὲ ἀδελφέ, ἐὰν τύχῃς προκοπῆς"</note> page 145.

41. *Exhortation XXXI.* If bitterness occurs. <note>original Greek: "ἐὰν συμβῇ πικρία"</note> page 146.

42. *Exhortation XXXII.* A man living in negligence. <note>original Greek: "ἀνὴρ ἐν ἀμελείᾳ διάγων"</note> page 148.

<page-num>VII</page-num>

### ON THE WORKS OF SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN.
<note>Saint Ephrem (c. 306–373 AD) was a prolific Syriac hymnographer and theologian. These entries describe a Greek translation of his works.</note>

<margin>A</margin> 43. *Exhortation* 33. **A brother was seeking for himself a brother.** <note>original Greek: ἀδελφὸς ἐζήτει τ’ ἑαυτῷ ἀδελφόν</note> page 149.

44. *Exhortation* 34. **Beloved, if you should be tempted.** <note>original Greek: ἀγαπητέ, ἐὰν πειραχθῇ σοι</note> page 153.

45. *Exhortation* 35. **To the brother or to the sister the evil one suggests.** <note>original Greek: τῷ ἀδελφῷ, εἴτε τῇ ἀδελφῇ ὑποβάλλει ὁ πονηρός</note> page 154.

46. *Exhortation* 36. **Exhort toward purity.** <note>original Greek: παρακάλεζε τ’ ἀγνείαν</note> page 157.

<margin>B</margin> 47. *Exhortation* 37. **Not only through the abstaining from foods.** <note>original Greek: οὐ μόνον δι’ ἀποχῆς βρωμάτων</note> page 158.

48. *Exhortation* 38. **Monk, let it be for Christ.** <note>original Greek: μοναχέ, ἔτω τῷ Χριστῷ</note> page 160.

49. *Exhortation* 39. **Beloved, if a brother should come and entreat you, saying.** <note>original Greek: ἀγαπητέ, ἐὰν ἐλθὼν ἀδελφός παρακαλέσῃ σε λέγων</note> page 163.

50. *Exhortation* 40. **Beloved, pay very close attention.** <note>original Greek: ἀγαπητέ, πρόσεχε σφοδρῶς</note> page 170.

<margin>C</margin> 51. *Exhortation* 41. **Brother, have you desired the solitary life?** <note>original Greek: ἀδελφέ, τὸν μονήρη βίον ἐπεθύμησας;</note> page 173.

52. *Exhortation* 42. **To those who sit in subjection.** <note>original Greek: τοῖς ἐν ὑποταγῇ καθεζομένοις</note> page 179.

53. *Exhortation* 43. **As our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ said.** <note>original Greek: τοῦ κυρίου καὶ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ εἰπόντος</note> page 186.

54. *Exhortation* 44. **Hear the Apostle saying.** <note>original Greek: ἄκυε τ’ ἀποστόλου λέγοντος</note> page 188.

<margin>D</margin> 55. *Exhortation* 45. **Where the Scripture speaks concerning Eli the priest.** <note>original Greek: τῆς γραφῆς λεγόσης περὶ ἠλὶ ἱερέως</note> page 190.

56. *Exhortation* 46. **Pay attention to yourself, beloved.** <note>original Greek: πρόσεχε σεαυτῷ ἀγαπητέ</note> page 195.

57. *Exhortation* 47. **Beloved, if you are deemed worthy of knowledge.** <note>original Greek: ἀγαπητέ, ἐὰν ἀξιωθῇς γνώσεως</note> page 198.

58. *Exhortation* 48. **Looking toward the fruit of obedience, brother.** <note>original Greek: εἰς τὸν καρπὸν τῆς ὑπακοῆς ἀφορῶν ἀδελφέ</note> page 199.

<margin>E</margin> 59. *Exhortation* 49. **The Lord of Glory said.** <note>original Greek: ἔφη ὁ κύριος τῆς δόξης</note> page 204.

60. *Exhortation* 50. **In the same way a sword hamstrings a horse.** <note>original Greek: ὃν τρόπον ξίφος νευροκοπεῖ ἵππον</note> page 212.

61. *Exhortation* 51. **Beloved brother, if after renouncing the world you become a monk.** <note>original Greek: ἀγαπητέ ἀδελφέ, ἐὰν ἀποταξάμενος γένῃ μοναχός</note> page 213.

62. **How one might possess humility, One Hundred Chapters.** Chapter 1 begins: **The beginning of fruit-bearing is the blossom.** <note>original Greek: ἀρχὴ καρποφορίας ἄνθος</note> page 215. <margin>F</margin>

63. **On the perfection with which a monk ought to be endowed.** **Last year some came to me asking.** <note>original Greek: πέρυσί μοι προσῆλθον τινὲς αἰτῶντες</note> page 241.

64. **On divine grace.** **Hasten to possess divine grace.** <note>original Greek: σπεῦδε ἔχειν τὴν θείαν χάριν</note> page 254.

65. **On the disease of the tongue and the passions of the soul.** **All kinds of rest have been sown.** <note>original Greek: ἐσπαρμέναι εἰσὶν αἱ ἀναπαύσεις πᾶσαι</note> page 259.

66. **In imitation of the Proverbs.** The Prologue begins: **He who believes in the Son of God has eternal life.** <note>original Greek: ὁ πιστεύων εἰς τὸν υἱὸν τοῦ θεοῦ, ἔχει ζωὴν αἰώνιον</note> page 268. **Here ends the first part of this most excellent Manuscript. The second part follows** on page 269: **The beginning of the second book.**

67. **On repentance.** **Repentance is a multi-fruiting fruit, beloved.** <note>original Greek: καρπὸς πολύφορός ἐστιν ἡ μετάνοια, ἀγαπητοί</note> page 269. This Sermon is absent from the Manuscript of Saint Mark. <note>original Latin: Codice S. Marci</note> All the rest are written in the same order in both.

68. **On virtues and vices, 22 Chapters.** Prologue: **I call you blessed in your life, O lovers of Christ.** <note>original Greek: μακαρίζω τ’ ὑμᾶς ζωὴν ὦ φιλόχριστοι</note> page 313.

69. **On the fear of God, Chapter 1.** **Blessed is that man.** <note>original Greek: μακάριος ὁ ἄνθρωπος ἐκεῖνος</note> page 314.

70. **On him who does not have the fear of God, Chapter 2.** **He who does not have the fear of God.** <note>original Greek: ὁ μὴ ἔχων τὸν φόβον τοῦ θεοῦ</note> page 314.

71. **On charity, Chapter 3.** **Blessed is the man.** <note>original Greek: μακάριος ὁ ἄνθρωπος</note> page 314.

72. **On those who do not have charity, Chapter 4.** **Wretched and miserable.** <note>original Greek: ἄθλιος καὶ ταλαίπωρος</note> page 316.

73. **On long-suffering, Chapter 5.** **Truly blessed.** <note>original Greek: μακάριος ἀληθῶς</note> page 316.

74. **On him who lacks long-suffering, Chapter 6.** **He who does not have.** <note>original Greek: ὁ μὴ ἔχων</note> page 316.

75. **On patience, Chapter 7.** **Blessed, brothers.** <note>original Greek: μακάριος ἀδελφοί</note> page 316.

76. **On him who lacks patience, Chapter 8.** **Wretched ones.** <note>original Greek: ἄθλιοι</note> page 316.

77. **On kindness, Chapter 9.** **Blessed.** <note>original Greek: μακάριος</note> page 317.

78. **On anger, Chapter 10.** **He who by quick-temperedness.** <note>original Greek: ὁ τῇ ὀξυχολίᾳ</note> page 318.

79. **On gentleness, Chapter 11.** **Blessed.** <note>original Greek: μακάριος</note> page 318.

80. **On wickedness, Chapter 12.** **To mourn.** <note>original Greek: θρηνεῖν</note> page 319.

81. **On truth, Chapter 13.** **Blessed.** <note>original Greek: μακάριος</note> page 320.

82. **On lying, Chapter 14.** **Wretched.** <note>original Greek: ἄθλιος</note> page 320.

83. **On obedience, Chapter 16.** <note>Note: The numbering jumps from 14 to 16 in the original source.</note> **Blessed.** <note>original Greek: μακάριος</note> page 321.

84. **On disobedience and murmuring, Chapter 16.** **Cursed.** <note>original Greek: ἐπικατάρατος</note> page 321.

85. **On him who is a stranger to envy and jealousy, Chapter 17.** **Blessed.** <note>original Greek: μακάριος</note> page 322.

86. **On envy and jealousy, Chapter 18.** **But he who is in these things.** <note>original Greek: ὁ δὲ ἐν τούτοις</note> page 323.

87. **On him who [is free] from curses and insults** <note>The text cuts off here. The catchword indicates the continuation is "and insults" or "reproaches".</note>

## PREFACE

<margin>A</margin> 87. One who is a stranger to better things, Chapter XIX. <note>original: "μακάριος" — Blessed.</note> page 324.

88. On slander and the slanderous, Chapter XX. <note>original: "ὁ ἐπὶ λοιδορίᾳ" — The one prone to insult.</note> page 324.

89. On temperance and self-control, Chapter XXI. <note>original: "μακάριος" — Blessed.</note> page 324.

90. On intemperance and lack of self-control, Chapter XXII. <note>original: "ὁ δὲ ἀκρατής" — But the one who lacks self-control.</note> page 325.

<margin>B</margin> 91. Self-reproach and confession. <note>original: "ἀδελφοί, συμπαθήσατέ μοι" — Brothers, have compassion on me.</note> page 327.

92. On the overthrow of pride. <note>original: "ματαία πᾶσα ἄσκησις" — All ascetic practice is vain [without humility].</note> page 332.

93. A sermon of <term>compunction</term> <note>A feeling of deep regret or "piercing" of the heart by remorse for sins.</note>. <note>original: "δεῦτε ἀγαπητοί, δεῦτε πατέρες κὶ ἀδελφοί μϗ" — Come, beloved; come, fathers and my brothers.</note> page 337.

94. An <term>ascetic</term> <note>Relating to the practice of severe self-discipline and abstention from indulgence for religious reasons.</note> discourse. <note>original: "ὁ πόνος τϗ λέγειν ἀναγκάζει με" — The pain of speaking forces me.</note> page 349.

<margin>C</margin> 95. An ascetic discourse, in imitation of the Proverbs. The Prologue begins. <note>original: "ὁ πιστεύων εἰς τὸν υἱὸν τϗ θεϗ, ἔχει ζωὴν αἰώνιον" — The one who believes in the Son of God has eternal life.</note> page 374. The same sermon is found above in this Manuscript on page 268.

96. On the fear of God. <note>original: "φοβϗ τὸν κύριον" — Fear the Lord.</note> page 375.

97. For the correction of those who live wickedly and seek honors. <note>original: "ἀγωνιῶ, ἀδελφοί μϗ, καὶ τρέμω" — I struggle, my brothers, and I tremble.</note> page 417.

<margin>D</margin> 98. Self-reproach and confession. <note>original: "εν πολλοῖς ὑμῖν, ἀδελφοί, δοκῶν χρησιμεύειν" — Appearing to be useful to many of you, brothers.</note> page 425.

99. On the passions of the soul. <note>original: "θέλω εἰπεῖν ἐνώπιον τῆς δόξης σϗ" — I wish to speak before your glory.</note> page 450.

100. On repentance. <note>original: "ὁ κατελθὼν ἐκ τῶν κόλπων τϗ πατρός" — He who descended from the bosom of the Father.</note> page 454. This Sermon is missing from the St. Mark’s Manuscript.

101. On compunction. <note>original: "δοτὴρ πάντων ἀγαθῶν" — Giver of all good things.</note> page 460.

102. A sermon of compunction. <note>original: "ἐν μιᾳ τῶν ἡμερῶν ὄρθρϗ λίαν ἀναστάς" — On one of the days, having risen very early at dawn.</note> page 465.

103. Another sermon. <note>original: "κατανύγηθι ψυχή μϗ" — Be pricked in your heart, my soul.</note> page 467.

104. A <term>parænetic</term> <note>Exhortatory or advising; a sermon meant to encourage moral improvement.</note> sermon. <note>original: "πρόσεχε σεαυτῷ ὦ νεότης" — Pay attention to yourself, O youth.</note> page 472.

105. On the second coming of our Lord Jesus Christ. <note>original: "προσέλθετε, καὶ δεῦτε υἱοὶ φωτός" — Approach and come, sons of light.</note> page 473. This sermon is found in the St. Mark’s Manuscript below at number 118.

106. On the departed Fathers. <note>original: "τὴν καρδίαν μϗ ἀλγῶ" — My heart is in pain.</note> page 477.

107. Another sermon on the departed Fathers. <note>original: "ἐν ταύτῃ ἡμέρᾳ τῇ πρώτῃ" — On this first day.</note> page 481.

108. A four-syllable sermon. <note>likely referring to the rhythmic meter of the Greek text.</note> <note>original: "νικηθείς ὁ κρᾶτος" — The power having been conquered.</note> page 486.

109. A seven-syllable sermon. <note>original: "εἰδότες οὖν ἀδελφοί" — Therefore, knowing, brothers.</note> page 487.

110. On the fear of the soul. <note>original: "ἐγὼ Ἐφραὶμ ἁμαρτωλός" — I, Ephrem, a sinner.</note> page 488.

111. A prayer. <note>original: "ἥμαρτον εἰς τὸν οὐρανόν" — I have sinned against heaven.</note> page 492.

112. A memorandum. <note>original: "πᾶσαν ὥραν ἐν νῷ ἔχω" — I have in mind every hour.</note> page 493.

113. How the soul ought to pray to God with tears when it is tempted by the enemy. <note>original: "ψυχή τεθλιμμένη" — A distressed soul.</note> page 499.

114. On judgment and compunction. <note>original: "δεῦτε πάντες ἀδελφοί, ἀκούσατε συμβουλῆς ἐμϗ ἁμαρτωλϗ καὶ ἀπαιδεύτϗ Ἐφραίμ" — Come all, brothers, hear the counsel of me, a sinner and unlearned Ephrem.</note> page 504.

115. On patience. <note>original: "ὁ βϗλόμενος εὐαρεστῆσαι" — The one who wishes to be well-pleasing.</note> page 512.

116. On the beatitudes and miseries. <note>original: "μακάριοι οἱ τὸν θεὸν ἀγαπήσαντες" — Blessed are those who have loved God.</note> page 519.

117. A confession, or a prayer to God. <note>original: "ἴασαί με κύριε" — Heal me, Lord.</note> page 521.

118. On the second coming of our Lord Jesus Christ. <note>original: "προσέλθετε, καὶ δεῦτε υἱοὶ φωτός" — Approach and come, sons of light.</note> page 523. The same sermon is found above on page 473.

119. On faith. <note>original: "ὁ Κύριος ἐν τῷ εὐαγγελίῳ" — The Lord in the Gospel.</note> page 528. Here ends the St. Mark’s Manuscript.

120. The Life of Saint Ephrem the Syrian. <note>original: "οὗτος ὁ ὅσιος πατὴρ ἡμῶν Ἐφραὶμ ἦν ἐξ ἀνατολῶν" — This holy father of ours, Ephrem, was from the East.</note> page 533.

<margin>E</margin> 121. The Testament of our holy father Ephrem. <note>original: "ἐγὼ Ἐφραὶμ ἀποθνήσκω" — I, Ephrem, am dying.</note> page 536. Thus far the Sforza Manuscript I. The sermons which follow exist in other manuscripts of the same library.

122. A sermon of our holy father Ephrem on the harlot who anointed the Lord with ointment, on the Great Wednesday of Holy Week. <note>original: "τοῦ ὁσίου πατρὸς ἡμῶν Ἐφραὶμ λόγος εἰς τὴν πόρνην, τὴν ἀλείψασαν τὸν κύριον μύρῳ, τῇ μεγάλῃ τετράδι" — Discourse of our holy father Ephrem regarding the harlot who anointed the Lord with myrrh, on the Great Wednesday.</note>

<margin>F</margin> Beginning: <note>original: "πολλῶν ὄντων ὁσίων" — While there are many holy things.</note> page 213. Found in a very ancient parchment manuscript of 287 leaves, the beginning of which is: *The Cross that appeared in the sky to the Emperor Constantine, by Saint Epiphanius of Justiniana, Bishop in Cyprus.*

123. A sermon of the blessed Ephrem on the most beautiful Joseph. <note>original: "τοῦ μακαρίου Ἐφραὶμ λόγος εἰς τὸν πάγκαλον Ἰωσήφ" — Discourse of the blessed Ephrem regarding the all-beautiful Joseph.</note> Beginning: <note>original: "ὁ θεὸς τοῦ Ἀβραάμ" — The God of Abraham.</note> In the same manuscript, page 197.

IX
ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHREM THE SYRIAN.

<margin>124</margin> Sermon of the Blessed Ephrem on Saint Elijah the Prophet. <note>original: "τοῦ μακαρ. Ἐφραῒμ λόγος εἰς τὸν ἅγιον Ἠλίαν τὸν προφήτην"</note> Beginning: "Give me silence." In the same manuscript, page 247.

## SERMONS
### OF SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN,
->*Which are found in the Vallicellana Library in an ancient*<-
->*parchment manuscript, which once belonged to*<-
->*Achilles Statius of Portugal, a most illustrious man;*<-
->*marked with the letter C, number 34.*<-

<margin>A</margin>
1. **S**ermon on the spiritual struggle <term>luctamine: from the Latin 'luctamen,' referring to the spiritual wrestling or intense effort required in the monastic life</term>. It lacks the beginning: it starts, however, with these words: "For the monk is like a sown field." <note>original Greek: "ἔοικεν γὰρ ὁ μοναχὸς χώρᾳ τῇ ἐσπαρμένῃ"</note> It ends: <margin>10 :</margin> "unto all ages of ages, amen."

2. Ascetic Sermon of our Holy and Blessed Father Ephrem, Admonition to the Brothers. <note>original Greek: "τοῦ ἁγίȣ καὶ ὁσίȣ πατρὸς ἡμῶν Ἐφραῒμ λόγος ἀσκητικός, νουθεσία τοῖς ἀδελφοῖς"</note> Beginning: "Brothers, let us desire blessings."

3. By the same author, the second Sermon on the second coming of the Lord and on the Judgment. <note>original Greek: "τοῦ αὐτοῦ περὶ τῆς δευτέρας παρουσίας τοῦ Κυρίου καὶ περὶ κρίσεως, λόγος β'"</note> Beginning: "Come all brothers, hear the counsel of me, a sinner." Thus, the first Sermon has been lost from this manuscript.

4. By the same author, on virginity. <note>original Greek: "τοῦ αὐτοῦ περὶ παρθενίας"</note> Beginning: "Paul the Apostle, as a most excellent counselor, teaches us all."

<margin>B</margin>
5. By the same author, on repentance and patience. <note>original Greek: "τοῦ αὐτοῦ περὶ μετανοίας καὶ ὑπομονῆς"</note> It lacks the beginning: but, as the Most Eminent Baronius <note>Cesare Baronius (1538–1607) was a prominent Italian Cardinal and ecclesiastical historian</note> rightly notes, and as is gathered from the fragments, this Sermon is about the distress by which the soul is pressed when it is tempted by the enemy. Beginning: "An afflicted soul." <note>original Greek: "ψυχὴ τεθλιμμένη"</note> See Volume I of the Greek-Latin edition, page 193.

<meta>Volume II.</meta>
b

Page XI
PREFACE

# IV.
## SERMONS
### OF SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN,
->Which are found in five Greek Manuscript Codices of the Moscow Imperial Library, edited by Athanasius Schiade, Professor of the Greek Language in the Monastery of Nefki.<-

---

<margin>A</margin>
**1.** **T**he confession of Saint Ephrem, or rather, prayers to God. <note>original: "Sancti Ephraimi confessio, nimirum preces ad Deum"</note> Opening: *Heal me Lord, and I shall be healed* : *Sana me Domine, & sanabor.* Page 2.

**2.** The life of the same author. <note>original: "Ejusdem vita"</note> *There was born in the East a certain man by the name of Ephrem the Syrian.* p. 3.

<margin>B</margin>
**3.** An exhortatory sermon of the same author. *Glory to you O God, glory to you.* p. 4.

**4.** The second admonition of the same author. *Beloved, are you a Christian through grace?* p. 5.

<margin>C</margin>
**5.** The third admonition of the same author. *If, having renounced this life, you have entered in.* p. 7.

60. Question: What is it that the Apostle says, that through the transgression of the Law one dishonors God, and how is God dishonored? Answer: Just as one who [disregards] the King's letters. <note>original: "ἐρώτησις. τί ἐστιν ὃ λέγει Ἀπόστολος... ἀπόκρισις. ὥσπερ ὁ τὰ γράμματα τοῦ βασιλέως."</note> p. 139.

<margin>F</margin>
61. How it is written concerning heretics, "I have hated those who hate You, O Lord," and elsewhere it is said, "that you have hated them as my enemies," and elsewhere, "that you shall not hate any man." Answer: Heretics [should be hated] as blasphemers. <note>original: "πῶς γέγραπται περὶ τῶν αἱρετικῶν... ἀπόκρισις. τοὺς αἱρήσεώτας ὡς βλασφήμους."</note> 145.

62. Which sin is unforgivable? 60. Answer: That against the Holy Spirit, and that of all heretics. <note>original: "ποία ἁμαρτεία ἐστὶν ἀσυγχώρητος; ξ'. ἀπόκρισις. ἡ εἰς τὸ ἅγιον πνεῦμα, καὶ ἡ πάντων τῶν αἱρετικῶν."</note> p. 145.

63. Question: Which sin is heavier, apart from heresy? Answer: Just as of all the virtues. <note>original: "ἐρώτησις, ποία ἁμαρτεία βαρυτέρα, χωρὶς τῆς αἱρέσεως; ἀπόκρισις. ὥσπερ πασῶν τῶν ἀρετῶν."</note> p. 147.

XIV
PREFACE
<meta>catchword: through</meta>

This is the first chapter of the Discourse entitled, <margin>A</margin> *On Virtues and Vices* <note>original Greek: περὶ ἀρετῶν καὶ κακιῶν; Latin: De virtutibus & vitiis</note>. The Prologue is missing, which begins, *I pronounce your life blessed* <note>original Greek: μακαρίζω τὴν ὑμῶν ζωήν; Latin: Beatam praedico vitam vestram</note>. There follow twenty-one other chapters, as found in the Vatican Manuscripts 439, 440, 729, 1815; the Alexandrian Manuscripts 8 and 16; and the Clementine Manuscript 62. However, in the catalog of the Moscow manuscripts, only the first seven chapters are reviewed, namely: I. *On the fear of the Lord, as above*. <margin>B</margin> II. *On the soul lacking fear*, *he who does not have fear* <note>original Greek: ὁ δ' μὴ ἔχων φόβον; Latin: non habens timorem</note> page 173. III. *On Charity*, *blessed is he who has the love of God* <note>original Greek: μακάριος ὁ ἔχων ἀγάπην τοῦ Θεοῦ; Latin: Beatus habens caritatem Dei</note>. IV. *On those not having charity*, *wretched and miserable* <note>original Greek: ἄθλιος καὶ ταλαίπωρος; Latin: miser & infelix</note>. V. *On Long-suffering*, *truly blessed is that man* <note>original Greek: μακάριος ἀληθῶς ὁ ἄνθρωπος ἐκεῖνος; Latin: Beatus vere homo ille</note>. VI. *On those not having long-suffering*, *he who does not have it* <note>original Greek: ὁ μὴ ἔχων; Latin: non habens</note>. VII. *On patience* page 176. Discourse LXXV. *Blessed, brothers, is he who has attained patience* <note>original Greek: μακάριος, ἀδελφοὶ, ὅστις ὑπομονὴν ἐκτήσατο; Latin: Beatus, fratres, qui patientiam consequutus est</note>. See Johann Peter Kohl in the small book titled *Introduction to the History and Literature of the Slavs*, Altona, 1729. Concerning the Slavonic Codex of Ephrem, chapter I, from page 245 to page 265.

---

# V.
# S E R M O N S
# OF SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN,
## Which exist in Slavonic <note>The liturgical language of the Slavic Orthodox Church</note> in the book printed at Moscow in the year of Christ 1701.
### From the revision of Johann Peter Kohl, Professor of Ecclesiastical History and Humanities at the Saint Petersburg Academy of Sciences.

**S**ermon I. Instruction on piety and the salvation of the soul, and on the renunciation of life. Beginning: *I, Ephrem, a sinner and a fool, always weak and negligent*. Page 1.

Sermon II. Giving of thanks to God the Savior. *Glory to you, O God, glory to you*. p. 5.

Sermon III. Instruction on religion, and for those wishing to enter the monastic life. *Dearest, are you a Christian by divine grace?* p. 6.

Sermon IV. Instruction for newly-made monks. *Beloved, if you have renounced this life*. p. 8.

Sermon V. Instruction for monks, that they may bravely endure temptation. *Beloved, behold I set before you another deposit from the Lord*. p. 9.

<margin>C</margin> Sermon VI. Instruction that the monk should be gentle. *Beloved, if you have made a good beginning*. p. 10.

Sermon VII. Instruction that he should always be occupied in labors. *And concerning manual labor, brother, do not be negligent*. p. 11.

Sermon VIII. Instruction that he should not succumb to desires. *Regarding an existing scandal, we know the one saying...* p. 12.

<margin>D</margin> Sermon IX. Instruction that he should exercise daily patience. *And concerning time, do not be indifferent*. p. 13.

Sermon X. Instruction on the thoughts that happen upon us. *Beloved, do not turn your back on thoughts that come to meet you*. p. 14.

Sermon XI. Instruction that he should always be sober— <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page</note>

xv
->ON THE WORKS OF SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN.<-

<margin>A</margin>... Brother, fight like a good soldier.
page 15.

Sermo XII. Instruction that one should not have arrogance in their mind. <note>original: "Sermo XII. Instructio, ne habeat in mente arrogantiam."</note> *Brother, if you have left behind a vain life*. p. 16.

<margin>B</margin>Sermo XIII. Instruction on the status of superiors and on pastoral care. <note>original: "Sermo XIII. Instructio de statu præfectorum, & de pastorali cura."</note> *Brother, if you have surrendered your soul, gird your loins like a man*. page 17. <gloss>Read: "if a soul has been entrusted to you."</gloss>

Sermo XIV. A parabolic instruction for superiors and subjects. *A city is built part by part, and war is waged by command*. page 19.

Sermo XV. Instruction on the struggles of the monastic and hermit life. *Brother, if you have been deemed worthy of the holy monastic habit*. <note>The "habit" refers to the distinctive clothing of a monk, symbolizing their vow.</note> p. 20.

<margin>C</margin>Sermo XVI. Instruction against being desirous of empty glory. *Brother, why do you abandon your confidence, having been defeated by the devil*. p. 22.

Sermo XVII. Instruction on the lifting up of the soul in prayers to God. *Brother, when a brother knocks at your door at night so that you might rise*. p. 23.

<margin>D</margin>Sermo XVIII. Instruction on the correction of spiritual desire. *Beloved, we know those standing before worldly Kings*. p. 24.

Sermo XIX. On the vigil and sober prayers. *Brother, I think there are three types of dreams*. p. 25.

Sermo XX. Instruction for those tempted by their own thoughts. *If it should happen that a certain brother enters the monastic life*. p. 26.

<margin>E</margin>Sermo XXI. Instruction on the enemy's battle against monks who have recently entered the monastic life, and on manual labor. *The devil attacks younger monks living in community in various ways*. p. 28.

<margin>F</margin>Sermo XXII. Instruction on the entrance by which the devil overcomes monks, and on moving from one place to another. *The devil fights in different ways against those who live under a spiritual father*. p. 30.

Sermo XXIII. That a monk ought to remain in one place until death. <note>This refers to "stability of place," a common monastic vow.</note> *Thought imposes itself upon the monk*. p. 34.

Sermo XXVI. That a monk ought not to laugh or act like a buffoon, but weep continuously. *The beginning of a dissolute monastic life is laughter*. p. 35.

---

Sermo XXV. Instruction for junior monks on obedience and gentleness. *Guard yourself from your youth, lest through laziness and laughter you lose your time*. p. 39. <gloss>Read: "O youth."</gloss>

Sermo XXVI. On patience. *Beloved, we shall be like great-hearted soldiers*. p. 42.

Sermo XXVII. That a monk ought not to move from one place to another. *Faith is the mother of every good work*. p. 43.

Sermo XXVIII. On love and mercy toward a penitent. *If it should happen that a brother departs from the monastic community*. p. 45.

Sermo XXIX. A narrative about Palladius the monk, who lived without the fear of God in disobedience. *A certain brother told me*. page 48.

Sermo XXX. That to him who has, more will be given. *It is written: to him who has, it is given*. p. 49.

Sermo XXXI. Instruction that one who falls into temptation should not be judged, but prayed for. *Brothers, if any of the religious brothers standing in the assembly*. p. 49.

Sermo XXXII. On mild wisdom. *Most beloved, if you obtain the prerogative to stand*. p. 52.

Sermo XXXIII. That a Christian ought to exercise long-suffering and gentleness. *If anger should come upon you unexpectedly, do not [give way] to anger*. p. 52.

Sermo XXXIV. On desires. *A man spending his days in laziness*. p. 54.

Sermo XXXV. How a monk ought to comfort the suffering and the weak. *A brother seeking his brother*. p. 55.

Sermo XXXVI. How a reader ought to read diligently and attentively before the brothers. *Brother, if you are commanded to read in the ears [of the assembly]*. p. 59.

Sermo XXXVII. On celibacy and <term>temperance</term> <note>Self-control, especially regarding food and physical desires.</note>. *The wicked one suggests a thought to a brother or sister*. p. 59.

Sermo XXXVIII. On chastity. *Most beloved brother, compare chastity to a palm tree*. page 72.

Sermo XXXIX. That the eyes should not be cast about here and there, but cast down to the ground. *Question: how is the battle settled*. page 63.

The following Slavonic <term>distichs</term> <note>Couplets or two-line verses.</note>, composed by the translators, place a crown <note>metaphorically: a finishing touch or conclusion.</note> upon the whole work; they were translated into Latin by Kohl <note>Johann Peter Kohl (1698–1778), a scholar who studied Slavonic manuscripts.</note> on page 337 as follows:

> To God, in a three-substantial unity of all things,
> We sing great praise with a joyful heart,
> Having been deemed worthy, in these books of divine wisdom
> Of the spiritual man Ephrem, full of the wisdom of his sermons,
> To see a happy beginning and a holy end.
> And with a unanimous will to complete the translation
> Of the Greek sermons, newly translated
> For the eyes of the faithful and the benefit of those under our care.
> However many of you there are, living in either a sacred or secular state, read these things, we pray,
> And grant forgiveness to those who have worked on these:
> And with your wisdom, kindly correct
> If anything in the translation is less than accurate.

<page-num>XXX</page-num>
->PREFACE<-

## CHAPTER II.
## CONCERNING THE LATIN WORKS
# OF SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN
### *With the interpretations of Ambrose the Camaldolese, Pietro Francesco Zini, and Gerard Vossius.*

---

**A**lthough in the Preface to the first volume of the Greek Works of Saint Ephrem the Syrian—specifically in the second chapter concerning ancient Latin versions and those following, in which I treated the editions of Ambrose, Zini, and Vossius—it seems to me to be sufficiently and abundantly clear from the very sequence of facts how excellently those translators bestowed their labor and zeal in performing their duty; nevertheless, I judge it would not be out of place to recount and refute individually here those things which certain modern writers <note>neoterici: refers here to recent scholars of the 17th or 18th century</note> have mistakenly left in their writings concerning them.

<margin>I. It is shown against Oudin that other Latin versions existed before the Ambrosian version.</margin>
And first indeed, Oudin <note>Casimir Oudin (1638–1717), a former monk and bibliographer of ecclesiastical writers</note> extols the Ambrosian version more than is just, when he makes it the very first of all Latin versions:
> "The first," (he says in *On Ecclesiastical Writers,* 
> *Vol. 1. page 502.*) "who rendered into Latin 
> some small works of St. Ephrem the Syrian 
> which had already been translated into Greek 
> long ago, was a man of great name, Ambrose, 
> Abbot of the Camaldolese and Superior 
> General of the whole Order." 

But from the Catalog which I prefixed to the first volume on page 81 and following, it is clearly evident that far more Latin sermons of Saint Ephrem exist than those which Ambrose translated; these were converted from the Greek language long ago by an anonymous translator. I noted in Chapter 2 of the Preface, page 12, that some of these were mentioned by learned men older than Ambrose, and are preserved not only in handwritten manuscripts in libraries, but have even appeared in public light through printed editions.

In a very ancient Vatican Manuscript Code, marked number 6443 (which had belonged to Cardinal Pierre of Rouen <note>original: "Petri Rothomagensis," referring to Pierre de la Forêt or a similar figure associated with the Rouen see</note>, as is noted on folio 1), eleven sermons of Saint <margin>A</margin> Ephrem are contained in eight books. ### ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHREM THE SYRIAN.

"Chosen of the Savior: come, let us trade, while the market is celebrated." <note>original: "Salvatoris electus : venite negotiemur, quoadusque nundina celebratur." This is a metaphor for performing good works while the "market" of life is still open.</note> page 284. The instruction of St. Ephrem to the monks ends here. There follows in the same manuscript another *Sermon on Penance*, preceded by a prologue from the scribe, which is taken from the book of St. Jerome regarding ecclesiastical writers. <note>referring to Jerome's *De Viris Illustribus* or "On Illustrious Men."</note> Beginning on page 285: *Ephrem, Deacon of the Church of Edessa*. <note>Edessa was a major center of Syriac Christianity, now modern-day Urfa, Turkey.</note> *Here begins the book of St. Ephrem the Deacon on penance.*

> "We see many established in the Church who do not hold the doctrine of ecclesiastical piety and faith, although they attribute too much to themselves regarding the merits of justice. The other previous ones exist in both manuscript and printed volumes, provided in different translations by Ambrosius or Vossius."

<margin>II. It is demonstrated against Kohlius that Ambrosius was the Abbot General of the Camaldolese Order.</margin>

Kohlius <note>Johann Peter Kohl (1698–1778), a scholar of Oriental and Slavonic studies.</note> refutes Oudinus <note>Casimir Oudin (1638–1717), a French bibliographer and former monk.</note> because he calls Ambrosius "Abbot and Prefect of the entire <term>Camaldolese Order</term> <note>A reformed branch of the Benedictine monks founded by St. Romuald.</note>": he thinks this error arose because Oudinus attributed the book of the Abbot of Cluny—which is sometimes attached to the versions by Ambrosius—to Ambrosius himself. Here are his words from *Book 2 of the history of the versions of Ephrem the Syrian and especially of the Slavonic version, Altona 1729.*

> "page 268. He calls the same man 'Abbot and Prefect of the whole Order'; I do not know on what foundation, since he himself calls himself a 'Camaldolese monk,' and is given this title by all authors who mention him. Thus, Oudinus undoubtedly committed an error here, perhaps arising from the fact that he believed the book of the 'Venerable,' as they call him, Abbot of Cluny—which is sometimes found annexed to the translations of Ambrosius—belonged to Ambrosius himself."

Indeed, we do not need that small glory of the "Prefecture" to adorn a man most famous in both deeds and writings with praise; but, with all due respect to Kohlius, he should not have so freely criticized Oudinus without having read all the authors who mention Ambrosius. Kohlius wrote this work in Hamburg on April 13, 1729, as he testifies in his Preface to the Reader. However, the twenty books of the *Letters of Ambrosius the Camaldolese*, formerly collected by Mabillon, were finally published in Paris in the year of Christ 1724 through the study and labor of Edmund Martene and Ursinus Durand, <note>Two famous Benedictine scholars of the Congregation of St. Maur, known for their massive historical collections.</note> in which Ambrosius himself narrates, not without tears, the leadership of the entire Camaldolese Order entrusted to him (*Book 6, letters 40, 41; Book 10, letters 5, 11, 40, 41; Book 18, letter 14; and Book 20, letter 2*). This took place in the year of Christ 1431. The praised Martene and Durand, in the Preface to the third volume of the *Collection of Ancient Writers and historical, dogmatic, and moral monuments*, at number 13, report it in these words:

> "And when he had devoted himself to these studies and labors for thirty years at most, and was in the meantime enjoying the sweetest company of all learned men, he was snatched unwillingly to the 'helm' of his Order in the year 1431. In the first letter of Book 11, given to his brother Hieronymus on the 17th of the Kalends of July <note>June 15th</note>, the same Ambrosius writes that he had intended for his blessing to be carried out on the octave of the Apostles."

Therefore, Kohlius could have learned not only from Ambrosius but also from other authors that he was not a simple monk, but the Abbot and Prefect of his entire Order. As for why he calls himself a "Camaldolese monk" and not "Abbot General" in his translation of Ephrem's Sermons, this happened because he had translated those Sermons into Latin several years before he was elected General Prefect of the whole Camaldolese Order, as the same learned men Martene and Durand observe in the place cited, number 10: for the name of "monk" which he had used for himself in the letter to Cosimo de' Medici <note>The famous Florentine statesman and patron of the arts.</note> was retained both in the hand-written copies and in the printed ones. Meanwhile, on the authority of Augustine, a monk of Florence and writer of his life, we gladly correct that error which...

### PROLEGOMENA
