...which we admit we accepted in Chapter 3 of these *Prolegomena*, having drawn it from Bellarmine, Labbe, and other more recent authors regarding the year of Ambrogio's death. In that section, we extended his life to the year of Christ 1490, even though it is established from the testimony of Augustine <note>Augustinus Florentinus, a biographer of Traversari</note> that he died in Florence on October 21st <note>original: "12. Kal. Novemb." — the 12th day before the Calends of November</note> in the year of Christ 1439. This is excellently confirmed by Martene and Durand in the previously cited passage, number 19, based on a funeral oration by his disciple Mauro, where he says that Pope Eugenius IV was among the first to grieve the loss of such a great man. From this, I believe, the opportunity for error arose: some have handed down the tradition that the first edition of Ambrogio's *Sermons* was prepared at Brescia in the year 1490. However, I refuted their opinion in Chapter 3 of the *Prolegomena* by citing older editions from the years 1475 and 1481.

<margin>IV. *Ambrogio was indeed skilled in the Greek language, but ignorant of Syriac.*</margin>
Furthermore, it is a fact known and explored by everyone that Ambrogio made such progress in Greek literature under his Byzantine master, Manuel Chrysoloras, that whether he was translating or explaining in Greek or Latin—especially at the Council of Florence—he was believed to be more Greek than Latin. But as for the claim that he also mastered the Syriac language, from which he allegedly translated the *Sermons* of Ephrem <note>Saint Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373), a prolific Syriac-language hymnwriter and theologian</note> into Latin, no one besides Kohl <note>Johann Peter Kohl (1698–1778), a scholar of Oriental languages</note> has entertained such a notion. Kohl raises a doubt on page 259: *whether those ascetic sermons translated by Ambrogio are more rightly said to be from the Syriac itself, or from a Greek translation* <note>original: "μεταφράσει" (metaphrasei)</note>. He then brings forward authors for both sides. For the Greek side, he says *Andreas Rivet* asserts it at the end of his *Sacred Criticism*, book 3, chapter 21, page 335, where he discusses the writings of Ephrem in these words: "Ambrogio, the Camaldolese monk, published many Sermons under the name of Ephrem, translated from Greek into Latin." For the Syriac side, however, Kohl says: "This seems to be able to be gathered from the life of this Ambrogio, composed by Augustinus Florentinus and inserted into his history of the Camaldolese order, where among other things these words are read: 'An excellent volume of the Blessed Ephrem, the Syrian Hermit, was brought to him from Syria, which had never until then appeared in Latin. Therefore, having rendered it into the Latin language, he offered it to Cosimo himself' <note>Cosimo de' Medici (1389–1464), Traversari's patron</note>." Finally, Kohl responds to the arguments of Tentzel, who had

---

**A** explained the passage cited from Augustine regarding the Greek codex brought from Syria in a convenient way. Kohl says on page 270: "Tentzel thinks he solves all this doubt in the cited passage, page 280, pronouncing thus: 'But beware of concluding from Augustine's words that Ambrogio translated from the native Syriac idiom of Ephrem. For it is most clearly gathered from the Preface itself that he translated it only from the Greek.'" **B** To this reasoning of Tentzel, Kohl replies as follows: "I do not know, however, if this doubt has been fully satisfied. Certainly, that argumentation on which Tentzel’s solution rests is not sufficient. Specifically, he appeals to those words of the Camaldolese monk in the Preface, where, using a mere allegory, he introduces **C** Ephrem as an old man setting out from Syria; when he perceives him to be Greek in conversation, he invites him into his cell and asks him to learn to speak Latin as well. By those words, *he noticed he was knowledgeable in the Greek tongue*, Tentzel holds that nothing else is indicated but a Greek version which he converted into Latin. In my judgment, however, **D** he did not look to that, wishing for nothing else here than to hint that the writings of Ephrem had already been translated at some time into the Greek language." But with Kohl’s kind permission, Ambrogio knew that Ephrem was a Syrian, not a Greek; and therefore, unless he had been referring to the Greek version of his sermons which he had undertaken to render into Latin, **E** he would have called him knowledgeable in the Syriac tongue, not the Greek. Furthermore, we read nowhere that the Syriac language was cultivated by anyone in Tuscany or any other part of Italy during those times; nor do we know of those Sermons which Ambrogio made Latin existing in Syriac in any library, whether **F** in Florence, Venice, or Rome. On the contrary, they exist in Greek everywhere, as is clear from the cited Catalogs. The first person who is recorded to have applied himself to Syriac letters was Ambrogio Theseus <note>Teseo Ambrogio degli Albonesi (1469–1540), an early Italian Orientalist</note>, a Regular Canon of the Order of St. Augustine, who had as his teachers Joseph the Archpriest, Elias the Deacon, and another Elias a Subdeacon, [envoys] of Peter, the Patriarch of the Maronites, to...

XXIII
ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHREM THE SYRIAN.

### ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHREM THE SYRIAN.

...sent legates to the Lateran Council <note>The Fifth Lateran Council (1512–1517) was an important assembly of the Catholic Church held in Rome.</note> under Pope Leo X, as Johann Albrecht Widmanstetter <note>A German diplomat and scholar who helped introduce Syriac studies to Europe.</note> affirms in his Preface to the new Syriac New Testament printed in Vienna in the year 1555.

<margin>V. Augustine’s oversights regarding Ambrose and his versions.</margin>
V. Meanwhile, regarding those points produced above by Kohl and Augustine, one might reasonably require greater diligence. For Kohl, while putting forward the words of Augustine, seems to criticize himself; he stubbornly denies that Ambrose <note>Ambrogio Traversari (1386–1439), a humanist monk and key translator of Greek and Syriac fathers into Latin.</note> was the Abbot General of the entire Camaldolese Order, even though he could have learned this with little effort from the author’s own testimony. Furthermore, Augustine, when he writes that the volume of Blessed Ephrem had never appeared in Latin until that point, is convicted of carelessness and falsehood by the four sermons which existed in Latin before the time of Ambrose, and which were later given a new translation by the same Ambrose, as we recorded in the Prolegomena <note>Preliminary introductory remarks.</note> on page LXXXI.

<margin>VI. Refutation of Tentzel and Kohl's claim that there were no other Latin translators of Ephrem besides Ambrose, Zini, and Voss.</margin>
VI. I proceed now to Zini and Voss. Neither Tentzel nor Kohl acknowledge any other Latin translators of Ephrem’s sermons from the Greek after Ambrose, except for Voss and Zini. However, from the volumes published by Luigi Lippomano and Laurentius Surius in the years 1554 and 1570, it appears that Julius Clemens—a Secular Canon of Saint George in Alga—contributed his efforts to rendering the *Testament of Saint Ephrem* into Latin from Greek manuscripts. It is also apparent from the catalogs we prefixed to the first volume on pages C and CII that certain other small works were translated from Greek into Latin and published by Jacob Gretser, François Combefis, and Jean-Baptiste Cotelier. Finally, in the Vatican manuscript codex 6170, there exist the following sermons of Saint Ephrem translated from Greek into Latin by Cardinal Sirleto <note>Guglielmo Sirleto (1514–1585), a cardinal and scholar involved in the Vatican Library.</note>. Namely:
I. A letter to the brothers concerning the day of death (page 1). Beginning: *We are about to pass, brothers.* 
II. A letter to the monk John concerning patience and temperance (page 3). Beginning: *Since we have often and extensively admonished the most religious Theodore.* 
III. On virtue, to a younger monk, First Admonition (page 9). Beginning: *When our Lord Jesus Christ, for the sake of human* <insert>A</insert> *salvation, had come into this world.*
IV. Second Admonition (page 13). Beginning: *One must beware of evil counsel.*
V. Admonition on Prudence (page 16). Beginning: *Be prudent as a serpent.* 
VI. Third Admonition on restraining evil thoughts (page 17). Beginning: *Concerning those things you wrote to me, namely these thoughts which usually disturb the mind itself.*
VII. Fourth Admonition, incomplete (page 22). Beginning: *A certain one of the number of holy men said: meditate on good things so you do not meditate on evil.*

<margin>VII. Both Zini mentioned the Ambrosian version, and Voss mentioned the Zinian version.</margin>
<insert>B</insert> Kohl wonders on page 275 why no mention was made of the Ambrosian version by Zini. Lambecius, Tillemont, Fabricius, Kohl, and others demand the same regarding Voss: namely, why he did not <insert>C</insert> praise Zini, Ambrose, or Julius Clemens, whose translations he nevertheless used. Indeed, for Voss’s part, I believe I have satisfied these unjust complaints in Chapter 7 of the Prolegomena on page XIX. I now add this one thing: express mention of Zini is made by Voss in Volume 3, page 116, in the notes to the *Confession of St. Ephrem*, where he gives the reason why he himself undertook to interpret this sermon which had already been translated long ago by Zini:

> "Because, surely, not a few things are found in this version which are missing in the earlier translation by Zini, perhaps due to a defect in the manuscripts; and also because some things are held and read differently here than they are there."

But Zini, because he adorned only a single sermon with a new interpretation that had already been converted long ago by Ambrose (it is that one whose beginning in Ambrose is <insert>E</insert> Sermon 7, *my heart grieves* <note>original: "cor meum doleo"</note>, and in Zini, Sermon 12, *I am tortured* <note>original: "excrutior"</note>), he thought it superfluous to make mention of the man whom he otherwise praised—though suppressing his name—in a letter given to Agostino Valerio in Venice on the day before the Ides of May, 1574, in these words: "If to that great Luigi Lippomano, as he himself testifies, in the difficult Polish Legation, one or another of those [sermons] brought so much <insert>F</insert> consolation, etc." Here he signifies the sermons of Ephrem, which Luigi Lippomano affirms he brought back to Italy from his Legation in Poland, carried in a litter <note>A "hexaphoro" is a litter or sedan chair carried by six people.</note>, so that the journey's...

->XXIV<-
->**PREFACE**<-
->A B C D E F<-

...he would read greedily, so as not to grow numb from the boredom of the journey, and he gathered material to illustrate his eighth volume on the saints. For the sermons of Ephrem described in that eighth volume do not have Zinus or others as their translator, but **Ambrose**, and they clearly display his name.

<margin>VIII. Praises and flaws of the Vossian edition.</margin>
Although I do not cling to the version by **Vossius** <note>Gerardus Vossius (1577–1649) was a Dutch scholar whose edition of Ephrem’s works was standard for centuries.</note> so strictly that I have proposed to follow it always and everywhere, I have not withdrawn from it by even a hair’s breadth unless the Greek text placed alongside it forced me to do otherwise.

“Vossius certainly deserves praise,” as **Kohlius** says on page 279, “because he collected the Greek writings of Ephrem with such diligence and care as no one ever had before, searching throughout all of Italy for whatever manuscripts were extant in various libraries, and he published them translated into the Latin language for the common benefit of all. Vossius's industry is also to be praised in this: that by diligently noting various readings from the rarest and oldest manuscripts he employed, he added <term>commonplaces</term> <note>original: "loca communia"; these are thematic indexes or collections of similar passages used for teaching.</note> to each of Ephrem's sermons. That is to say, he added writings of similar argument from both the Greek and Latin Fathers—especially for the use of those who handle sacred matters and explain holy discipline to the people—and he either explained or confirmed certain passages of Scripture using other clearer and parallel passages. He exhibited no less laudable labor in illustrating obscure places, amending corrupt ones, and confirming doubtful ones.”

Nevertheless, that version by the most learned translator also has not a few flaws. For sometimes, being more intent on the matter than the words, he set out to make things shorter; at other times, he employs luxuriant phrasing and heaps up things that are entirely redundant. In certain places, he even deflects from the true sense or expresses the author’s meaning in an obscure and distorted way. I say nothing of the minor faults, which no work, however polished and refined, can be entirely without. Therefore, having followed this translator, I judged that he should be preferred over the others and not abandoned by me, except where it is certain that some error has crept into his edition, or where the Greek manuscripts persuade us to express a variant reading differently in Latin. For this, in my opinion, is the best method of translating: to follow closely in the footsteps of the Syrian author as converted into Greek, and to weigh his words religiously and faithfully. Nor indeed do I think I have done Vossius an injury because I occasionally correct a slip, but I maintain and contend that I have served him well, because I have worked diligently to remove the flaws scattered over an otherwise excellent body of work (just as **Boivinius** says regarding Wolf’s version of **Nicephorus Gregora** <note>Nicephorus Gregora was a 14th-century Byzantine historian; Boivinius and Wolf were later scholars who edited his work.</note>).

<margin>IX. What our Latin commentators of the Leipzig Acts thought.</margin>
The learned editors of the <term>Leipzig Acts</term> <note>original: "Actorum Lipsiensium"; refers to the *Acta Eruditorum*, the first scientific journal in Germany, founded in 1682.</note> for the year 1738, starting on page 289, observe that in preparing this new edition, I have adhered to the Greek edition published at Oxford in 1709 in such a way that I have sometimes retained the reading given by that edition, even though another was provided in the margin. Sometimes, the marginal reading of the Oxford edition was preferred in our new edition over the reading shown in the text itself; and finally, not infrequently, both were departed from. Truly, in doubtful matters—where the text offered either no sense or a contradictory one—I followed the authority of the oldest manuscripts, especially those of our **Vatican Library**. I have therefore presented a catalog of these and the beginnings of each of Ephrem's sermons in alphabetical order, so that anyone who has the leisure may compare whatever has been published, whether in Greek or Latin, with the manuscripts.

<margin>X. On the translation from Greek to Latin, the number of manuscripts, and ignorance of the Syriac language.</margin>
The same learned censors offer the reason why I have sometimes departed from the Vossian version. *“He did not hesitate,”* they say, *“to either leave behind or emend that same Latin translation of Vossius in those places where it represents a sense different from the Greek.”* I preferred to wipe away these flaws of Vossius rather than prepare a new translation myself. I call them “flaws,” however, if that Latin version is compared to the Greek Oxford manuscripts or our own Vatican manuscripts. For who does not know that in such a great number of codices, one reading after another is frequently presented?

<page-num>XXV</page-num>

-> ### ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHREM THE SYRIAN. <-

<margin>...that these are variant readings of Ephrem's works.</margin>

...and this should be attributed not only to the choice or carelessness of scribes, but also to the nature of the Greek translators. Indeed, several Vatican manuscripts present us with different interpretations of one and the same discourse by Ephrem. I believe this is why—just as the Latin versions we possess by anonymous authors, as well as by <term>Ambrose</term><note>likely refers to Ambrogio Traversari</note>, Zinus, Vossius, Julius, Clement, Combefis, and Cotelier, do not entirely agree with one another—so too the Greek interpretations produced by various authors do not always render the same meaning, or at least not in the same words. 

There are, in fact, those who rendered the text word-for-word when interpreting; others, content to retain the general sense and indulging in the nature of the Greek language, used circumlocutions. Finally, not a few, owing to the difficulty or their own ignorance of the Syriac language, wandered quite far from the holy Father’s genuine intent. As a witness to this matter, I call first upon <term>Photius</term><note>A 9th-century scholar and Patriarch of Constantinople</note>, who notes in his *Library* (chapter 196) that Ephrem’s translators sometimes erred in this regard:

"It is no wonder if his speech and figures of speech incline toward the common use of homilies and an unrefined style of speaking; for this fault is not to be laid upon the author of the ideas, but upon their translator. For those who are well-skilled in the Syriac language know how to excel so much in words and metaphors that it is uncertain whether his great power and elegance of speech proceed from these or from the hidden meaning."

Indeed, from the Syriac discourses that survive, it clearly appears that the Greek translators did not always or everywhere hold the art of translation so exactly as to capture the author's sublime style and lofty thoughts; on the contrary, when a comparison is made with the Syriac text, it is discovered that they more than once wandered from the true meaning, or left in obscurity things that are full and clear in the author’s original. Examples, which we shall indicate very briefly, will provide proof of this.

On page 230, in the discourse entitled *The Testament of Blessed Ephrem*, the Greek interpretation has: "For I leave you a testament, a symbol for a memorial of teachings, from that grace and gift which was granted to me there." <note>Original Greek: διαθήκην γὰρ ὑμῖν καταλιμπάνω, σύμβολον μνημοσύνης διδαγμάτων, ἐξ ἐκείνης, οἷς μοι ὑπῆρξε χάριτος καὶ δωρεᾶς</note>. 

<margin>A</margin> But the Syriac text on page 395, according to Ephrem’s own mind, represents only this:

> *I write a testament, so that it may be a memorial for you of those things which I possess.* 
> <note>Original Syriac: ܕܝܬܝܩܝ ܓܝܪ ܐܢܐ ܫܡ ܐܢܐ ܕܬܗܘܐ ܠܟܘܢ ܕܘܟܪܢܐ ܡܢ ܐܝܠܝܢ ܕܩܢܝ ܐܢܐ</note>

In Volume 2 of the Syriac-Latin edition, page 360, in the discourse 
<margin>B</margin> concerning the Prophet Jonah and the Ninevites:

> *From his own experience, Jonah learned that those who perform penance deserve to live. For divine grace gave him a form and a vision, so that as he himself had been snatched from the sea, he might save the sinking city.*
> <note>Original Syriac text omitted here for brevity, but follows the provided Latin translation</note>

Which words <term>Vossius</term><note>Gerardus Vossius, a 17th-century scholar</note> renders from the Greek in Volume 3, page 150, as follows: 
"And from himself the Prophet Jonah learned that it is just for those who do penance to live: for divine grace gave him a form and an example. Jonah, performing penance, returns from the sea to lead back the submerged city." 

<margin>C</margin> I omit other examples which, to anyone comparing both <margin>D</margin> discourses, make it plain that the Greek or Latin translators did not always reach Ephrem's true intent.

### XI. Vossius's good faith is defended against Kohl and others.

However, I have not said these things because I share the opinion of Kohl, who calls Vossius's honesty into doubt, or because I subscribe to the judgment of Rivet and Tentzel, who reject all those works of Ephrem that Vossius published in translation 
<margin>E</margin> as spurious and adulterated. On the contrary, I am quite persuaded, and I firmly maintain, that Vossius did not try to sell under Ephrem’s name anything that was not inscribed with that name in the Greek manuscripts; nor, in translating those works into Latin, did he thrust upon us a meaning foreign to the truth of the genuine sense of the Greek codices. 

<margin>F</margin> This very point is proven by the Greek copies—not only those preceding the age of Vossius, but those which survive from eight hundred and even nine hundred years ago—in which all those discourses appear in Greek that Vossius later published in Latin. Rivet should have consulted those manuscripts, and Tentzel should have studied them, before...

# PROLEGOMENA
<note>Latin for "Preface" or "Introduction."</note>

...how unworthy of an honest man they would find this opinion expressed against Vossius on page 285:
> “Let our friend Dupin, therefore, make a trial, and weigh Vossius’s translation against the Greek manuscripts in his King’s Library, and thereafter inform the learned world whether he has not undertaken to cleanse the **Augean stable**?” 
<note>The "Augean stable" is a mythological reference to a task of cleaning a place of extreme filth; here it implies Vossius's work was a mess that needed cleaning.</note>

Let Tentzelius himself make a trial, and weigh Vossius’s translation against the Greek codices <note>Handwritten books</note> of France, Germany, England, and Moscow, and thereafter inform the learned world whether Vossius has not faithfully transcribed from the Greek manuscripts everything he rendered into Latin?

<margin>XII. That Vossius, in preparing his version, followed the reliability of the Greek manuscripts.</margin>
But this, says Kohlius on page 281:
> “is turned into a fault against Vossius: that in translating the writings of **Ephrem** <note>Saint Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373), a prolific Syriac deacon and hymnographer whose works were widely translated into Greek and Latin.</note> into the Latin language, he showed himself to be anything but a faithful and sincere interpreter; instead, according to his own whim, he omitted some things, changed others, and even interpolated <note>Inserted new material</note> many things and thrust them into the text.” 

The same Kohlius, on page 281, following Tillemont (Volume 8, in the notes on page 746), rebukes the same Vossius:
> “because for some unknown reason he often divided and separated those things which were coherent and joined in the manuscripts, and often tore apart and separated entire parts of one and the same body of work.”

One cannot help but wonder here—and I will not hide it—that the mind of a man otherwise not unlearned should be so carried away by **partisan zeal** that he seems to be **blind in broad daylight**. <note>A Latin idiom for failing to see something completely obvious.</note> For he could have—even from the titles of those sermons of Ephrem which he himself accurately reviews in Greek and Slavonic on pages 245 and 304—he could have, I say, with little effort been made certain that Vossius, in translating Ephrem’s writings into Latin, omitted nothing, changed nothing, interpolated or thrust nothing into the text, and finally, divided or separated nothing that is coherent and joined in the manuscripts. If there is some discrepancy in the order of the sermons, or some diversity in the thoughts or the division of parts, this does not arise from the whim of Vossius the translator, but from the manuscript <term>codices</term> <note>ancient handwritten volumes</note> themselves, of which one can hardly find one that agrees entirely with another. Let Kohlius compare the Moscow codices with those of the **Coislin**, **Ottoboni**, **Vatican**, and others, and judge whether he himself could have surpassed Vossius’s diligence if he had undertaken to bring together sermons scattered across so many volumes into a single book. Let him consult Vossius himself in the notes to the sermons of Ephrem, and see whether he does not everywhere give a reason why he sometimes thought that things which were joined in some codices should be divided based on the authority of others, and why he joined things that were divided elsewhere. For Kohlius’s benefit, I produce a few examples from Vossius. In Volume 1, page 245, in the note to the sermon titled *On those things to be revealed after the Cross*, which begins *“You have heard, most beloved,”* he has this to say:

> “These words, along with others preceding them in praise of the holy and life-giving Cross, are joined in the same context and thread of the discourse; but because this part is also found separately with its own title in the ancient **Grottaferrata** codex, <note>original: "Cryptaferratensi"; referring to the Abbey of Santa Maria di Grottaferrata, famous for its Greek manuscript collection.</note> it seemed right to us to separate it and place it here as a more appropriate location.”

Likewise, in Volume 3, page 207, regarding *“You have heard, most beloved, etc.”*: “Since this part is found separately with its own title in certain Greek manuscripts, we have therefore placed it separately toward the end of the first volume of Ephrem, before the sermons on the Last Judgment, as they appear in the ancient Greek manuscript of Grottaferrata. But because in three Vatican manuscripts, as well as the **Sirleto** and **Cretan** (or Candian) manuscripts and others, these following parts are placed consequently after the earlier words on the Cross, it seemed right to also leave and read them here in a joined manner.”

Finally, in the same Volume 3, page 58, in the note to the <term>ascetic</term> <note>Related to self-discipline and spiritual exercise.</note> sermon on the religious life, which begins *“Labor and pain compel me to speak and say,”* regarding the second part of the same sermon, which begins on page 52, *“Therefore let us be sober and watch, brothers,”* he provides the reason why he joined that part with the former. “These,” he says, “are distinguished and separated from the preceding by a new chapter and title, and many more things are added here in the version by **the Camaldolese** <note>original: "Camaldulensis"; referring to the translation by Ambrogio Traversari.</note> and in another older version by an uncertain translator; since these are found elsewhere in Ephrem in the 50th **Parænesis** <note>original: "Paræn. 50"; a Parænesis is a sermon of moral exhortation or advice.</note> of Volume 2, according to...”

XXVII

-> ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHREM THE SYRIAN. <-
d 2
<meta>Volume II; catchword: formatæ</meta>

<margin>
XIII.
The passage in Vossius’s interpretation, Volume 3, page 201, is examined.
</margin>

> "...our edition, having deliberately omitted these things here, we have conformed to the Greek and Roman copies (where they are also missing in this place); and we have replaced these, as they were there joined together, without a new title or chapter."

Thus far Vossius. Therefore, he did not prepare his version by his own whim, but based on the reliability of the Greek manuscripts.

[B] I admit that certain things escaped Vossius—as happens to any man—which can hardly be excused. One such example is in Volume 3, page 201, in the sermon *On the Precious and Life-Giving Cross*, at the beginning: *all the celebration of our Lord Jesus Christ:* where he introduces Ephrem describing the triumphs of the cross in this way:

> "The error of idols has been destroyed, [C] death has been despoiled, the captivity of the underworld has been called back, the empire of the error of many gods has been shattered, man has been liberated, **the Lord has reigned from the wood**, <note>original Latin: *Dominus regnavit a ligno*. This is a famous variation of Psalm 96:10 (95 in the Vulgate). While standard Bibles read "The Lord reigns," many early Church Fathers added "from the wood" to interpret it as a prophecy of the Crucifixion.</note> the creation rejoices, the cross dominates."

In the Oxford edition, page 377, and in Gretser's work *On the Cross of Christ*, Volume 2, page 475, it reads: *The Lord has reigned* <note>original Greek: ὁ Κύριος ἐβασίλευσεν</note>. The ten Vatican manuscripts [D] which I reviewed in Volume 1, page CXCVIII, also have it this way. I followed their reliability in this Volume 2, page 248. Vossius, however, not only reads "The Lord has reigned from the wood," but also in the notes to this passage on page 206, he indicates that the same words are represented in the Greek text of Ephrem:

> "In St. Augustine, [E] and in the Old Psalter, and the Roman one, there is added: **FROM THE WOOD**, as here in Ephrem; just as it is also read by St. Cyprian, Justin Martyr, Tertullian, and Pope St. Leo, surnamed the Great, in his 4th sermon *On the Passion of the Lord*."

From which Greek manuscript he actually drew this, however, is not clear. Yet it is certain that in his interpretation of that sermon, he used several Greek manuscripts: three Vatican ones, [F] the Sirletan, the Cretan, and the Grottaferrata manuscripts. For in the note to those words on page 202, *Regarding those things which will happen after the cross: You have heard, most beloved:* giving the reason why he had published this same sermon separately in Volume 1, page 243, he mentions on page 207 the manuscript from which he had excerpted it:

> "This part," he says, "since it is found separately with its own title in certain Greek manuscripts, we have therefore also placed separately near the end of the first volume of Ephrem, before the sermons on the Last Judgment, as they appear in the ancient Greek manuscript of Grottaferrata. But because in three Vatican manuscripts, as well as the Sirletan, and the Cretan (or Candian), and others, these following parts are placed sequentially after the earlier ones regarding the Cross, it seemed best to leave them here joined together as well."

I have deliberately chosen to repeat here what I had recited from Vossius above. For by these words, not only is the reliability of the translator established in other matters, but Vossius also defends himself in this instance, indicating that the sentence "The Lord has reigned from the wood" is displayed in at least some of the cited manuscripts. Since we have not yet been able to consult all those manuscripts, we can neither rightly condemn Vossius nor fully defend him. Meanwhile, from Vossius's own words, we repel the injury brought against him by Kohl <note>Johann Peter Kohl (Kohlius), a scholar known for critiquing previous editions of patristic texts.</note>, who on page 291 accuses him of drowsiness and negligence because the same things often recur in his version, proposed in almost the same words. However, Kohl later changed his mind and pronounced a milder judgment on page 295; while he had previously cited repetitions from four of Ephrem's sermons on pages 292 and 294, he later thought they arose because:

> "the sermons which were repeated twice were inscribed differently in other manuscripts and perhaps had a different beginning when they presented themselves to the translator."

Nothing is more inconstant than the inconsistency of a man who delivers such diverse judgments on the same matters in one and the same place.

<margin>
XIV.
Kohl's slander against Vossius is refuted.
</margin>

It stands, therefore, that the Vossian version is contaminated by certain blemishes and flaws, which we do not deny. But we certainly deny what Kohl blathers on page 281: that Vossius did this with no other purpose "than to set sail for the Papal cause and to bring some color to the errors of his own sect." We refer Kohl himself to the Greek manuscripts—not just the Roman, Florentine, or Venetian ones, but the Parisian, Viennese, Spanish, and those which even a man not estranged from the Roman communion could not suspect of doubtful reliability: the English, Dutch, and Muscovite manuscripts. We send Kohl to all these manuscripts so that, if the Reformed cause...

The author defends Vossius against accusations of bias and negligence made by the critic Kohl, arguing that variations likely stem from the diverse Greek manuscripts Vossius consulted.</summary>

### PREFACE

<meta>image quality: excellent; layout: two-column text with vertical letter markers A-F in the intercolumnar space; long s modernized to s; catchword: quam,</meta>

if he wishes to sail with the wind of a fashioned sect and bring some color of legitimacy to the errors of his own party, let him produce for us even one tiny word that was omitted, changed, or interpolated <note>Vossius (1577–1649) was a Dutch scholar whose editions of the Church Fathers were sometimes accused by Protestant critics of being biased toward Roman Catholic views.</note> by Vossius. Let him bring forth even one sentence through which Vossius attempted to bolster the Catholic dogmas of our entire Church—claims which are supposedly not present in the Greek sermons of Ephrem. <note>Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373 AD), a prominent theologian and hymnographer whose works were widely translated into Greek and Latin.</note> Although men of weak and stubborn character are often accustomed to making such assertions, they are unable to prove or demonstrate them. In opposition to them, we point to the Greek codices <note>A "codex" is an ancient manuscript in book form.</note> preserved in their own libraries, by which the Apostolic doctrine of the Holy Roman Church is wonderfully confirmed, and the edition prepared by Vossius is vindicated from calumnies of this sort.

<margin>XV. The impudence of Kohl, railing against the Catholics</margin>
XV. Kohl <note>Johann Peter Kohl (1698–1778), a German scholar who criticized Catholic editions of early Christian texts.</note> proceeds to support a poorly pronounced judgment with even worse arguments. He says in the place cited: "This Latin version by Vossius is rendered suspect of bad faith by that ill-timed diligence of the Catholics in suppressing the Greek writings of Ephrem. For if Vossius had translated the Greek with the faith and sincerity that was proper, what need was there to suppress these very manuscript codices he used so studiously and cautiously even to this day? Why would they not wish them to be published, like so many other writings of the Fathers which have emerged most splendidly from the workshops of Italy?" What reasoning unworthy of a rational animal! Who told Kohl that those codices were suppressed by the Catholics to prevent them from being brought to light? Indeed, if they had emerged from the Italian workshops, would not this same Kohl say the same thing about them that he pronounces about the Vossian version: namely, that they were interpolated <note>altered or edited with unauthorized text</note> by the Catholics to favor their cause? Why, then, does he not instead recur to the sources that exist in the aforementioned libraries of the Reformers and Protestants, so that he might support with certain documents that which he so impudently hawks while beating the air? Finally, if he is wise, let him examine the most splendid Oxford edition, prepared from English codices, not Roman ones; and if he finds there even the slightest hint of such an injurious suspicion against the Catholics, let him bring it forth into the light of day.

---

<margin>A</margin> But let us hear on what foundation Kohl heaps this slander upon us. He cites the deed of a certain French Abbot, whom he says (on page 282), despite whatever diligence and finally through most earnest prayers, requested several manuscript codices from the Vatican Library for his new Greek-Latin edition of Ephrem <note>This refers to a planned scholarly edition that would show both the original Greek and the Latin translation side-by-side.</note> which he was preparing, <margin>B</margin> having been already begun by Aubert, to be arranged in the name of the entire French Clergy. He claims, however, that he received a rejection instead of the codices. He brings forth Tillemont <note>Louis-Sébastien Le Nain de Tillemont (1637–1698), a famous French ecclesiastical historian.</note> himself as a witness to this matter, who wrote in Volume 8, page 746: *The Lord Abbot of Bourges of Chaise-Dieu, a doctor distinguished for piety and most worthy of trust, affirmed to me that the Greek text was denied to him at Rome, even though he had requested it in the name of the Bishops of France, who wished to publish it in print.* This is an old complaint of <margin>C</margin> scoundrels. When they undertake a journey to Italy at the expense of others to engage in some literary work, they spend their days and nights overflowing in luxuries rather than enduring labors. Returning to their homeland with empty hands, as they say, they remove all blame from themselves and <margin>D</margin> cast it upon the Vatican Library. Yet, on appointed days, as is the custom in libraries throughout the world, the Vatican is open not only to honorable and learned men, but even to commoners. But granted that some codices are sometimes denied to some people by the Custodians; nevertheless, if that Abbot of Bourges had been sent to that work by the Gallican Clergy, <margin>E</margin> he could have easily obtained them through the French Royal Orator <note>a diplomatic representative or ambassador</note>, or through the Cardinals and other princely men who are exceedingly well-disposed toward the inhabitants of that Kingdom, by appealing to the Supreme Pontiff or the Cardinal Librarian so that a supply of those codices might be made available to him. We see this happen every day in Italy, France, Spain, and Germany; namely, <margin>F</margin> that access to the more private libraries is opened to men adorned with some dignity whenever the favor of Princes is involved. Therefore, that Abbot, although he is said to be distinguished for piety and most worthy of trust, seems to have deceived Tillemont; or Tillemont, being more credulous than is just, wished to abuse the patience of the literary world. For who was forbidding him from visiting other Roman libraries, such as the Barberini, or...

XXIX

### ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHREM THE SYRIAN.

<note>The author is arguing that if the Vatican had really blocked access to St. Ephrem's Greek manuscripts, the scholar in question had many other options.</note> ...the Sfortian, Altempsian, and Grottaferrata libraries, in which at that time there existed a great many Greek codices <term>codices<gloss>handwritten manuscript books</gloss></term> of Ephrem, if he had been excluded from the Vatican? Who would have denied the Abbot of Bourgueil <note>original: "Abbati Burgensi," referring to a French scholar-monk</note> access to the libraries of Florence, the Ambrosian in Milan, or the Venetian libraries, where he could have quenched his thirst for knowledge? Finally, who would have dared to bar a Frenchman, working on behalf of the French Clergy, from the Royal Library in Paris itself, or from the Coislinian collection, to prevent him from consulting, transcribing, interpreting, and bringing to light the Greek works of Ephrem? Since, therefore, that Abbot did none of these things, nor even attempted to do them, it is proof that the man sold a grand tall tale to Tillemont <note>Louis-Sébastien Le Nain de Tillemont, a famous 17th-century ecclesiastical historian</note>, and Tillemont sold it to us—the story that the Greek Ephremian codices of the Vatican Library were so ungenerously denied.

<margin>XVII. The puerile and unworthy gullibility of William Cave.</margin> Nor is it a wonder that this occurred to the mind of the Abbot of Bourgueil, or that it was recorded in writing by Tillemont. Others have spewed even more atrocious crimes against the Vatican Library. William Cave <note>An English church historian (1637–1713)</note>, in his *Prolegomena to the Literary History of Ecclesiastical Writers* (section 5, page 16), while handing down general rules for distinguishing genuine editions of the Holy Fathers from corrupt ones, tells a fable unworthy of such a critic, which he asserts was reported to him by his kinsman James <note>Thomas James (1573–1629), the first librarian of the Bodleian Library and a fierce critic of the Vatican</note>:

> _Namely, that in the Vatican Library no few "painting-artists" are maintained daily, whose job it was to transcribe the Acts of the Councils and the works of the ancient Fathers, and to do so in letters imitating the most ancient codices as much as possible. With what intent this was done, they themselves know; certainly, James testifies that he received this account from the mouth of a certain most noble Englishman, who had surveyed these things in the Vatican Library with his own eyes, and who spontaneously offered to strengthen the credibility of the story with his own oath._

I think he meant to say "writing-artists" <note>original: "scribendi artifices," meaning skilled scribes</note>, not "painters" <note>original: "pictores"</note> skilled in painting: for the former, not the latter, are accustomed to transcribing codices among us in the Vatican Library. Unless, perhaps, William Cave looked toward our own age and divined that eventually, after fifty-four years, Father Antonio Piagi <note>A Piarist monk famous for inventing a machine to unroll the carbonized scrolls from Herculaneum</note>, a religious professor of the Pious Schools, through the benefit and providence of the Most Holy Father **BENEDICT XIV**—the patron and Maecenas of all good arts—would obtain the dual role of both Painter and Scribe in the Vatican Library on account of his singular skill in transcribing and painting.

<margin>A</margin> But away with the fictions of James and Cave! <margin>B</margin> Painters are indeed maintained at the Pontifical expense, as are Scribes and learned professors of every kind of art and science. This yields the highest praise to the Roman Pontiffs, who strive to contribute their effort and expense not only to the orthodox religion but also to the promotion of the fine arts. But the idea that Painters and Scribes are maintained in the Vatican Library <margin>C</margin> (as Cave does not blush to report from some scoundrel) for the purpose of corrupting the Acts of the Councils and the ancient writings of the Fathers—that is something anyone who has even stood on the threshold of antiquarian studies would judge more worthy of laughter than a serious response.

Indeed, Antiquarians know which writings are ancient and <margin>D</margin> which are recent; they discern between parchment, papyrus, and paper. They distinguish Greek, Hebrew, Syriac, Arabic, and Latin letters—whether they are very ancient, from the Middle Ages, or more recent. The English themselves, to whom Cave appeals, are not such strangers to these studies that they would allow themselves to be deceived by those who would foist upon them recent copies as the oldest codices, even if described in letters imitating antiquity as much as possible. <margin>E</margin> Nor do these men, being most studious of Antiquities, cease to visit the Vatican Library daily, viewing and re-viewing the pages, locations, and shelf-marks of ancient codices of every kind, and most diligently recording everything into notebooks and diaries so that nothing escapes them upon their return to their homeland. <margin>F</margin> I will not even mention the many learned men from England, Germany, and France who flock here daily; when they are preparing new editions of Authors, they undertake the transcription of Vatican volumes, extolling their reliability and authority to the heavens. Yet, to this day, not one of them, except for James, has thought or said such unworthy things about the Vatican Library. But I am loath to dwell on these matters any longer.

CA-

->PREFACE<-

# CHAPTER III.

## On the credibility and authority of the Greek and Latin manuscript codices containing the sermons of Ephrem.

---

<margin>I. The preposterous judgment of Rivet and Tentzel regarding the sermons of Ephrem translated into Latin from Greek.</margin>
**W**ilhelm Ernst Tentzel, in his *Select Exercises* <note>Wilhelm Ernst Tentzel (1659–1707) was a German historian and numismatist</note>, page 285, confidently denies that any of the Greek versions preserved in various libraries deserve any authority or trust. "Allow us," he says, "to doubt them all until a Syriac copy appears and makes it clear whether, and which, translators acted in good or bad faith in the translation." André Rivet <note>André Rivet (1572–1651) was a French Huguenot theologian</note> shows himself an equally rigid critic in his *Sacred Critique*, to use the words of Kohl <note>Johann Peter Kohl (1698–1778), a scholar of Oriental languages and church history</note> on page 279, when he pronounces all those works of Ephrem that Vossius <note>Gerardus Vossius (1577–1649) published a famous edition of Ephrem's works</note> published in translation to be thrust upon us as genuine <note>original Latin: "pro genuinis nobis obtrusa"</note>.

<margin>II. Kohl admits several of Ephrem’s sermons but rejects some as spurious.</margin>
Kohl himself, thinking he should distance himself from both opinions and take a middle path, does not hesitate to embrace most of what is contained in the first volume of the Vossius edition as genuine, along with a few from the second volume. However, he declares that the vast majority of the writings in the third volume must be considered counterfeit <note>original Latin: "supposititia"</note>. He lists which ones they are on page 286: *The first Ascetic Sermon is spurious, written in imitation of the Proverbs; though Tillemont <note>Louis-Sébastien Le Nain de Tillemont (1637–1698), a French ecclesiastical historian</note> considers it not entirely trustworthy, he does not think it should be totally rejected. Likewise, the Sermon on the Precious Pearl; although Tillemont deems it of indubitable faith, we reject it as too obscure and too subtly philosophical. To the adulterated <note>meaning forged or altered</note> sermons also belongs that famous sermon titled "Confession and Self-Reproof," which begins here:*
> "Since I seem to be of use to many of
> you, brothers. In the same category I
> place the sermon on page 539, *On 
> Religious and Monastic Life*, which 
> seemed suspect even to Tillemont. 
> Likewise, the "Lamentations of the 
> most glorious Virgin Mother Mary 
> over the Passion of the Lord" (page 
> 697), "On the Sinful Woman" (page 
> 711), "In Praise of Mary" (page 705), 
> "On Saint Basil the Great" (page 
> 724), "On the Forty Holy Martyrs" 
> (page 727), and finally the "Testament 
> of Ephrem," which is the last in the 
> Vossius collection, and the falsity of 
> which we will demonstrate at length 
> below. He adds among the adulterated 
> and <term>spurious<gloss>original Greek: ὑποβολιμαίους (hypobolimaious), meaning substituted or counterfeit</gloss></term> (page 237) the sermon <term>on the departed Fathers<gloss>original Greek: εἰς πατέρας τελειωθέντας (eis pateras teleiothentas), referring to monks who have died</gloss></term>.

---

<margin>The Greek translators of Ephrem are rashly regarded as impostors by Rivet and Tentzel.</margin>
Before I present the grounds upon which Kohl rejects the mentioned sermons of Ephrem as spurious and adulterated, it seemed appropriate to examine the opinion of Tentzel and Rivet and show how erroneous it is. For it is surprising that men who wish to appear as students of antiquity would accuse all the Greek translators of Ephrem of bad faith, when they can offer no reason why the Greeks would attribute such works to the Syrian Father. Such works would bring no glory to their own nation, nor any authority to their doctrine or religion that they did not already possess in greater measure from the Greek Fathers. What of the fact that if, according to Rivet and Tentzel, the writings of the Holy Fathers were to be rejected at the whim of anyone as spurious for no other reason than that the original text from which those copies were transcribed has not yet been seen, it would be the end for those authors? Many fragments of authors who wrote in Chaldean, Phoenician, or Egyptian are preserved only in Greek by ecclesiastical or secular writers—such as those precious relics of venerable antiquity which Josephus the Hebrew, Clement of Alexandria, and Eusebius of Caesarea inserted into their books and transmitted to posterity. Who in their right mind would say such a thing? Indeed, learned men agree in...

<meta>The catchword "niunt" at the bottom of the original page is the end of the word "conveniunt," meaning "they agree" or "they come together," which continues on the next page.</meta>

The author defends the Greek manuscript tradition by comparing it to other ancient texts preserved only in translation, such as those cited by Josephus and Eusebius.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of the Greek translations of Saint Ephrem the Syrian’s works. It follows a discussion of how ancient texts are often preserved only in translation. The layout uses a two-column reference system (A, B, C, D, E) in the margins.</meta>

-> <page-num>31</page-num> <-
-> ON THE WORKS OF SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN. <-

...it is well known that many works of the Fathers and illustrious writers, which were first composed in Greek or Latin, would not have reached us if they had not been translated into another language, the original text having been lost. Examples include many letters of the Roman Pontiffs written by them in Latin, which now exist only in Greek in the works of the praised Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea, a 4th-century church historian.</note> or in the Acts of the Councils; and the books on conics by Apollonius of Perga, <note>A Greek geometer known for his work on conic sections.</note> which are preserved in Arabic. Into this category must be placed several works of the holy Fathers or of heretics which we possess only in Armenian and Syriac, the catalog of which I have reviewed in Volumes 1, 2, and 3 of the *Eastern Library* <note>Original: *Bibliotheca Orientalis*, the author’s own major bibliographic work.</note>. Whether all these should indeed be rejected, as Rivetus and Tentzelius <note>André Rivet and Wilhelm Ernst Tentzel, influential Protestant scholars known for their rigorous—and sometimes dismissive—criticism of ancient texts.</note> suggest, until a Syriac, Greek, or Latin original appears to make it manifest whether the translators acted in good or bad faith, let others who exercise judgment decide. For our part, to establish the due credit of the Greek translators of Ephrem, we do not need general arguments of that sort, but can bring forward other invincible reasons drawn from authors almost contemporary with Ephrem. For those Fathers who praised the wisdom of Ephrem and his learned works in Greek had neither seen the Syriac text nor, perhaps, did they understand it—such as Gregory of Nyssa, Ephrem of Antioch, Leontius of Byzantium, Anastasius of Sinai, Theodore the Studite, Theodorus Prodromus, Antiochus the Monk, and others whom I have praised in the Introductions <margin>A</margin> to Volume 1, Chapter 1. 

Photius also <note>A 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople and a famous literary critic.</note>, that rigid and accurate censor of genuine works, even though he places blame on the Greek translators of Ephrem’s sermons (which he reviews in his *Library*, codex 196) because those sermons do not excel in words and metaphors in the Greek version as they do in the Syriac text; nevertheless, he does not <margin>B</margin> regard them as spurious, as Rivetus and Tentzelius do. Instead, he praises and admires how deeply the power of persuasion penetrates within them, how much delight flows from them, and finally, how full they are of an abundance of emotions. Nor does he offer such a judgment only regarding those he had read; he also writes that he holds in the same high esteem *many other short works of the holy man which are circulated, having been translated into the Greek language.* <margin>C</margin> Finally, Sozomen <note>A 5th-century historian of the Christian Church.</note> in his *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 3, Chapter 16, is the authority for the fact that *while Ephrem was still alive, his books were translated into the Greek tongue, and they did not cease to be translated up until his own time.* From these words, and especially from the catalog of those sermons which Gregory of Nyssa, Ephrem of Antioch, Leontius of Byzantium, <margin>D</margin> Anastasius of Sinai, and Photius enumerate, it is clearly gathered how undeservedly Rivetus and Tentzel rail against the Greek codices of Ephrem, and against Vossius <note>Gerardus Vossius, who published a famous Latin edition of Ephrem's works in the late 16th century.</note>, who gave them to the Latin world, since both those codices and the Latin translation of Vossius present us with the very same sermons <margin>E</margin> praised by the most ancient as well as the most learned authors.

---

## C H A P T E R IV.

### *Regarding the Sermons of Ephrem which are Unjustly Rejected as Spurious.*

**N**ow I return to Kohllius <note>Johann Peter Kohl, a scholar who wrote about the history of literature.</note>: to whom I profess myself most grateful that, on page 285, *he judges that most of the works contained in the first volume of the Vossius edition should be considered genuine.* For thus, in one word, he acquits nearly ninety sermons of Ephrem translated there by Vossius from the fault of being <term>spurious</term> <note>Original: *νοθείας* (notheias), a Greek term for "bastardy" or "corruption," used here to mean "forged" or "unauthentic".</note>. <margin>He translates the sermons as if they were counterfeit.</margin> But as for what he subjoins—that *few of the second volume meet his approval, and the greatest part of those in the third volume are regarded as supposititious* <note>Meaning substituted or fake.</note>—I cannot approve of that, since it does not agree with what Kohllius himself had pronounced on page 244 concerning the Greek Moscow codices, in these words: *Among other codices, the Greek writings of Ephrem...*

<page-num>XXXII</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

...containing these writings, those five parchments still residing in the Imperial Moscow Library easily stand out and excel in everyone's judgment. Whether you consider their antiquity (which adds great value to the codices), or their <term>authenticity</term> <note>original: γνησιό-τητα (gnesiotes)</note> and the purity of the text, or finally the diverse collection of unpublished writings they contain, no other manuscripts hidden anywhere else can easily be compared with these. 

For, whatever the case may be regarding those Greek Moscow manuscripts—and I certainly do not deny they possess much value—I would argue more confidently than Kohl asserts that no other manuscripts anywhere can be compared with them. In fact, at Rome, Florence, Milan, Venice, Paris, Vienna in Austria, and elsewhere, there exist manuscript works of Ephrem <note>Ephrem the Syrian, a prolific 4th-century deacon and hymnwriter</note> far older than the Moscow ones, as is clearly shown in our Introduction to Volume I from page 103 to page 181. These same works, which Vossius <note>Gerardus Vossius, a 17th-century scholar who edited Ephrem's works</note> published in Volumes 2 and 3, are contained in those Moscow manuscripts. They are also found in the Slavonic edition, which Kohl <note>Johann Peter Kohl, a scholar of Slavonic literature</note> values so highly that he thinks it should be considered equal to, if not preferred over, the oldest Greek manuscripts themselves, owing to the diligence of the translators and the antiquity of the manuscript sources. On page 341, he writes: 

*“Two things especially recommend the Slavonic version: First, the sufficiently accurate diligence in translation by the interpreters, who were no less skilled in the Slavonic language than in the Greek. Second, the antiquity of the manuscripts which they had at hand and followed while translating. Indeed, by carefully comparing the Slavonic with the existing Greek discourses, we have found such congruence and similarity in most places that individual points seemed expressed in almost the same words and phrases. Furthermore, the antiquity of the manuscripts used by the translators is sufficiently proven by the conformity of the Slavonic codex with that very ancient Grottaferrata manuscript which Vossius used, as we have already indicated above.”*

Does not such a glowing judgment by Kohl regarding the Slavonic edition and the Greek Moscow manuscripts convict Kohl himself of inconsistency? He separates many parts of the second 

<margin>A</margin> **Vossian Volume** from the forged works, yet he considers the largest part of the third volume to be counterfeit. He ought to have declared that most—indeed, all—of both volumes were free and immune from the defect of forgery. For these works, as is clear from the catalogs, are contained in both the Moscow manuscripts and the Slavonic edition; and, what is most important, they are praised 
<margin>B</margin> by Photius <note>A 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople and renowned scholar</note> in his *Bibliotheca*. Finally, they exist for the most part in the Arabic version, which I reviewed in Volume I of the *Eastern Library* <note>Bibliotheca Orientalis</note> from page 150, based on manuscripts written in the years 1325 and 1329 AD. Why Kohl thinks more weight should be given to the Arabic and Slavonic versions, and even the Greek Moscow codices, than to 
<margin>C</margin> Vossius's Latin edition or the very ancient Greek manuscripts from which he transcribed it, I wish he would explain himself. For my part—though I must not pass over in silence my respect for this most distinguished man because he makes very honorable mention of me in his work whenever the opportunity arises, especially regarding this Greek, Slavonic, and Arabic version of Ephrem of which we speak—
<margin>D</margin> for the love of truth, I cannot hide that all learned men are deeply annoyed by this reasoning of the otherwise learned Kohl. He embraces with "both arms," as they say, the Slavonic version used by the Russians and 
<margin>E</margin> the Arabic version used by the Christians of Syria and Egypt (versions which were once translated, revised, and corrected according to the reliability of Greek manuscripts); yet he calls the Latin version, which Vossius translated from those same Greek manuscripts, a version of "doubtful reliability." What is even more surprising is that he characterizes the Greek text from which those copies were taken as if it were forged and corrupted. What then? Is this 
<margin>F</margin> not putting the streams before the fountain?

But let us now move from generalities to particulars. *“The first ascetic discourse, written in imitation of the proverbs, is forged,”* says Kohl. This appeared *in Latin* in Vossius, volume 3, page 1 to page 28 of the Roman edition; *in Greek* in the Oxford edition from page 46 to page 76; and *in Greek and Latin* in our edition, Volume 1, from page 70 to

Moreover, it survives in Greek, written by hand in the Vatican-Alexandrine codex 16, page 193; the Imperial Vienna codex 31, page 290; the Laud codices 97 & 99; the Coislin codex 59, page 227; the Ottoboni codex 2, page 46; the Medici-Laurentian codex 28, page 111; twice in the Passionei <note>Referring to the collection of Cardinal Domenico Passionei (1682–1761).</note> collection, namely at pages 268 & 374; and in the Venetian codex of St. Mark 51, page 33. In Arabic, it is number 6 in our Arabic codex 8, written in the year of Christ 1325. Volume 1, *Oriental Library*, page 151. Indeed, I do not see what in this sermon might be unworthy of Ephrem, unless Kohl <note>Johann Peter Kohl (1698–1778), a scholar who questioned the authenticity of several works attributed to Ephrem.</note> wishes to reject it on the grounds that it is not found in the Slavonic version. Yet, the Slavs did not translate all of Ephrem's works—whether ascetic, exegetical, or polemical—into their own language; nor are all the works now emerging in Greek and Syriac from the Vatican Library contained in every codex, whether Syriac, Greek, Arabic, or Slavonic.

Should Kohl object to us that a Syrian man, as Ephrem certainly was, ought not to have cited the words of Holy Scripture in any way other than according to the Syriac version <note>The Peshitta, the standard Syriac translation of the Bible.</note> (which adheres more closely to the Hebrew text), whereas in the sermon of which we treat, the Greek reading of the Seventy Interpreters <term>Septuagint</term> <note>The ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament.</note> is presented, which differs somewhat from the Hebrew source—as Vossius <note>Gerardus Vossius (1577–1649), a Dutch scholar and theologian.</note> himself notes in Volume 1, page 29 and following—we reply that this was not done by Ephrem himself, but by his Greek translator. This translator interpreted those passages according to the version common among the Greeks which Ephrem had used according to the Syriac. Nevertheless, there are those who think it does not stray from the appearance of truth that in Ephrem's age there existed a Greek edition of the Seventy Interpreters translated into the Syriac language, which he might have used rather than the standard Syriac. But of this question, elsewhere. Meanwhile, it is pleasing to place here the very words of Kohl, with which (even if he makes some exceptions) he praises and approves most of the Ephremic sermons of the Arabic version reviewed by us on page 297: *It would be desirable,* he says, *that the Arabic version also exist carefully translated into Latin. For, if you except that sermon inscribed "Reproof of Self and Confession" and a few others,*

---

A *it consists of purely genuine writings of Ephrem, never called into doubt by anyone: so that much is seen to be attributed to it, both as to its antiquity and as to its purity and genuineness.* If you ask Kohl for what reason that version B *consists of purely genuine writings of Ephrem never called into doubt by anyone,* he answers: *For the "Testament" and other writings of a similar mark are not found there.* And yet, in that same place are indeed found some writings which Kohl himself rejects; while many others are not found there which he does not hesitate to admit. You see, therefore, how weak is the argumentation C drawn from the number or order of the Ephremic sermons contained in one version or in one codex, applied to others not existing in the same place.

<margin>
III.
Concerning the Sermon of St. Ephrem on the Precious Pearl.
</margin>

Kohl considers the *Sermon on the Precious Pearl* not to be the genuine offspring of Ephrem, page 286. It was published in Latin by Vossius, volume 3, page 163; in Greek in the Oxford edition, page 384; and in Greek and Latin in our edition, in this Volume 2, page 259. D Moreover, it exists in the manuscript codices: Imperial Vienna 31, page 432, and Barocci 212. This sermon of St. Ephrem is cited by Ephrem, Patriarch of Antioch <note>Ephrem of Antioch, patriarch from 527–545 AD.</note> in Photius's *Library*, codex 229, pages 819–820; by Leontius of Byzantium, book 1, page 465, and book 3, page 485, in the *Library of the Fathers* (Cologne, 1618), volume 6; and by Anastasius Sinaita E in the *Guide* <note>The "Hodegos" or "Via Dux," a polemical work against heresies.</note> chapter 10, page 197. By their testimony, it is certainly more than sufficiently proved that the Greek Fathers of the fifth and sixth centuries not only did not doubt its genuineness, but even produced authority drawn from it against the Syrian Monophysite heretics <note>A group believing Christ had only one divine nature, rather than being both fully divine and fully human.</note>—namely the Severians and Jacobites—who were otherwise admirers of Ephrem's works, to demonstrate two natures in Christ. This they surely would not have dared to do if that sermon had an author other than Ephrem. Seven other sermons on the pearl exist in Syriac on the same subject, which F Father Peter Benedict the Maronite <note>Pietro Benedetti (1663–1742), a Maronite Jesuit who was a primary editor of the Vatican's Syriac works of Ephrem.</note> of the Society of Jesus recently—that is, a little before he departed from the living to the greatest grief of all the learned (he died on August 25...

### XXXIV
### PREFACE
<warning>Visible show-through from reverse page (ghosting) makes some letters in the middle gutter slightly faint.</warning>

...published in this year, 1742; a man to be remembered by me for many reasons, and never without praise: <margin>A</margin>) he published Latin translations of these works. The first of these begins on page 150: <note>original Syriac: ܕܚܙܝܗ ܠܡܪܓܢܝܬܐ</note> *It happened once, brothers, that a pearl came into my hands.* The second, on page 152: <margin>B</margin> <note>original Syriac: ܠܡܢ ܐܕܡܝܟܝ ܡܪܓܢܝܬܐ</note> *To what shall I compare you, O pearl?* The third, on page 154: <note>original Syriac: ܬܗܪܐ ܒܟܝ ܡܪܓܢܝܬܐ</note> *Rightly I marvel at you, O pearl, who, though stripped bare, seek the light and flee the shadows.* The fourth, on page 155: <note>original Syriac: ܨܕܗ ܓܝܣܐ ܠܗܝܡܢܘܬܐ</note> *Captured faith captured the thief.* <note>A reference to the "Good Thief" on the cross whose faith led to his salvation.</note> The fifth, on page 157: <note>original Syriac: ܚܙܝ ܡܘܗܒܬܐ ܕܗܘܬ ܒܐܝܕܗ</note> <margin>C</margin> *Now marvel at the gift (that is, the pearl): which, meeting the diver who desired it, emerged from the waters of its own accord.* The sixth, on page 159: <note>original Syriac: ܐܠܘ ܡܢ ܩܒܪ̈ܐ ܕܐܒܗ̈ܐ</note> *Would that the fragrance breathing from the tombs of the Fathers would frequently inspire their sons.* Finally, the seventh, on page 161: <note>original Syriac: ܦܪ̈ܫܐ ܒܥܝܬ ܘܠܓܘܡܢܣܝ ܐܬܝܬ</note> <margin>D</margin> *I was seeking a retreat, but I fell into a wrestling ground; <note>The original uses the Greek-derived terms "palaestra" and "gymnasium" to describe a place of intellectual and theological debate.</note> the sophists of the heterodox had gathered here.* In these discourses, Ephrem, remaining consistent with himself, discusses God and the incarnation of the Word against heretics, whom he calls "contentious searchers of divine matters." Indeed, because Ephrem spoke so clearly about the two natures of Christ even before the Eutychian heresy <note>The Eutychian or Monophysite heresy argued that Christ had only one divine nature, rather than being both fully God and fully man.</note> arose, <margin>E</margin> as appears from these words in Volume 2, page 263: <note>original Greek: "τῆς δύο φύσεων ὁ μαργαρίτης μετέχει ὁ πολύτιμος... κ.τ.λ."</note> "This precious stone (that is, the pearl) partakes of two natures; so that it may show Christ: because since He is the Word of God, He was born a man from Mary. He did not have a partial nature, for then He would be nothing other than a living creature. He has a perfect double nature, so that He might not lose the two. For neither was the one nature of God seen alone on earth; nor did the other, the human, ascend alone into heaven; but perfect from the perfect, man from man, God from God, and Christ from the Virgin." <margin>F</margin> From the fact, I say, that Ephrem speaks so clearly about the two natures of Christ, it does not follow—as Kohl <note>Johann Peter Kohl (1698–1778), a scholar who questioned the authenticity of certain works attributed to Ephrem.</note> contends—that this discourse must be denied to Ephrem. Kohl argues this partly because such a "curious" and "scholarly" investigation into Christ’s divine nature is supposedly contrary to Ephrem's own homilies (where he forbids prying into the nature of the Son of God), and partly because such a discourse seems rather to have been composed by another author after the time of Eutyches and the Council of Chalcedon <note>Held in 451 AD, this council defined the "two natures" of Christ.</note>, when controversies over the one or two natures of Christ began between Catholics and heretics.

However, the most holy Doctor of the Syrians, both in this work on the Pearl and in his other Syriac writings on Faith against the "searchers" <note>Ephrem often criticized those who tried to "scrutinize" or "probe" the mysteries of God through human logic alone.</note> which are now appearing in the second and third volumes, does indeed forbid prying too curiously into the divine nature. Yet he does not forbid the orthodox profession of those things concerning God, Christ, and the Sacraments of the Church which have been handed down to us and are clearly contained in both Testaments, or which are easily gathered from the clear words of Scripture according to the common sense of the Fathers. Indeed, he does this everywhere when he discusses the Trinity, the Incarnation, and the mysteries of Christ. Just as he clearly establishes the equality of the three divine persons against the Arians, so against the Apollinarians <note>Followers of Apollinaris, who denied that Christ had a human mind/soul.</note> and other heretics with wrong views of Christ, he felt compelled to provide a lucid confession of the two natures. This was also performed by the Fathers contemporary with or even older than Ephrem, whose testimonies are cited in the Councils of Ephesus and Chalcedon, and who are also praised by those who wrote specifically against the Monophysites. See Anastasius Sinaita in the *Guide* (*Hodegos*), chapter 10, and Leontius of Byzantium in Book 1 *Against the Nestorians and Eutychians*: from which I offer only these...

The author argues that Ephrem's clear teaching on the two natures of Christ (divine and human) was a legitimate defense of orthodoxy that predated later 5th-century controversies.</summary>

<margin>A</margin>...authorities, to omit the rest: *Athanasius*, namely from his book against Apollinarius: "The same was made man, not changed into the form of a man, nor—having despised the essence of man—did he appear like a shadow; but rather, since he was God by nature, he was born as man: so that he might be one, perfect in both according to all things, showing a natural and most true birth." <note>Athanasius was a 4th-century Bishop of Alexandria and a major defender of orthodoxy against Arianism.</note>

<margin>B</margin>*Gregory of Nyssa* against Eunomius: "If, therefore, according to the divine nature he is impassible <note>term: impassible (incapable of suffering or pain)</note>, but capable of suffering according to the human nature, who, whose heart is wise, would dare to say his two natures are one?"

<margin>C</margin>*Basil the Great from Book 3 against Eunomius*: "That word, 'he begot,' must therefore be understood regarding God the Son; but that other, '*he created*,' regarding him who took the form of a servant. In all these things, however, we do not speak of two—God separately, and man separately—for he was one: but in thought we consider the nature of each." You see, my dear Kohl, <note>Johann Peter Kohl (1698–1778), a scholar to whom this discourse is addressed.</note> how clearly these fathers discourse on the two natures of Christ: yet there is no one who calls their praised books into doubt. Why then must such a rigid judgment be passed on the writings of Ephrem alone, so that what he teaches alongside other Fathers of his age is immediately rejected as spurious?

<margin>E</margin>Meanwhile, I observe that this passage of Ephrem’s regarding the Pearl, which I produced above, was read differently among the ancients than it is in our own codices. For *Anastasius Sinaita* <note>A 7th-century abbot and theological writer.</note> in the *Hodegos* <note>term: Hodegos (The "Guide," a famous polemical work by Anastasius)</note> chapter 10 of the Ingolstadt edition of 1606, page 197, has it thus from the version of the famous Jacob Gretser: <note>original: "τοῦ ἁγίου Ἐφραΐμ εἰς τὸν μαργαρίτην· εἰ δὲ μία εἰσὶν, ἆρα ἂν ἡμίθεος ἐσαρκώθη θεὸς. καὶ ἡμιφυὴς ἀνελήφθη ἄνθρωπος."</note> *Of Saint Ephrem on the pearl. If indeed the divinity and humanity are one nature, then God was incarnated as a demigod: and a half-man was taken up into heaven.*

<margin>F</margin>The same *Anastasius* cites the same words of Ephrem, translated slightly differently into Greek, on page 163. <note>original: "καὶ ὁ θεοφόρος Ἐφραΐμ εἰς τὸν λόγον τοῦ μαργαρίτου λέγει· ὅτι τῶν δύο φύσεων μετέχει ὁ μαργαρίτης ὁ ἀτίμητος, τοῦτ᾽ ἔστιν ὁ Χριστός. καὶ πάλιν λέγει . διπλῆν ἔχει τὴν φύσιν , ἵνα μὴ τὰς δύο ἀπολέση· οὔτε γὰρ ἡμίθεος κα-"</note> "And the divine Ephrem says in his discourse on the pearl: *The priceless pearl, which is Christ, participates in two natures.* And again he says: *He has a double nature, lest he lose the two: for neither did a demigod de-

---

-scend upon the earth, nor did a half-man ascend into the heavens.*" Likewise, *Leontius of Byzantium* in Book 1 against the Nestorians and Eutychians, page 465, volume 6 of the Cologne edition of 1618: *St. Ephrem from the homily on the pearl.* "The pearl of great price is a participant in two natures, to show that Christ, since he was the Word of God, was born of Mary. He did not have a nature in part; for he was not some other animal. He has a perfect double nature, lest he lose the two. Neither was a demigod seen on earth, nor did a half-man ascend to the heavens. From the perfect, he is perfect; from man, man; from God, God; from the Virgin, Christ." <margin>D</margin> In both places it is denied that Christ appeared on earth as a *demigod*, and ascended into heaven as a *half-man*. For this reason, I believe a mistake has crept into our codices and into the Oxford edition in these words: <note>original: "οὔτε ἡ μία ἡ θεὸς φύσις ὤφθη μόνη ἐπὶ τῆς γῆς· οὔτε ἡ ἑτέρα, ἡ ἄνθρωπος ἀνέβη εἰς τὸν οὐρανὸν μόνη:"</note> "Neither was the one nature of God seen alone upon the earth: nor did the other, the man, ascend alone into the heavens." Unless we say there were different Greek translators of this sermon, one of whom rendered Ephrem’s Syriac words as they appear in Anastasius and Leontius, and the other as they are found in the Roman, English, and Viennese codices; one should certainly read *demigod* [hemitheos] for "the one divine nature alone"; and *half-human* [hemiphues anthropos] or *half-man* [hemianthropos] for "the other human nature alone."

Leontius brings forward another passage from this very sermon, which differs slightly from our edition. For he reads it thus in Book 3 against the Nestorians and Eutychians, page 485, with the famous Francisco Torres as translator. "From Saint Ephrem, from the discourse on the pearl against Marcion. Those things which you see and touch,
