### PREFATORY REMARKS
<page-num>XXXVI</page-num>

<margin>A</margin>
> “...let nature be a testimony to you—nature which you cannot explain—in that the Son is born without seed, and two things contrary according to their substance have agreed together, a <term>hypostasis</term> <note>original Latin: "hypostasi"; a theological term for a distinct person or individual existence, used here to describe the union of Christ’s divine and human natures.</note> being shown in each nature.” These words, both in the Oxford edition and our own edition on page 268, are presented as follows:

<margin>B</margin>
“You are compelled to believe what you see and what you touch. Let nature be a witness for you, which you do not know how to describe: that the Son of God is born without seed, and two things essentially opposite have harmonized, with the hypostases being shown in each nature.” <note>original Greek: "Ἀναγκάζῃ πιστεύειν... ἐπὶ ἑκάστης φύσεως"</note>
> “You are compelled to believe what you see and what you touch. Let nature be a testimony to you, which you do not know how to declare: that the Son of God is born without seed: and two opposites essentially agree, with the hypostases shown in each nature.” <note>original Latin: "Cogeris credere..."</note> Vossius, in volume 3, page 168, near the end, has it thus:
> “You are compelled to believe what you see and touch. Let nature be a testimony for you, which you do not know how to declare: because the Son of God is born without seed. In that instance, there are two opposites, whose hypostases essentially agree, and are shown in any nature whatsoever.”

It seems that Leontius <note>A 6th-century Byzantine theologian.</note> uses this testimony of Ephrem to indicate the single hypostasis of Christ subsisting in his two natures—namely, the human and the divine. For this reason, he uses the singular number, *the hypostasis being shown in each nature*, that is, one hypostasis shown in two natures. However, our manuscripts have it in the plural: *hypostases in each nature*. Nevertheless, a <term>Nestorian</term> <note>Followers of Nestorius, who were accused of teaching that Christ existed as two separate persons (divine and human) rather than one.</note> cannot take this as an opportunity to support his error using Ephrem's authority—specifically the claim that there are two Persons in the two natures of Christ. For these words of the Holy Doctor <note>Referring to Saint Ephrem.</note> refer not to Christ, but to water and lightning, water and fire, or the shell and the flash of light, of which he had spoken immediately before: *The lightning and the water agreed as one: and two opposites embraced each other.*
> “In what way, then, are you ignorant of what you hold, and why do you curiously investigate what you do not hold? From fire come lightning and fire: from which it both illuminates and inflames. Shells come from water and grow through water; how then does the flash of lightning not consume the small body of the snail?”

> “How do water and fire agree substantially and essentially, and not destroy one another? You cannot say, but you are compelled to believe what you see and touch.”

The rest is as above. Furthermore, the same Holy Doctor declares in the following words that from the union of Christ's natures, not two, but one hypostasis follows: “I anticipate your accusation; I know some of you say: 'Unbegotten God, visible flesh. Impassible God, a nature (that is, the human nature) subject to suffering.' How, in such opposites, did a harmony toward a single hypostasis occur?” <note>original Greek: "φθάνω ὑμῶν κατηγορίαν... πρὸς μίαν ὑπόστασιν ἐγένετο;"</note>

<margin>C</margin>
> “I anticipate your accusation, for I know some among you are saying: God is unbegotten, the flesh is visible; God is incapable of suffering, the nature (*that is, the human nature*) is capable of suffering. How then, in such opposites, was a harmony and agreement into one hypostasis achieved?”

He asserts, therefore, that the two natures of Christ came together into one, not two, hypostases, as is apparent from what follows. But enough on these matters.

<margin>D</margin>
<margin>Concerning the Testament of Ephrem</margin>
Kohlius includes the *Testament of Saint Ephrem* and his *Confession* in the list of "adulterated" <note>Spurious or forged.</note> orations. The opinion of Rivetus had been the same regarding both. They were published in *Latin* by Vossius: the *Confession* and the *Reproof of Himself* in Volume 3, page 99; the *Testament* indeed in the same Volume 3, page 289. They appeared in *Greek* in the Oxford edition, pages 82 and 365. They are in *Greek and Latin* in our edition, Volume 1, page 119, and Volume 2.

<margin>E</margin>
On page 230: The *Testament* exists in *Syriac* in the Nitrian manuscript 5 and the Urbanus manuscript of the *Propaganda Fide*, from which it was published by us in this Volume 2, page 395. It exists in *Greek* in the following manuscript codices: Vatican 438 (p. 186), Vatican 440 (p. 151), Vatican 1815 (p. 266), Imperial Vienna 61 (p. 259), Basilian 69 (p. 16), Basil 113 (p. 300), Laudin. 97, and Coislin.

<margin>F</margin>
238 (p. 168), Coislin 256 (p. 120), Medici Laurentian 76, and Passionatei (p. 536). In *Slavic* <note>Old Church Slavonic</note> sermon 30, page 336. Likewise, the *Confession and Reproof of Himself* exists in *Greek* in the manuscript codices: Vatican Alexandrian 16 (p. 218), Imperial Vienna 31 (p. 325), Laudin. 99, Ottoboni 1 (p. 184), Ottoboni 2 (p. 68), Medici Laurentian 30, Venice St. Mark 51 (p. 33), and Passionatei 425. Finally, it is in *Arabic* in Volume 1 of the Library...

<page-num>XXXVII</page-num>

### ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHREM THE SYRIAN.

<meta>The page contains a significant block of Syriac script. Reference letters B, C, D, and E appear in the gutter margin to mark text locations.</meta>

<margin>Oriental Library, page 151. Slavonic manuscript number 111, page 329.</margin>

I have shown that both works should be attributed to Ephrem in Volume 1 of the *Oriental Library*, pages 141 and 151, and in the *Prolegomena* <note>A formal introductory essay or preliminary remarks.</note> to Volume 1 of the works of St. Ephrem, Chapter 1, page 10. There, I have sufficiently answered the points raised by those who cite his *Confession* and *Testament* as if they were contradictory or mutually exclusive. Now it remains for us to dissolve the arguments of Kohl <note>Johann Peter Kohl (1698–1778), a scholar known for his studies on Eastern European and Oriental literature.</note> which are brought against Ephrem’s *Confession*.

**B**
He says on page 287: *“many things in this sermon offer themselves as obscure and shrouded in Cimmerian darkness <note>original: "cimmeriisque tenebris"; an idiom referring to the mythical Cimmerians who lived in perpetual mist, used to describe total obscurity.</note>: such as those on page 607.”*
> “And if indeed reason is content with natural things, it usually demands nothing: for it has defined the measure of nature, and established a limit for its own subsistence. *Of the same character*,” he continues, *“are the things that follow on page 611.”* 

**C**
> “But they possessed and wore leather garments, as if they were suited for spiritual exercise. For temperance requires heat: and that kind of clothing was appropriate, both at home and abroad.”

Thus says Kohl. I, however, maintain that a work by any author is not to be rejected as spurious simply because it contains some obscure passages. Rather, a hidden sense should be extracted from what precedes and follows the passage, or certainly from other manuscript codices. 

**D**
And yet, in the cited text of Ephrem, the meaning is obvious: for the Holy Doctor <note>A title for a significant Christian theologian and teacher.</note> distinguishes between the natural operations of man—which can be performed without sin so long as they do not cross a predetermined limit—and actions that depend on free will. Regarding these, he says in Volume 1 of our edition, page 133: 

**E**
> “If the free will succumbs to nature and is conquered by it, an account is demanded of it, as one that will never be satisfied and violates the boundaries set by God.”

I do not see what is obscure in this; just as I see no obscurity in the other sentence that Kohl produces from Ephrem. It is found in Volume 1, page 140, where St. Ephrem, speaking of the leather clothing of the Eremites <note>Desert-dwelling hermits or monks.</note>, notes that they used this very modest kind of clothing both in the wilderness and on a journey. They did not, however, wear it in the city, so as not to make themselves conspicuous to men like the Pharisees; they used leather garments because they were suited both for preserving vital heat and for professing religious poverty. This meaning is clearly expressed both by the Greek version published by us in the place cited, and by the Arabic version in these words:

<insert>For they were clothed in skins as if for spiritual exercise; for renewed purity needs warmth. And such a clothing was found to be good for those who dwell in the desert and for those who travel on the road. For purity is able to provide warmth because of the poverty of the fear of God. But they were not found walking in the villages in such clothing, lest they appear to the sons of men like the Pharisees, and lest they be prideful in what they wore. For the clothing of skins was used as if for spiritual exercise.</insert> <note>This translates the block of Syriac script provided in the source text.</note>

> “But since the Apostle had said that some went about in sheepskins and goatskins <note>A reference to Hebrews 11:37.</note>, it follows that they were accustomed to do so in the solitude: for in the cities they changed their clothing; for they did not want men to know what they were doing. Their clothing was therefore different from that of the Pharisees, who, being foolish, dressed in the manner of wise masters. And indeed, leather garments served as their covering; for they did not desire to be conspicuous to the people. But they had leather garments as if suited for spiritual exercise: for heat and temperance require a specific manner. Therefore, that kind of clothing was appropriate both for those staying in the desert and for those traveling: for through heat and temperance, a limit is imposed on religious poverty.”

From these things it becomes manifest that Vossius <note>Gerardus Vossius, a 17th-century Dutch scholar.</note> was right to criticize, in Volume 3, page 118, the translation by Zini <note>Pietro Francesco Zini, a 16th-century translator of patristic texts.</note>, who, twisting that sentence to refer to the Pharisees, thought it should be read affirmatively:
> “Indeed, leather garments were for them <gloss>that is, the Pharisees</gloss> for comparison; for they wanted to be conspicuous to the people.”

For we nowhere read that the Pharisees used sheepskins and goatskins. The codices, moreover...

# XXXVIII
## PREFACE

<margin>A</margin> they all contain the negative particle: *for they did not wish to be visible to the people*, or, *when they wished for men to know what they were doing*: <note>original: "οὐδὲ γὰρ ἤθελον τῷ λαῷ εἶναι καταφανεῖς, seu, ὅτε γὰρ ἤθελον τοὺς ἀνθρώπους εἰδέναι ὅπερ ἔπραττον"</note> *for they did not desire to be conspicuous to the people, or, they did not desire for men to see that which they themselves were doing.* This is also confirmed by what immediately follows here; and that this is the true and genuine reading in this place is also proven <margin>B</margin> from the preceding passages, which apply to hermits, not to Pharisees. For this reason, the conjecture of the same Vossius <note>Gerardus Johannes Vossius (1577–1649), a Dutch classical scholar and theologian.</note> is by no means to be admitted, which he adds at the end of his very learned note for the sake of Zinus, as I believe: unless perhaps, he says, we might wish to read it here by distinguishing and supplementing it otherwise: "Garments of skin <margin>C</margin> <note>original: "Pelliceae vestes." In the early church, garments made of animal skins were symbols of extreme asceticism and repentance, mimicking the "garments of skin" given to Adam and Eve.</note> were indeed for them (namely, the Pharisees) for the sake of worship and clothing: for they desired to be conspicuous to the people: but these men (namely, the Hermits) had garments of skin because they did not wish to be conspicuous to the people, and they were clothed in them as things better suited for spiritual exercise." An ingenious explanation: but one that would never have occurred to Ephraem, nor could it <margin>D</margin> suit the customs of the Pharisees, who did not have the habit of covering their delicate little bodies with goat skins, as I said above.

Kohl <note>Johann Peter Kohl (1698–1778), a scholar who challenged the authenticity of the "Confession of Ephraem."</note> insists, attacking the authenticity of the Ephraemian Confession. He says on page 288: *The author's Homily on the Word, the second person of the Trinity, is cited*; likewise the treatise *on Providence*, in these words: *I have written concerning providence <margin>E</margin>: but whether my writings have pleased God, I do not know. Yet these writings neither exist today, nor are they found cited by any of the ancients under Ephraem's name.* Not found cited? Do they not exist today? Let Kohl read, I pray, the Syriac writings of Ephraem, which have recently come forth in volumes 2 and 3 from the very ancient Nitrian manuscripts <note>Manuscripts from the Monastery of St. Mary Deipara in the Nitrian Desert of Egypt, a major source for early Syriac literature.</note>: there he will find <margin>F</margin> Ephraem discussing the Word and Providence very frequently. What if he had found them cited by any of the ancients under Ephraem's name? Would he therefore have considered them genuine? Kohl affirms it; but I most confidently deny it: for this very sermon which we have at hand, titled *Confession and Self-Reproof*, is cited by Photius <note>Photius the Great (c. 810–893), Patriarch of Constantinople and a famous bibliophile and scholar.</note>, a man not unlearned among the ancients, and it is cited in the first place among Ephraem's sermons with these words: *Forty-nine sermons of the holy Ephraem were read; the first of which makes him lament the things he had done in his life, and serves as a prologue for the subsequent exhortations.* <note>original: "ἀνεγνώσθησαν ἐν ἁγίοις Ἐφραΐμ λόγοι ἐννέα καὶ τεσσαράκοντα· ὧν ὁ πρῶτος, ἑαυτόν τε ποιεῖ ἀπολοφυρόμενον οἷς ἐβίω, καὶ τόπον ἐπέχει προλόγου τῶν ἐφεξῆς παραινέσεων"</note> What could have been said more clearly about this sermon by Photius? And yet Kohl does not regard it as genuine. In vain, therefore, is the authority of Ancient writers and the venerable antiquity of manuscript codices opposed to Kohl by us, when he intends to deny or affirm everything according to his own whim. But I let these things pass. I come closer to the matter itself. Kohl says: the author of this sermon indicates that a Treatise on Providence was written by him. The words are of this sort in Vossius, Vol. 3, p. 102, and in our edition, vol. 1, p. 123: *I have written of providence: but whether that pleased God, I do not know.* The Arabic translator has the same:

<note>original Syriac: ܟܬܒܬ ܥܠ ܬܕܒܝܪܗ ܕܐܠܗܐ ܃ ܟܕ ܐܠܗܐ ܡܕܒܪ ܃ ܐܢܕܝܢ ܡܫܠܡܐ ܗܝ ܠܐܠܗܐ ܐܘ ܠܐ ܃ ܠܐ ܝܕܥ ܐܢܐ ܃</note>

" *I have written concerning the divine governance providing for the universe. But, whether God has accepted this, I do not know.* " By which words it is undoubtedly signified that Ephraem wrote about divine Providence. But whether it is this very thing—that Ephraem discusses divine providence extensively and learnedly in the present sermon—or another written work published before, is not clear to me: for both, if we look at Ephraem's style, his words can indicate. But whether we affirm the first or the second about this writing of Ephraem; it is nevertheless clear that Kohl is hallucinating in thinking it is indicated in the same sermon that another was composed by the holy Doctor *on the Word, the second person of the Trinity*. Ephraem's words are plain in Vossius, vol. 3, page 104, and in our edition, page 127.

> " *And since the Father foresaw that nothing he had made could grasp Him, He brought forth the Son without beginning from His own substance*,"

<page-num>XXXIX</page-num>

-> ### ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHRAEM THE SYRIAN. <-

<margin>A</margin>
"...and the Holy Spirit; not out of necessity, or some cause, <note>original Greek: ēper eipomen, schēmatizontes ton logon</note> **as we said, when we were sketching out the discourse**, just as we said when we were outlining the Word (or, when we were treating of the Word:) so that the fullness of his divinity (or, as Vossius <note>Gerardus Vossius, a 16th-century scholar and editor of Ephraem’s works</note> has it, of his goodness) might be demonstrated." The Arabic Translator: <note>The following is a translation of the Syriac text provided in the manuscript, which reflects Ephraem's original language</note> **For God is beautiful / because as he provided for us / so that we might receive the Word / not from a consequence nor from a cause for God / and in a simple giving / so that it might be known intellectually.**
<margin>B</margin>
"And since he foresaw that nothing among those things which are created could contain or bear him,
<margin>C</margin>
he produced from his own substance, by a production that has no beginning, the Son and the Holy Spirit; not out of necessity, nor from a cause, just as we said before when outlining the discourse, so that the fullness of his goodness might be received." Here, as you see, the discourse is not about the Word as the Son of the Eternal Father; rather, Ephraem means that this kind of matter had been treated by him a little earlier, or at least sketched out. Read the very discourse we are discussing, starting from those words 
<margin>D</margin>
on page 123: *I went away from there, therefore, anxious and bathed in many tears, and it will appear clearly to you,* that Ephraem had previously discussed not a few things concerning God’s providence over all things, and concerning the persons of the Most Holy Trinity.

I had almost finished; when behold, I see 
<margin>E</margin>
Kohl <note>Johann Peter Kohl (1698–1778), a scholar who questioned the authenticity of these texts</note> hurling a final spear against this "Confession" of Ephraem. "The clearest argument of all for its being <term>spurious|nothias</term>," he says, "is that the opinion regarding the <term>procession|the theological concept of how the Holy Spirit 'comes forth' from the Father</term> of the Holy Spirit from the Father alone is defended here in open words. Since this view is of a much more recent age than could possibly have Ephraem as its author or follower, there is 
<margin>F</margin>
scarcely any doubt that this writing was compiled by some more recent Greek." So far, so good: this is the argument of a very fine critic. But by what reasoning does Kohl demonstrate this? Let us listen. "The procession of the Holy Spirit from the Father *alone*," he adds, "is proposed in these words: *And since he foresaw that nothing of the good things he made could contain him, he produced the Son without beginning from his substance, and the Holy Spirit not out of necessity.* He says the same a bit more clearly elsewhere: *Furthermore, the Holy Spirit was not begotten, but proceeded from the substance of the Father, not imperfect, nor confused.* And likewise later: *And the Father, wishing for another companion, did not beget him, but produced him: the Holy Spirit from his own substance.* Behold," says Kohl, "the expressive assertion of the procession of the Holy Spirit from the Father alone. To which is added that passage on page 604: *The Father, therefore, neither required time in the generation of the Word, nor did he produce the Holy Spirit after any interval of time following the Word.*" 

It clearly misbecomes a learned and upright man, such as Kohl is, to use a line of reasoning so shrinking from the rules of right logic. It is as if, by the same argument, the Holy Gospel of John could be said to have been compiled by some more recent Greek, because the same opinion and the same words regarding the procession of the Holy Spirit are found in it: *But when the Counselor comes, whom I shall send to you from the Father, the Spirit of truth, who proceeds from the Father* (John 15:26). Yet who would say such a thing unless they were of a disturbed mind? Kohl would bring this same slander against the writings of the holy Fathers Athanasius, Gregory, Basil, Chrysostom, and Epiphanius, who, according to the Gospel truth, pronounce this absolutely concerning the Holy Spirit: namely, that he proceeds from the Father. 

But if, in Kohl’s mind, saying "he proceeds from the Father" is the same as saying "he proceeds from the Father *alone*," he should see for himself whether he is not "playing the Greek"—that is, passing from the Lutheran sect to the schismatic sect of the "Greeklets." In that sect, however, those futile little arguments will never prove that Ephraem’s discourses are the offspring of some more recent author just because the Holy Spirit is said in them to proceed from the Father. Indeed, Kohl himself sees, and does not hide, that it is one thing to say "he proceeds from the Father" and another to say "he proceeds from the Father *alone*." The former is the oracle of the Gospel and the common utterance of all the ancient Fathers; the latter began to be used by Photius <note>A 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople associated with the break between the Eastern and Western churches</note> and more recent sectarians only after the eighth century. Nevertheless, Kohl does not admit defeat, but continues on his poorly laid foundations...

The author argues that Ephraem’s description of the Holy Spirit "proceeding from the Father" is consistent with early Church Fathers and the Gospel of John, rather than being evidence of a later medieval Greek interpolation.</summary>

->XL<-
# PROLEGOMENA

<margin>A</margin> ...to build a worse mass upon the foundations: *Someone might object here, citing that well-worn philosophical dictum: "the positioning of one does not exclude the other." But who does not see that in all these expressions the Son is excluded here from the production of the Holy Spirit? <note>This refers to the "Filioque" controversy, a major theological dispute over whether the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father alone or from both the Father and the Son.</note> Indeed, who can see this, unless he has no eyes? Such a person, dreaming, seems to see something that exists nowhere.*

<margin>V. Concerning the Testament of Saint Ephrem.</margin>
<margin>B</margin> So much for Ephrem’s *Confession*. I come now to his *Testament*. In four chapters, the scholar Kohlius <note>Johann Peter Kohl (1698–1778), a German scholar who critiqued the authenticity of many ancient texts.</note> (page 353) attempts to make the truth of this writing suspect, or rather to demonstrate its falsehood. First, he says, because the genre of speech, the style itself, and the entire structure of the discourse <margin>C</margin> throughout the whole writing *is by no means harmonious or conforming to the Ephremian style.* He certainly asserts this himself, but others deny it: Natalis Alexander, Tillemont, and Dupin <note>These are prominent 17th-century ecclesiastical historians and theologians.</note>. It is denied by anyone who compares this writing—at least as edited by us from the Syriac text—with the rest of Ephrem's genuine works. Finally, it is denied by anyone who reads the *Encomium* <note>A speech or piece of writing that praises someone highly.</note> by Saint Gregory of Nyssa, which notes the *Testament* of Ephrem with these words in the Prolegomena (Vol. I, p. XVII): <margin>D</margin>

> "This holy man, being now about to depart for heaven, commanded those who were then present not to bury his body with any precious garment. And if anyone, out of love for the father, had thought of or prepared such a thing, he should not carry it out. Instead, changing his mind for the better, <margin>E</margin> he should give to the poor the very thing he had intended for the honoring of the burial. And a few things being interjected: when this miracle had been demonstrated, and when the end of his life was imminent, he exhorted those who were present with many pious warnings to the zeal and love of virtue, as his last words declare; and so he happily migrated <margin>F</margin> to the quiet harbor of the eternal kingdom."

Nor does Sozomen <note>A 5th-century historian of the Christian Church.</note> list the names of Ephrem’s disciples from any other source, I believe, than from the *Testament* (Book 3 of his *Ecclesiastical History*, Chapter 16), when he says:

> "It is said, moreover, that he had many disciples who studiously emulated his teaching. Among these, the most noble were Abba, Zenobius, Abraham, Maras, and the Simeons, in whom the Syrians, and whoever among the Syrians were students of more exquisite learning, greatly glory. In the same way, they celebrate Paulonas and Aranad for their eloquence; though they say these men wandered away from the true doctrine of the faith."

The indications of obscurity and incoherence that Kohlius gathers should be attributed to the Greek translator; for in the Syriac text, everything is clear and transparent.

Kohlius’s second argument: *This writing contains many things,* he says, *which by no means suit Ephrem’s prudence, gravity, orthodoxy,* <term>humility <note>original: ταπεινοφροσύνην</note></term>, *and finally his piety joined with firm trust in God.* If you ask what these things finally are, he heaps up many absurdities according to his own measure. First, *that in order to clearly declare his tireless zeal in defending sacred things, he compares himself to a shepherd's dog.* Oh, what a delicate temperament the man has! What would this rigid censor say about Christ Himself, who compared Himself to a hen (Matthew 23:37)?

> "How often I wanted to gather your children together, just as a hen gathers her chicks under her wings, and you were not willing!"

What would he say of Moses, who describes God as being like an eagle (Deuteronomy 32:11)?

> "Like an eagle prompting its young to fly, and hovering over them, he spread his wings and took him up."

Finally, what would he say of David, who calls himself a dog and a flea (1 Samuel 24:15)?

> "Whom are you pursuing? You are pursuing a dead dog and a single flea."

He continues to declare the Ephremian *Testament* unworthy of Ephrem, saying: *What is more imprudent than to devote those people to so many and such great curses, who would place his dead body either under the altar or in another place in the temple?* But I, on the contrary, reply: what is more worthy of Ephrem’s humility than to judge himself unworthy of those honors which were granted to holy men after their death by the piety of the faithful? The chief of these honors was to be buried in the temple or under the altar. For this was the custom of that age—the practice that the bodies of martyrs or distinguished confessors were snatched up by Christians and so [placed] in the temple [for] hono—

The author argues that Ephrem's self-deprecating metaphors and his refusal to be buried under an altar are signs of his profound humility rather than evidence of forgery.</summary>

### ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHREM THE SYRIAN.

...were buried honorably, just as Ephrem, out of extreme humility of spirit, forbade for himself: moreover, from their rags, from ashes, or from their bones, whatever could be obtained was eagerly taken for the sake of religion, and was preserved with burning faith to ward off, by the protection of the saints, evils of both body and soul. Indeed, no one would deny this unless they were completely ignorant of ecclesiastical history, or carried away by the fury of the Hagiomachorum <note>original: "Hagiomachorum"; refers to "Saint-fighters" or those who opposed the veneration of relics</note>, such that they dared to cast doubt upon or completely reject the writings and deeds of our holy Fathers of the first, second, third, fourth, and subsequent centuries.

And therefore, that argument which Kohl <note>Johann Peter Kohl (1698–1778), a scholar who questioned the authenticity of Ephrem's works</note> brings forward from Cave <note>William Cave (1637–1713), an English church historian</note> on page 358 is turned back against himself: “Empty fables and most inept trifles, abhorrent to the Apostolic age or the character and gravity of any ancient writer, cry out that the book containing them—whatever name it may bear—is a forgery, or at least provide a just reason for doubting it.” It is neither abhorrent to the age of Ephrem, nor to his character and gravity, that he should have requested of the people of Edessa <note>The city in modern-day Turkey where Ephrem lived and died</note>, under the threat of divine judgment, that his corpse should not be buried in a temple or under an altar like the saints, nor that any of his rags should be taken in place of relics.

“Kohl requires judgment in the author of the Testament <note>The *Testament of Saint Ephrem* is a famous deathbed poem/document attributed to him</note>, because he binds himself with such great oaths and calls upon the faith of gods and men to witness that he has never turned even a hair’s breadth from the true faith, even though none of his listeners (whom he primarily addresses in this writing) had ever doubted him.” But I, in turn, require judgment in Kohl, because he thinks these protestations of Ephrem were directed solely toward the goal of persuading his listeners that he had never receded from the right faith. For everyone was already persuaded of this; no one doubted it.

Indeed, Ephrem’s mind was different from what Kohl understands. He wanted the people of Edessa to guard themselves against every heresy and to keep the orthodox faith undefiled by his example; and thus he implored his disciples: “Remain in my doctrine, my disciples,

<margin>A</margin> “and do not fall away from my faith... engrave my words upon your hearts, and be mindful of me. For it will come to pass that perverse men will come to you after me, clothed indeed in sheep’s clothing, but who are inwardly ravenous wolves.” And many other things of this kind, by which he commands them to avoid the doctrine and company of heretics.

<margin>B</margin> “That speech is unworthy of Ephrem’s piety,” says [Kohl], “in which, dreading imminent death and praying, he says: *Woe is me, for the snares of death have overtaken me! Alas, for the days of life have failed!* Is this speech worthy of a Christian man, let alone so holy a man as Ephrem was? Is this how those who piously believe in Christ the Savior are accustomed to behave, or how the dying prepare themselves for the journey to heaven?”

<margin>C</margin> How great is Kohl's confidence in God! He clearly believes most firmly that Christ is the redeemer of men; but I do not know whether he believes with the same firmness that He is the severe judge of good and evil deeds. Ephrem, who even when he was in full strength always kept the final Day of Judgment before his eyes and could never reflect upon it without tears, <margin>D</margin> is not to be wondered at for trembling so greatly now that he is about to meet the approaching Judge; yet it is a wonder that Kohl should attribute such a most religious horror in Ephrem to the words of one clearly despairing.

Kohl passes from trifles to heretical dogmas. “First of all,” he says, <margin>E</margin> “this is found to be repugnant to the sounder doctrine with which Ephrem was imbued: that he entreats for prayers to be made and offerings to be established for himself once he has departed. While he was alive, he often asked his friends and listeners to join their prayers to his, to make God more favorable and reconcile Him to him. But nowhere in his writings did Ephrem say that the prayers of the living for the dead were lawful, let alone did he seek them for himself. <margin>F</margin> He clearly indicates in various places that he thought these were done in vain, as in the sermon in *Volume I of Vossius’s version, page 69: ‘May the mercies of God,’ he says, ‘prevent <note>original: "praeveniant"; used here in the older sense of "come before" or "anticipate"</note> me through your prayer, before I am dragged away along with those who work iniquity.’* By which...

> „ as it is immediately contrary to Holy Scripture,
> „ began to be cherished and publicly defended
> „ in the Church much later. „

Perhaps I have been longer than I should have been in reciting these follies of Kohli. But because both he and Rivet seem to reject Ephrem's *Testament* for no other reason than that the holy Doctor <note>St. Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373), a prolific hymnodist and theologian</note> asks there that prayers be said for him after his death, and shows how much the prayers of the living avail for the dead; I therefore deemed it necessary to omit nothing that they produce in defense of their error. Truly, anyone can see how foolishly they argue that prayers of the living for the dead were forbidden by Ephrem, simply because while he was alive, he had asked his friends to pray for him. Could not Ephrem, my dear Kohli, seek both: that they pray for him while living and for him when dead? 

But, says Kohli, Ephrem could indeed have written both, but he did not; and he did not write it because in Ephrem's era that plague had not yet grown strong, where the living would offer prayers and offerings for the dead. But where does Kohli get the idea that Ephrem did not write it, except because he himself denies this writing of Ephrem? And he denies it even though all the Greeks and Syrians affirm it. Whom then should we believe more? Kohli who denies, or the Syrians and Greeks who affirm? The case should be decided, I think, from no other source than from Ephrem himself and from the Fathers of that age. 

Let Ephrem therefore come forward and undertake the defense of his own writings. He, according to the testimony of Ebediesu of Soba <note>Also known as Abdisho bar Berika, a 13th-14th century East Syrian scholar and bibliographer</note> in the *Catalogue of Syrian Books*, chapter 52 (*Oriental Library*, Vol. 3, page 62), composed many Odes inscribed "Concerning the Deceased": <term>ܡܪ̈ܬܝܢܐ ܕܥܢ̈ܝܕܐ</term> <note>original Syriac: "martyane d'anyide" — songs or chants for the departed</note> and all the songs for the Deceased.

<margin>A</margin> These exist in Syriac in the very ancient Nitrian codex <note>Manuscripts from the Monastery of the Syrians in the Nitrian Desert, Egypt</note> XVI, written in the year of Christ 823, and in another Nitrian [codex] XVII, regarding which see Vol. 1 of the *Oriental Library*, pages 132 and 567. They were published in Syriac and Latin by the illustrious Father Peter Benedict in Vol. 3 of the *Syro-Latin Works of Ephrem*, starting at page 225, with this prefixed title: <term>ܡܪܝ ܐܦܪܝܡ ܣܘܪܝܝܐ</term> <note>original Syriac: "Mari Apryem Suryaya" — Saint Ephrem the Syrian</note>

<margin>B</margin> <term>ܬܘܒ ܕܥܠ ܥܢ̈ܝܕܐ ܡܐܡܪ̈ܐ ܐܘܟܝܬ ܩܢܘ̈ܢܐ</term> <note>original Syriac: "Tub d'al anyide memre okit qanone" — Again, Discourses or Canons on the Deceased</note>. *The Death Songs, or Funeral Canons, of our Holy Father Ephrem the Syrian*. In these Odes, songs, or canons of the Deceased, countless sentiments of Ephrem occur in which, just as in the *Testament*, the Holy Doctor frequently insists on the orthodox truth, defending it against

<margin>C</margin> Aetius <note>A 4th-century radical Arian who taught that prayers and offerings for the dead were useless</note> (though the name of the heretic is suppressed) who taught that one should not pray for the dead (as St. Epiphanius in *Heresy 75* and St. Augustine in *Heresy 53* record): namely, that the dead are helped by the prayers and offerings of the living. We shall select a few from many. In Canon V, page 231, he introduces a deceased Bishop speaking thus:

<header>ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHRAEM THE SYRIAN.</header>

<meta>catchword: Li-; bottom-left: Tom. II.</meta>

# ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHRAEM THE SYRIAN.

<note>original Syriac: "ܐܶܢܳܐ ܕܶܝܢ ܟܰܕ ܪܳܚܶܩ ܐܢܳܐ ܡܶܢܟܽܘܢ..."</note>
But I, while I am far from you and departing, if you build temples to the fear of God, this is the reward of your love for me: write my name down.

> “And now again I pray you, brothers and friends, and all you who frequent the Church, and weeping I pray, and by God I adjure you, who commanded me to depart from you, that when you gather to offer prayers, you have a memory of me, beseeching the Lord that in the day of His coming He may have mercy on me, and through His clemency remit whatever sins I have committed before Him.” 
> <note>This is a citation from Ephraem’s "Testament," specifically Canon XVI, page 261, in the section regarding the Funeral of a Monk.</note>

<note>original Syriac: "ܕܽܘܟܪܳܢܳܐ ܢܶܗܘܶܐ ܠܰܢ..."</note>
Let there be a memory for us on the festivals of those who have departed from the world, and especially for this our brother whom God has brought near to Himself. We seek from the Judge that He might help us through his prayers—God, who does not desire the death of a sinner but rather his life—that He might have mercy on him in His goodness and be gracious to him in His mercies. Enter not, Lord, into judgment with your servant, for no man is innocent of debts. Do not remember his sins and follies. Forgive his debts in your mercy and set him at your right hand.

> “Specifically and distinctly, brothers, let us pray for our brother, who has departed from us. Let us supplicate the Judge, who hears the prayers of those who love Him. O God, who does not desire the death but the life of a sinner, have mercy on your servant in your goodness, and be favorable to your worshiper in your clemency. Do not enter, Lord, into right and just judgment with your servant; because no one is clean from debts <note>original: "debitis," often used in a legal sense for "sins" in Syriac and Latin theology</note> and faults. Remember not his sins and transgressions. Forgive his debts for the sake of your clemency, and place him at your right hand.” 

<margin>F</margin> Behold, Ephraem is everywhere consistent with Ephraem, praying himself for the deceased and likewise entreating his listeners that after his departure they should continually make memory of him in prayers and offerings. But if we also wish to collect the opinions of the holy Fathers of Ephraem’s own age regarding this article of Catholic doctrine, we will surely find so many and such clear testimonies <margin>A</margin> that neither Kohlius <note>Johann Peter Kohl (1698–1778), a scholar who wrote on the history of the Eastern Church</note>, nor other heretics who attack the custom of offering prayers <note>original: "suffragia"</note> for the dead, could reject them—unless, casting off all shame, they wished to deny the sun itself at midday. 

This one point may suffice for all, which Cardinal Bona <note>Giovanni Bona (1609–1674), a Cistercian cardinal and liturgist</note> brings forward in his *Liturgical Matters*, book 2, chapter 14, where he treats the commemoration of the Dead in the Liturgy: <margin>B</margin> “To enroll the names,” he says, “of the faithful departed in tablets, and to recite them within the solemnities of the Mass, as I indicated above, is a most ancient custom and an <term>Apostolic institution</term> <note>a practice believed to have been established by the Apostles themselves</note>. All Liturgies have a commemoration of the dead; and concerning this beneficial institution, almost all the ancient Fathers bear witness, whom I have reviewed <margin>C</margin> in the *Treatise on Divine Psalmody*, chapter 13. Chrysostom, in his 69th homily to the People of Antioch, says: *It was not without reason that these things were ordained by the Apostles, that in the awesome mysteries <note>the Eucharist</note> a commemoration of the departed should be made.*” 

Saint Monica, when about to die, commanded her son Augustine—as he himself relates in Book 9 of the *Confessions*—that a memory of her be <margin>D</margin> made at the altar of God. And Augustine himself, in the book *On the Care to be Taken for the Dead*, chapter 1, testifies that it is the custom of the whole Church that in the prayers of the priest, which are poured out to the Lord God at His altar, the commendation of the dead has a place. Likewise, in Sermon 32 on the words of the Apostle, he says: <margin>E</margin> “*The whole Church observes this tradition handed down from the Fathers: that for those who have died in the communion of the Body and Blood of Christ, when they are commemorated at the sacrifice itself in their proper place, prayer is offered, and it is mentioned that the sacrifice is offered for them also.*” 

Epiphanius also, in his work *Against Heresies* (75), testifies that it was an ancient custom to recite the names of the deceased and to pray for them. Not only does Cardinal Bona affirm these things, but he also demonstrates them by the authority of the Liturgies and the Fathers. Vossius <note>Gerardus Vossius (1577–1649), a Dutch humanist and theologian</note> also shows this in his notes on this passage of Ephraem’s *Testament*, volume 3, page 300. Finally, Renaudot <note>Eusèbe Renaudot (1646–1720), a French theologian and expert on Eastern liturgies</note> shows it in his notes on the *Oriental Liturgies*, volume 2, page 104. It remains, therefore, that Kohlius, Rivet <note>André Rivet (1572–1651), a French Huguenot theologian</note>, and other innovators of the same sort must either [reject] the writings of all the Fathers and all the Churches...

<summary>This page argues for the antiquity and legitimacy of praying for the deceased, using St. Ephraem the Syrian's own writings as primary evidence. It supports this "Catholic doctrine" by citing major Church Fathers like Chrysostom and Augustine, as well as modern liturgical scholars like Cardinal Bona, to refute Protestant critics.</summary>

...let them cast aside the Liturgies, or let them yield and surrender <note>original: "manus dent," literally "give their hands"</note> to the truth fortified by such great forces; otherwise, they are to be held and dismissed as being in that same place as the insane.

<margin>
VI.
Concerning the
Sermon of
St. Ephrem
on the monastic
life, which
begins, "pain
compels me
to speak."
</margin>

From the *Testament*, Kohl <note>Johann Peter Kohl (1698–1778), a scholar of Oriental languages</note> proceeds to the Ephremic sermon *on the religious and monastic life*; the beginning of which is, *Labor and pain compel me to speak*. Kohl himself, on page 289, calls this work *spurious* <note>original: "adulterinum"</note>, and it *seemed suspicious* to Tillemont <note>Louis-Sébastien Le Nain de Tillemont (1637–1698), a prominent ecclesiastical historian</note> himself. However, in Volume 8 of the Paris edition of 1713, page 756, Tillemont does not actually raise a doubt about its authenticity, but only asserts that a certain opinion is held within it *which in appearance may not be so favorable to grace: but it would be easy to explain it: that*, as it seems, *it does not favor divine grace much: yet with a simple effort it could be explained.* By these words, Tillemont is so far from considering this sermon suspicious that he actually moves no doubt regarding it; rather, he judges it to be just as genuine as the preceding sermon, which is titled *In Imitation of the Proverbs*. This sermon appeared in Latin by an anonymous translator in the Vatican manuscript 650, page 139; Vatican manuscript 5051, page 53; and Vatican manuscript 6443, page 274. It was further provided with a new version by Ambrose of Camaldoli <note>A 15th-century theologian and translator of Greek fathers</note> in Sermon 4, and by Vossius <note>Gerardus Vossius, an early modern scholar</note> in Volume 3, page 34. It appears in Greek in the Oxford edition on page 28, and in both Greek and Latin in our edition, Volume 1, page 40. It exists in Greek handwritten in the following manuscripts: Vatican 439 (p. 242), Vatican 1524 (p. 55), Vatican 1815 (p. 1), Alexander 8 (p. 74), Alexander 16 (p. 180), Clement 62 (p. 156), Basilian 69 (p. 26), Basilian 113 (p. 1), Laudin 97 and 99, Barberini 31 (p. 218), Coislin 59 (p. 211), Coislin 381 (p. 4), Laurentian Medici 27, Venice St. Mark’s Sermon 38, and Passion Sermon 93. Fragments of the same sermon exist in Greek in the Venice St. Mark’s manuscript 52, sermons 18, 19, 20, 21; and in the Greek Moscow manuscript, sermon 1. Finally, it is found in Arabic as sermon 5 in Volume 1 of the *Oriental Library*, page 150. I would greatly wish for Kohl to express exactly what fault he discovered in this sermon; for a simple response could be <insert>A</insert> returned to all things that might be objected, based on the undisputed reliability of manuscripts older than the ninth century. But since Tillemont, in his observations on this very sermon, refers the earthquake mentioned by St. Ephrem, the eclipse of the sun, and the incursion of the Persians into the lands of the Roman Empire to the times of Constantius or Valens <note>Roman Emperors of the 4th century</note>, let us first see what Ephrem’s words are; then let us see in what way Tillemont’s opinion can be reconciled with the annals of the Syrians and Greeks.

<insert>B</insert> In the Greek copies which we praised above, in Vossius Volume 3, page 36, and in our edition Volume 1, page 42, the holy Doctor <note>A title for an influential Christian teacher</note> affirms that these three things happened at the time he was writing. <insert>C</insert> *The earth*, he says, *trembling from the face of the Lord for our terror, is moved beneath our feet: and we are not even thus shaken by fear. Cities have been swallowed up by earthquakes, and places have been made desolate by the face of the wrath of the Lord: and neither thus are we seized by terror. The sun has now once and again <note>meaning twice</note> been darkened over us in the midday hours: and neither thus do we fear in our hearts. The wars of the Persians and of various barbarian nations have been stirred up, and have devastated our province, so that* <insert>D</insert> *we, fearing God, might perform penance, since we have need of penance not merely for a few days or months, but for many years and many ages: and not even thus do we wish to be converted.* Baronius <note>Cesare Baronio (1538–1607), author of the Annales Ecclesiastici</note> recites these words of Ephrem for the year of Christ 340, the 4th year of Constantius, in which many cities of the East fell in a horrible earthquake, as told by <insert>E</insert> St. Jerome in his *Chronicle*, Orosius in Book 7, Chapter 29, Socrates in Book 2, Chapter 7, and Sozomen in Book 3, Chapter 5. But Ephrem could not have spoken about that earthquake, both because he was still a youth and thus had not yet undertaken the duty of teaching and writing; for by the testimony of that same Baronius, when <insert>F</insert> St. James of Nisibis <note>The first bishop of Nisibis and Ephrem's mentor</note> departed to the heavens in the year 338, Ephrem, his disciple, had not yet reached manhood; and also because in the same year the earthquake occurred, St. Ephrem teaches that an incursion of the Persians into Roman jurisdiction also happened; however, in the year 340, the Persians were at peace with the Romans, having returned to their own lands after the siege of Nisibis was lifted in the year 338. Moved by these reasons...

The author argues against a date of 340 AD, noting that Ephrem was too young at that time and the political situation did not match the sermon's descriptions.</summary>

->XLV<-

# ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHREM THE SYRIAN.

Tillemont <note>Louis-Sébastien Le Nain de Tillemont (1637–1698), a renowned French ecclesiastical historian.</note> postpones the earthquake and the Persian incursion into Mesopotamia, which Ephrem mentions here, to another year of Constantius, or at least to the times of Valens, in these words from Volume 8, concerning St. Ephrem, chapter 22:

> "These things ought to be referred
> to the times of Constantius, under whom
> the excursions of the Persians into Mesopotamia
> were frequent. Nevertheless, they seem
> to have invaded those same
> lands even under Valens in the year 373."

And indeed, he strives to confirm the former opinion by citing the authority of Ammianus Marcellinus <note>A 4th-century Roman soldier and historian.</note>, book 29, page 383, in the margin; and the latter opinion in his notes on the Emperor Valens, § 12. But if we compare the cited passage of Ammianus with Tillemont's notes, we will find in both cases that no mention is made of that earthquake or excursion which happened under Constantius; but only of the Persian invasion into the lands of the Roman Empire, which Baronius <note>Cesare Baronio (1538–1607), an Italian cardinal and historian.</note> assigns to the year of Christ 371, and Pagi <note>Antoine Pagi (1624–1699), a French priest and chronologist.</note> to the following year. For this reason, this otherwise most diligent man—who is never to be mentioned by me without praise—fell into a common human error <note>original: "humanum quid passus est"; a polite way of saying he made a mistake.</note> in these observations when he used one passage of Ammianus for another. For Ammianus records both the earthquake and the Persian excursion explicitly, not in book 29 nor under Valens, but in book 17 and under Constantius. He narrates that this occurred during the consulship of Datianus and Cerealis, page 94, which is the year of Christ 358. Concerning their consulship, Pagi writes:

> "Consuls: Neratius Cerealis
> and Datianus. Regarding Datianus, one should read
> Valesius in his notes on Book 2 of Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century church historian.</note>, chapter 39,
> who has more about him from Libanius."

The same Valesius should be read in his notes on the praised passage of Ammianus, where first, regarding the peace made between Emperor Constantius and Sapor, the King of the Persians, on page 138, he has this:

> "Idatius mentions this embassy in his *Fasti* <note>A chronological list of events and officials.</note>:
> In the consulship of Datianus and Cerealis, the Persian
> ambassadors entered Constantinople on the 7th day
> before the Kalends of March <note>February 23.</note>. Themistius also speaks
> of the same embassy in his 13th Oration in praise
> of Constantius: 'While still residing in his winter quarters in Gaul,
> he compelled the Persians to seek peace. And recently in Antioch,

<margin>A</margin>
> I saw heralds sent from Susa and Ecbatana with
> letters wrapped in white linen, by which it
> was requested of the Prefect Musonianus that he
> obtain a treaty for the Persians from the Emperor.'"

Then on page 141, he describes the earthquake from the same Ammianus, by which Nicomedia collapsed.

> "On the 9th day before the Kalends of September <note>August 24.</note>.
> Idacius agrees in his *Fasti*: In the consulship of Datianus and Cerealis,

<margin>B</margin>
In this very year the earthquake occurred, such that the city of the Nicomedians was utterly overturned on the 9th day before the Kalends of September; and one hundred and fifty other cities were partially damaged. And Aurelius Victor <note>A Roman historian and politician.</note> says in his work on Marcus: 'Ephesus in Asia and Nicomedia in Bithynia were leveled by an earthquake, just as in our age Nicomedia was in the consulship of Cerealis.' Furthermore, Socrates in book 2, chapter 31

<margin>C</margin>
of his *Ecclesiastical History* says that Constantius, who had originally ordered a Council of Bishops to be held at Nicomedia, upon hearing of the disaster at Nicomedia, established Seleucia in Isauria as the seat of the Council. He adds: 'This happened under Datianus and Cerealis around the twenty-fourth of the month of August' <note>original Greek: "τὸ γέγονε Δατιανῷ κ᾿ Κερεαλίῳ περὶ τὴν ὀγδόην εἰκάδα τοῦ αὐγούστου μηνός"</note>; that is, the 9th day before the Kalends of September, not the 5th, as the Translator incorrectly rendered it, not noticing that the form of speech was Attic.

<margin>D</margin>
There still exists the *Monody* <note>A poem of mourning or lament.</note> of Libanius concerning this ruin of Nicomedia: which Ephrem the Deacon had also lamented in an elegiac poem, as witnessed by Gennadius and Marcellinus Comes in his chronicle." Thus far Valesius. There is therefore no reason to suspect that Ephrem spoke of any other earthquake or any other Persian excursion.

<margin>E</margin>
All things are so clear that it seems quite strange that most learned men twisted Ephrem's words to other times. The Syrian writers agree with Ammianus, Idatius, Socrates, and Aurelius Victor; they praise both the ruin of Nicomedia and Ephrem’s poem about it. The Author of the *Chronicle of Edessa* says:

<margin>F</margin>
> "In the year six hundred and seventy of the Greeks <note>original Syriac: "ܒܫܢܬ ܫܬܡܐܐ ܘܫܒܥܝܢ ܕܝܘܢܝܐ ܢܦܠܬ ܢܝܩܘܡܝܕܝܐ." This corresponds to 358/359 AD.</note>,
> Nicomedia collapsed."

Dionysius, the Patriarch of the Jacobites <note>The Syriac Orthodox Patriarch.</note>, says regarding the same year:

"In that same year six hundred and seventy of the Greeks, the great city of Nicomedia fell. And at this time, the King of the Persians defeated the Romans." <note>original Syriac: "ܒܗ ܒܫܢܬ ܫܬܡܐܐ ܘܫܒܥܝܢ ܕܝܘܢܝܐ ܢܦܠܬ ܢܝܩܘܡܝܕܝܐ ܡܕܝܢܬܐ ܗܝ ܪܒܬܐ. ܘܒܗ ܒܙܒܢܐ ܗܢܐ ܚܒ ܡܠܟܐ ܕܦܪ̈ܣܝܐ ܠܕܒܝܬ ܪ̈ܗܘܡܝܐ."</note>

> "The Easterners indeed assembled
> at Nicomedia:"

<summary>This page corrects the historian Tillemont's dating of an earthquake and Persian invasion mentioned by St. Ephrem, proving via multiple Roman and Syriac sources that the events occurred in 358 AD.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a scholarly discussion (likely by Giuseppe Simone Assemani in the *Bibliotheca Orientalis*) regarding the chronology of the life of St. Ephrem the Syrian, specifically synchronizing Church councils, Persian sieges, and the lives of contemporary saints like Jacob of Nisibis.</meta>

<page-num>XLVI</page-num>
# PREFACE

->A<-

> "...[they departed]: for when [the city] had collapsed from a massive earthquake and crushed the inhabitants, they withdrew to Seleucia, a city of Isauria, where they held a Synod."

That Greek year corresponds to the year of Christ 359, in which the Synod of *Seleucia* was held after the ruin of *Nicomedia* during the consulship of *Eusebius and Hypatius* <note>Consuls were the highest elected officials in Rome; their names were used to identify the year.</note>, as witnessed by *Socrates, Book 2, Chapter 31*. However, the earthquake occurred not in that year, but in the preceding one near the end of August, as is clear from what was said before. Furthermore, concerning the fall of *Nicomedia*, besides this sermon by *Ephrem*, there exists another, which *Gennadius* mentions in *Chapter 66*; from this, *Philoxenus of Hierapolis* also cites a fragment in his *book on the One of the Trinity incarnate and suffered, leaf 132.* See the *Eastern Library, Volume 1, page 56.* Regarding the beginning of the Persian War, *Pagi* notes this under the consulship of *Datianus and Cerealis*, that is, for the year 358: *During this consulship, the Ambassadors of the Persians entered Constantinople on the 7th day before the Kalends of March* <note>February 23rd.</note>. *Gothofredus, in the chronology of the Theodosian Code, says the beginning of the Persian movements must be dated from this year.*

->B<-

Meanwhile, another slip by *Tillemont* <note>Louis-Sébastien Le Nain de Tillemont (1637–1698), a renowned French ecclesiastical historian.</note> should be noted, who in *Volume 8 regarding St. Ephrem, article 8, page 278*, assigns the death of *St. Jacob*, Bishop of Nisibis, and the siege of the city of Nisibis attempted in a failed effort by *Shapur*, King of the Persians, to the year of Christ 350. *Ephrem, he says, was still staying in Nisibis at the time when Shapur, King of the Persians, surrounded that city in a tight and famous siege in the year 350; and the same Ephrem asked Saint Jacob to mount the walls and hurl the weapons of a curse at the enemy. If any room is to be given to conjecture, it could be believed that the death of St. Jacob, which seems to have happened in the same year 350, provided the occasion for Saint Ephrem to leave his homeland, where the holiness of such a great man had kept him until that day.* These things slipped from *Tillemont* as a man <note>A polite way of saying "to err is human."</note>. For it is certain that Nisibis was besieged by *Shapur* at the end of the year of Christ 337 or the beginning of 338, and that *St. Jacob* passed away to the heavens a few days after that siege was raised. See the notes we made in *Volume 1 of the Eastern Library, pages 17, 18, and 395*, where it is shown from the Edessene Chronicle and the annals of *Dionysius* that *St. Jacob*, Bishop of Nisibis, died in the Greek year 649, the year of Christ 338. *Tillemont* was led into error by the second siege of Nisibis, which happened in the year 350, during the consulship of *Sergius and Nigrinianus*, as is read in the Alexandrian Chronicle. But twelve years before this siege, *St. Jacob* had already departed for heaven.

->C<-

->D<-

I consider that the things said in the life of St. Ephrem (*Eastern Library, Vol. 1, page 50*) regarding the poems written by him *about the city of Nisibis, about St. Jacob, about the Emperor Constantine, about the calamities of his time sent by an angry God for the correction of mortals, and about the captivity and destruction of the villages and suburban monasteries of the same city*, should be referred to the first, rather than to this second siege of Nisibis. For Ephrem was present at the first, not the second siege; furthermore, when he joins *St. Jacob and the Emperor Constantine* together, he clearly seems to indicate that after the death of Constantine, while *St. Jacob*, Bishop of Nisibis, was still surviving, those calamities which he describes happened both to the city and to the suburban villages and monasteries. If only such a distinguished work were still extant, as well as another praised by Philoxenus in the *Eastern Library, Vol. 1, page 56*, titled: <insert>The Volume of the Martyrs of Nisibis</insert> <note>original Syriac: ܦܢܩܝܬܐ ܕܣܗܕ̈ܐ ܕܢܨܝܒܢܝܐ (Panqīthā d-Sāhdē d-Nsībhnāyē)</note>; for from those we might more accurately draw the ecclesiastical history of Mesopotamia during those times.

->E<-

The Arabic translator, following the authority of a certain Greek manuscript, omits from this sermon those things which we included between two asterisks in our edition, Volume 1, from page 41 to page 43, starting from those words: "the Veil of the Temple, though it had committed no sin" <note>original Greek: τὸ καταπέτασμα τοῦ ναοῦ μηδὲν ἁμαρτήσας</note>, up to that point: "Therefore let us cleanse ourselves, most beloved" <note>original Greek: διὸ δὴ καθαρίσωμεν ἑαυτοὺς ἀγαπητοί</note>. These same parts are also missing in the Oxford edition, page 29, in which, after those words, "and we do not wish to be changed from the darkness of our wickedness" <note>original Greek: καὶ ἡμεῖς ἀπὸ τοῦ σκότους τῆς κακίας ἡμῶν οὐ θέλομεν μεταβληθῆναι</note>, there is immediately added, "let us cleanse ourselves, most dear ones" <note>original Greek: καθαρίσωμεν ἑαυτοὺς ἀγαπητοί</note>, with no mention made of the earthquake, the eclipse of the sun, and the Persian raid—

->F<-

<meta>The catchword "excur-" indicates the next page begins with "excursionis," completing the phrase "Persian raid/excursion."</meta>

<page-num>XLVII</page-num>

-> ### ON THE WORKS OF SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN. <-

...mention of the excursion. The Vatican Greek codices <note>A "codex" is an ancient manuscript in book form.</note> 1524 (page 55, back side, line 27) and codex 1815 (page 2, line 20), as well as the Alexandrian codex 16 (page 75, back side, column 2, line 28), agree with the Oxford Greek codices. However, these [words] are found in the Vatican Greek codex 439 (page 244, line 4) and in Greek codex 14 (page 157, line 12), which formerly belonged to the Library of the Greek Patriarch of Alexandria and was transferred to the Vatican by Abraham Massad, as I noted in Volume 1 of the *Oriental Library*, pages 598 and 604. Finally, they exist in the ancient Latin versions that preceded the age of Ambrose <note>Likely referring to Ambrogio Traversari, a 15th-century translator of Greek fathers.</note> and Vossius <note>Gerardus Vossius, a 17th-century scholar who edited Ephrem's works.</note>, as is evident from the Vatican manuscript codices 5051 (page 53) and 6443 (page 275). Nor is there any doubt that these same words were represented in the Greek manuscript codices from which Ambrose himself and Vossius prepared their Latin interpretations: for it is highly improbable that both translators would have published in Latin something that was entirely absent from the Greek copies. Therefore, these points which I have produced so far not only prove my intended argument but also teach us that the Greek copies—as I said at the beginning—do not agree with one another at all.

<margin>Does not find [this] in...</margin> Among the spurious <note>Work of doubtful authenticity; not actually written by the claimed author.</note> works, Kohlius <note>Johann Peter Kohl, a scholar of the 18th century.</note> (page 239) reckons a sermon of Ephrem, that is, the *Encomium on the Departed Fathers* <note>original Greek: εἰς πατέρας τελειωθέντας; original Latin: in Patres Defunctos</note>. It begins: *“My heart aches; have compassion on me, brothers”* <note>original: τὴν καρδίαν με ἀλγῶ, συμπαθήσατέ μοι , ἀδελφοί</note>. It was published in Latin by Ambrose (Sermon 7) and Vossius (Volume 3, page 269). It appeared in Greek in the Oxford edition (page 119), and in both Greek and Latin in our edition (Volume 1, page 172). Furthermore, it exists in Greek manuscript form in the Vatican codices 439 (page 145) and 441 (page 2), Palatine 269 (page 44), Alexandrian 42 (page 163), Clementine 62 (page 229), Passioneo (Sermon 106), Saint Mark’s of Venice (Sermon 48), Vienna 31 (page 362), Barberini 31 (page 256), Coislin 60 (page 1) and 385 (page 107), Ottoboni 1 (page 157) and 2 (page 45), and Laurentian Medici 37. It is in Slavonic (Sermon 113) and Arabic (Sermon 14, Volume 1 of the *Oriental Library*, page 152). In all the codices I have just cited, three other sermons on the same subject are added to this one,

<margin>A</margin> specifically regarding the *Departed Fathers*, the first of which begins: *“On this first and notable day”* <note>original: ἐν ταύτῃ ἡμέρᾳ τῇ πρώτῃ καὶ εὐσήμῳ</note>. The second: *“For the wicked devil, overcome by force”* <note>original: νικηθεὶς γὰρ κατὰ κράτος ὁ πονηρὸς διάβολος</note>. The third: *“Knowing therefore, brothers, his weakness”* <note>original: εἰδότες οὖܢ ἀδελφοὶ τὴν ἀσθένειαν αὐτῷ</note>. And this third sermon in the Greek

<margin>B</margin> codices is said to be composed in *heptasyllabic meter* <note>Verse lines of seven syllables.</note>; while the other one, which precedes it, is in *tetrasyllabic* <note>Verse lines of four syllables.</note>. But in the Arabic version, these last three sermons are contained in a continuous oration under this single title:

<margin>C</margin> *Discourse XV. Also concerning the departed Fathers.*
“On this first and notable day, let us gloriously celebrate the mysteries of the Only-begotten Son” <note>original Syriac script: ܬܘܒ ܥܠ ܥܢܝܕܐ ܇ ܡܐܡܪܐ ܕܚܡܫܬܥܤܪ ܇ ܒܝܘܡܐ ܗܢܐ ܩܕܡܝܐ ܘܡܫܡܗܐ ܇ ܕܬܕܡܝܪܬܐ ܕܝܚܝܕܝܐ ܒܬܫܒܘܚܬܐ ܢܙܝܚ</note>. Where, after those words: “Because you have reached the quiet

<margin>D</margin> harbor, and have been made co-heirs of the great king” <note>original Syriac: ܕܡܛܝܬܘܢ ܠܠܡܐܢܐ ܫܠܝܐ ܇ ܘܗܘܝܬܘܢ ܝܪ̈ܬܐ ܕܡܠܟܐ ܪܒܐ</note>, there follows without any interruption: “For the Enemy, utterly defeated and sitting [down],

<margin>E</margin> says with a wail: woe to me, the unhappy one” <note>original Syriac: ܘܐܬܬܒܪ ܠܗ ܒܥܠܕܒܒܐ ܘܝܬܒ ܇ ܘܢܗܡ ܘܐܡܪ ܇ ܕܘܝ ܠܝ ܡܤܟܢܐ</note>. And after those words: “But I indeed rejoice in their loss, so that I may have them as companions in the unquenchable fire” <note>original Syriac: ܒܪܡ ܕܝܢ ܐܢܐ ܒܚܘܤܪܢܗܘܢ ܚܕܐ ܐܢܐ ܇ ܕܢܗܘܘܢ ܠܝ ܚܒܪ̈ܐ ܒܢܘܪܐ ܕܠܐ ܛܦܝܐ</note>, there is added:

<margin>F</margin> “We therefore, most beloved, knowing his weakness, let us attend to ourselves, imitating the Fathers” <note>original Syriac: ܚܢܢ ܗܟܝܠ ܚܒܝܒ̈ܝ ܡܐ ܕܝܕܥܢ ܚܠܫܘܬܗ ܇ ܢܙܕܗܪ ܒܢܦܫܢ ܇ ܒܕܡܘܬܐ ܕܐܒ̈ܗܝܢ</note>.
But although in the Arabic version these things are so joined together that they form one sermon, I have no doubt that the Greek translator expressed them separately from the Syriac text, as they were published by us in Volume 1, pages 175, 181, and 182. For Saint Ephrem, in composing those poems which are inscribed *on the Departed*, used both *tetrasyllabic* and *hepta-*

<meta>catchword: hepta-</meta>

<summary>This page analyzes various Greek, Latin, and Syriac manuscript sources for Saint Ephrem's sermons on the "Departed Fathers," addressing discrepancies in their content and poetic structure. The author argues that while some versions combine several discourses into one, they were originally distinct works written in specific meters.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory discussion (Prolegomena) regarding the poetic works and monastic subjects of Saint Ephrem the Syrian, specifically focusing on the "Shepherds of Mesopotamia" and the variety of manuscript traditions.</meta>

XLVIII

### PREFACE

...it is clear that he used seven-syllable [heptasyllabic] meters, as is evident from those which now appear in Syriac and Latin in Volume 3, sourced from the Nitrian codices <note>Ancient manuscripts found in the Monastery of the Syrians in the Nitrian Desert of Egypt</note>. See what I have noted on this matter in Volume 1 of the *Oriental Library*, page 61, concerning the multiple types of meters that Saint Ephrem employed, having borrowed the composition of musical modes from Bardesanes and Harmonius <note>Bardesanes (154–222 AD) was a Gnostic poet; his son Harmonius composed hymns that Ephrem later adapted for orthodox Christian use</note>.

<margin>B</margin> Tillemont <note>Louis-Sébastien Le Nain de Tillemont (1637–1698), a renowned French ecclesiastical historian</note> praises this sermon as being worthy of Ephrem in Volume 8, article 15, and in his notes on Saint Ephrem on page 758, in these words:

> "We have already spoken in article 15 about the elegant eulogy with which he adorns the **Shepherds of Mesopotamia**. The sermon that precedes this one seems no less worthy of him, and he calls himself by his own name within it. It is, to be sure, a vivid exhortation to repentance. Furthermore, the two poems that follow the eulogy of the Shepherds seem to be a continuation of this very eulogy—which nevertheless ends with its own conclusion and has its own glorification—or perhaps another similar hymn in praise of the saints who reported a glorious triumph over the devil."

Regarding who these ***Shepherds of Mesopotamia*** actually were, the same Tillemont explains more fully on page 292, drawing from <margin>D</margin> Sozomen <note>An early Christian church historian, c. 400–450 AD</note>, Book 6, Chapter 33. They were, in fact, hermits who wandered through the mountains and ate herbs like grazing cattle. These are the words of Sozomen:

> "Furthermore, the common people called them **Boskoi**, that is, *The Grazers*, who had then first introduced this type of philosophical life <note>In this context, 'philosophy' refers to the disciplined, ascetic life of a monk</note>. They call them this because they have no dwellings, they eat neither bread nor cooked food, nor do they drink wine; but living in the mountains, they perpetually celebrate God, occupying themselves with prayers and hymns according to the ritual of the Church. And when the time for eating comes, each one takes a sickle and, wandering through the mountain like grazing cattle, they eat herbs."
<margin>E</margin>

<margin>F</margin> Hence they were called *Boskoi* (the *Grazers*) by the Greeks. Their practice and origin began among the people of Nisibis around the mountain called Sigoron; the founders are praised by the same Sozomen as Batthaeus, Eusebius, Barges, Halas, Abbo, and Lazarus (who became a Bishop), <margin>A</margin> as well as Abdaleus, Zeno, and the Elder Heliodorus. Ephrem refers to this in that sermon when he says in Volume 1 of our edition, page 178:

> "They live in caves and among the rocks as if in bedchambers. Furthermore, they do not love or desire the mountains and hills that surround them as if they were wild barriers or high walls. For them, the whole earth and the mountains are their table; their dinner and lunch are wild and rustic herbs. Their chief drink is water from the streams, and likewise 'wine' from the caverns and crevices. Their own tongues are their churches, through which they continually perform their prayers. Throughout the twelve hours that the day contains, their prayer to the Lord is continuous. <margin>C</margin> The glory which they sing in the mountains and caves is an acceptable sacrifice which they offer to God. They are their own Priests, and they cure and heal our diseases with their prayers: for they are our perpetual petitioners and intercessors. They do not think of high things, nor do they seek the first seats; for their glory is humility, having become imitators of Christ, who became poor for the sake of us miserable ones. They grant themselves no rest in the world, because there <note>in heaven</note> they receive solace and refreshment. Let us also be made like them, who live in the mountains, and likewise partakers of the institution of their lives. They wander with the beasts like wild creatures, and fly like birds in the mountains. Like deer they graze with the wild animals: their table is always prepared, for they always eat the grasses and herbs. They themselves shine like lamps, wandering in the mountains."

I am pleased to transcribe these words here from the Arabic Translator, so that it may be rendered more and more manifest how great the diversity of both manuscripts and translators was even in ancient times.

<note>original: The following text is in Garshuni—Arabic language written in Syriac script</note>
And this is the praise of their new speech:
That they are in the desert caves and valleys.
They are like these peaks of the mountains.
And among them are those [who live] upon the rich leaves.
And this is like the religion of the... <unclear>hidden</unclear>... the trees.
And the beautiful pages: and their valleys are from them.
O God.

It also highlights the historical sources for these figures and the linguistic diversity of the manuscript traditions, including Syriac, Latin, and Arabic (Garshuni).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly analysis of the works of St. Ephrem the Syrian, specifically focusing on descriptions of ascetic monks and the linguistic variations between Greek, Arabic, and Syriac versions of his writings.</meta>

# ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHREM THE SYRIAN. XLIX

<margin>
VIII.
From a
Sermon on
the
Lifestyle
of the
Monks:
and con-
cerning
the Mon-
astic Fa-
thers.
</margin>

<margin>A</margin> They dwell in caves and among splendid rocks as if in decorated chambers. Their table is the whole earth and the mountains; their meal is the herbs of the field. Their drink is the sweet water of the streams. Within the hollows of the rocks, they have their churches. With their own tongues, they complete their prayers through the twelve hours that a day contains. Their prayer to the Lord consists of the Psalms, which they sing together in the mountains and caves. They serve as their own priests, and they heal our diseases through their prayers. They do not seek high status, nor do they ask for the first seats at a table, because their glory is humility. They have become imitators of Christ, who became poor for the sake of the wretched. They wander in the deserts like wild animals and fly like birds; like deer, they graze with the beasts, roaming the desert like shining lamps. <note>original: "In speluncis & rupibus splendidis habitant..." This passage describes the "Grazers" (Boskoi), a class of early Christian monks who lived in the wild without shelters or cooked food.</note>

Thus far the Arabic Translator: and there is no doubt that he faithfully transcribed these same words from an ancient Greek copy.

<margin>E</margin> Since these words differ significantly from our Greek manuscripts, it is clear that the value of the Arabic edition is increased here, and by comparing it with the Greek and Latin versions, no small benefit comes to the study of literature.

But to return to the "Grazer" <term>Boskos</term> <note>original: Βοσκός; literally "one who grazes" or "pastures," referring to monks who lived off the land like livestock.</note> monks, St. Ephrem mentions them in his *Epistle to John the Monk*, which begins: *Having many times exhorted the most devout <term>Theodore</term>...* <note>original: πολλὰ διανείσαντες τὸν εὐλαβέστατον Θεόδωρον</note>. Although we have dealt with the most religious Theodore with various exhortations, this appeared in Greek in the Oxford edition, page 343; in Greek and Latin in our edition, volume 2, page 186; and in Latin by <term>Vossius</term> <note>Gerardus Vossius, a 16th-century scholar and editor of Ephrem's works.</note>, volume 2, page 13. The title is: **To John the monk, regarding patience**, and on not being deceived by thoughts under the pretext of justifications, and saying "I walk as a **Grazer**"; and concerning temperance.

---

<margin>A</margin> *To John the monk regarding patience: and regarding the caution that no one should be deceived by thoughts under the pretext of justifications; nor say: "I walk as a Pastor/Grazer": and concerning temperance.*

<margin>B</margin> This exists written in Greek by hand in the Vatican codices 439, 440, 441, 442, 443; Alexander 16, 42; Coislinian 59, 60, 238; Venice St. Mark 51. It is the first sermon on the Passion; the 22nd sermon in Slavonic among those said to be recently translated from Greek; and the 1st sermon in Arabic. See Volume 1 of the *Oriental Library*, page 150, under this title:

<margin>C</margin> *The letter to John the Anchorite <note>original: ܐܒܝܠܐ (Abila), a Syriac term for a mourner or ascetic monk.</note> regarding patience, and that he should not be deceived in his thoughts concerning the asceticism of the lifestyle and concerning chastity.* <note>original: "ܐܓܪܬܐ ܠܘܬ ܝܘܚܢܢ ܐܒܝܠܐ..." This is written in Garshuni, which is the Arabic language written using the Syriac alphabet.</note>

> "The Epistle to John the monk regarding Patience: and that he not be deceived by thoughts in the pursuit of the solitary life: and regarding continence." Where the Greek says <term>Boskos</term> <note>Grazer</note>,
> and the Latin says *Pastor* or *Pascens* <note>Feeding/Shepherding</note>: the Arabic everywhere renders it as **Sajech** <note>original: ܣܐܝܚ</note>, that
> is, *one wandering in solitude*: and that type of life
<margin>D</margin> is called **Sajacha** <note>original: ܣܐܝܚܐ</note>, *wandering in the desert*. Hence, what is in the Greek text on page 187: "But many, having been admonished by the fathers and brothers, did not obey, saying: we depart as **Grazers**," <note>original: ἡμεῖς Βοσκοὶ ἀπερχόμεθα</note> is translated in Arabic as: **After they had been greatly admonished by the fathers and the brothers, they did not obey them, saying: We depart to be wanderers in the solitude.** <note>original: "ܒܥܕ ܡܐ ܡܢ ܐܠܬܘܫ..." This Garshuni text captures the monks' defiant desire for extreme isolation.</note>

<margin>F</margin> Likewise, what the Greek Translator has on the cited page: "Others, being mocked by the thought of self-satisfaction and lifted up by vainglory, hunting for praise from those who hear them, become, as they say, **Grazers**": this is rendered in Arabic as: **And others are deceived by the thought of pride in themselves, and they are lifted up by false greatness, seeking praise from the listeners, while they say that they have become wanderers.** <note>original: "ܘܐܚܪܝܢ ܐܢܟܕܥܘܐ..."</note>

<sig>g</sig>

<summary>This page discusses the lifestyle of the "Grazer" monks who lived in the wild and examines how the term for these ascetics (Boskos) is translated and interpreted across Greek, Latin, and Arabic versions of St. Ephrem's letters.</summary>
<keywords>St. Ephrem the Syrian, Boskos, Grazers, monasticism, Sajech, John the Monk, Garshuni, asceticism, Vatican manuscripts, philology</keywords>
