<header>PREFATORY REMARKS</header>

# PREFATORY REMARKS

->God, however, just as in perfect glory<-
->also led them out from the world so they might praise Him.<-
<note>original Syriac: ܐܠܗܐ ܕܝܢ ܐܝܟܢܐ ܕܒܬܫܒܘܚܬܐ ܓܡܝܪܬܐ ܐܦ ܢܦܩܐ ܡܢ ܥܠܡܐ ܕܢܫܒܚܘܢ ܠܗ̇</note>

Others were similarly deceived by the complacency of their own judgment and, puffed up by empty glory, sought praise from those listening to them, so that they wandered aimlessly in the desert.

Whatever they may be called—whether <term>Grazers<note>original Greek: Βοσκοὶ (Boskoi). These were a class of early Christian hermits who lived in the wild and ate only grass and herbs, like cattle.</note></term>, or <term>Strangers<note>original Syriac: ܐܟܣܢܝܐ (aksenaye). These were wandering monks or exiles who owned nothing.</note></term> wandering in the desert—it is certain that from their very origin they were adorned with the highest praises. This is clear from the passage of Sozomen <note>Salminius Hermias Sozomenus, a 5th-century Christian historian.</note> already cited, and Saint Ephrem confirms it in his sermon on the deceased Fathers. But when others later began to cultivate this same way of life, led away by their own desires and observing neither measure nor moderation, they sought primarily—or even exclusively—to be free from the yoke of the elders so they might have the liberty to follow their own wills. Then, quite vehemently and not without justice, they were attacked by all who wrote on ascetic matters: primarily Saint Jerome in his letter to Eustochium on maintaining virginity, John Cassian in his 18th Conference, chapter 7, and Saint Benedict in his *Rule*, chapter 1. These writers call them *Remoboth, Sarabaites, Circumcellions, and Wanderers*, <note>These are all technical terms for types of monks who lived without a rule or a superior, often wandering from place to place and living off the charity of others.</note> for they had no fixed home, but a perpetual lust for wandering here and there. Ephrem, in the sermon to John the Monk which I praised above, sharply rebukes such hermits because:

> ,, led by the stubbornness
> ,, and temerity of their own thoughts, they
> ,, progressed into a barren and waterless
> ,, land, bringing a violent death upon
> ,, themselves; or, being unwilling to live
> ,, under obedience and refusing
> ,, harmony in service, they threw
> ,, themselves into the same danger.
> ,, For everyone ought to measure themselves
> ,, by their own capacity and humble themselves
> ,, toward their neighbor in the love of God.
> ,, And even if one thinks they have
> ,, attained perfect virtue and have
> ,, risen above the passions, they should
> ,, not trust themselves even then, but
> ,, all things must be carried out with the
> ,, counsel of the elders. ,,

In these words, just as he praises those who first introduced this way of "philosophizing" <note>Early Christian writers often used the term "philosophy" to refer to the disciplined, ascetic life of a monk.</note>, he likewise refutes more recent ones who, though not endowed with the same virtues, 

<margin>A</margin> dared to begin at the point where one ought to finish. For it was the constant discipline of the Fathers that one should not attempt the hermit’s life before spending certain years in a community monastery. Ephrem confirms this with the examples of Saints Macarius and Anthony: namely, that it is not permitted to be led away by one’s own thoughts and plans, nor should one 
<margin>B</margin> trust in one’s own strength; rather, one must diligently insist on the footsteps and precepts of the holy Fathers and not deviate from them.

And here it should be noted that the deed of Anthony is cited by Ephrem in one way, and the deed of Macarius in another. Regarding Macarius, he speaks of him as if he were still living (for he did not die before the year of Christ 390, as Tillemont correctly shows 
<margin>C</margin> in Volume 8, pages 619 and 809, and thus he survived Ephrem himself). He says: "It is told of Abbot Macarius that he spoke thus:
'Trusting,' he said, 'in my cell, I practiced as a monk, while meanwhile my thoughts were causing me trouble, saying: Go into the inner desert.' And the rest in Arabic <note>Though the author says "Arabic," the script provided is Syriac, likely indicating a translation or a comparison of regional manuscripts.</note>:

> Once I was sitting in my 
> dwelling outside of the 
> region of Scetis, 
> and I was troubled by my thoughts 
> which said to me: 
> Go into the inner desert, and offer 
> your prayer and fast there, so that 
> your labor might be 
> beautiful.
<note>original Syriac: ܕܠܡ ܚܕܐ ܙܒܢ ܐܢܐ ܒܡܘܬܒܝܐ ܕܡܢ ܠܒܪ ܡܢ ܐܬܪܐ ܕܐܣܩܝܛܝ ܝܬܝܒ ܗܘܝܬ ܘܐܨܛܪܝܬ ܡܢ ܚܘܫܒܝ ܕܐܡܪܝܢ ܠܝ ܐܙܠ ܠܕܒܪܐ ܓܘܝܐ ܘܬܚܝܬ ܨܠܘܬܟ ܨܠܝ ܘܨܘܡܐ ܐܝܟܢܐ ܕܦܘܠܚܢܟ ܫܦܝܪ ܢܗܘܐ܀</note>

<margin>D</margin> ,, ... ,, Let us show, therefore, that the holy Fathers 
<margin>E</margin> ,, did nothing rashly or inconsiderately. It is told of the monk Macarius that he spoke thus: 'While I was dwelling in my cell in Scetis, my thoughts caused me trouble, saying: go into the inner desert.' ,, But regarding Saint Anthony, he speaks as if about one who had already passed into heaven, when he says: ,, And why do we not lower our eyes to the life of 
<margin>F</margin> ,, Abbot Anthony? We shall find that he did all things according to divine revelation. Nevertheless, did he not also live in a monastery? Did he not use clothing? Did he not eat bread? Did he not work with his own hands? Did he not have disciples, who indeed wept for him and buried him when he died? The Arabic interpreter:

> When I said to them, 
> "How you look [upon me]!"
<note>original Syriac: ܟܕ ܐܡܪܬ ܠܗܘܢ ܐܝܟܢܐ ܕܚܐܪܝܢ ܐܢܬܘܢ܀</note>

It cites various Church Fathers to argue that the hermit's life should only be attempted after years of obedience in a monastery.</summary>

<meta>This page (LI/51) continues the scholarly examination of the life and writings of Saint Ephrem the Syrian, focusing on the chronological markers provided by his mentions of Saint Anthony the Great and his famous meeting with Saint Basil the Great.</meta>

### ON THE WORKS OF SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN

<note>original Syriac: "ܐܝܟܢܐ ܕܐܢ ܐܬܒܩܝܢܢ..."</note>
As if we were to observe how all the divine mysteries flow to reveal his way of life. He had no food of bread; he did not even pray with his own hands alone; he had no private possessions, nor clothing, nor a grave provided by companions, nor even a small amount of money, nor anything at all, as if by a decree. “But if we consider the life of Anthony the monk <note>Saint Anthony the Great (c. 251–356), a central figure in early Christian monasticism.</note>, we will certainly find that he did everything according to divine revelation. For did he not live in a monastery? Did he not eat bread? Did he not labor with his own hands? Did he not have disciples who, wrapping him in cloths when he died, buried him?”

Yet Saint Anthony, the father of monks, departed from this life to the heavens in the year of Christ 356, according to the testimony of Tillemont <note>Louis-Sébastien Le Nain de Tillemont (1637–1698), a French ecclesiastical historian known for his rigorous use of primary sources.</note> in Volume 7, page 131. Therefore, this discourse was composed by Saint Ephrem neither before the year 356, in which Saint Anthony died, nor after the year 372. This was the year when Ephrem himself—after having wandered through the Egyptian desert (as narrated in his life in Volume 1 of the *Oriental Library*, page 40) together with a companion and an interpreter—set out for Caesarea in Cappadocia to enjoy a conversation with Saint Basil the Great. Thus, all the facts are consistent: both what is related concerning the <term>Boschi</term> monks <note>From the Greek 'bosko,' meaning 'to graze.' These were ascetics who lived in the wild and ate only grass or herbs, avoiding bread and cooked food.</note> and what is told of Saints Anthony and Macarius. These things were evidently witnessed or heard by Saint Ephrem both in Mesopotamia and in Egypt around the year 370.

<margin>
X.
In the Eulogy of Basil the Great, regarding the 40 Martyrs.
</margin>

And indeed, that Ephrem sought out Caesarea in Cappadocia, led by the Divine Spirit, so that he might see and know Basil—that "Mouth of the Church" and "Golden Nightingale of Doctrine"—is attested not only by Syrian writers but also by Greek ones. Foremost among these is Saint Gregory of Nyssa <note>Saint Basil’s younger brother.</note> in his eulogy of the same Saint Ephrem, which, having been translated into the Arabic language, is usually placed after Ephrem’s own discourses. Then, truly, Ephrem learned from a dove sitting upon Basil's right shoulder what sort of man Basil was, and how great; and Basil, instructed by that same dove, recognized the pilgrimage of this holy Father and knew him to be that famous Ephrem the Syrian. Most opportunely, therefore, did both enjoy mutual spiritual fellowship, so that the laborious pilgrimage was not useless to Ephrem.

---

**A** Indeed, having been taught by Basil, he learned the glorious deeds of the Holy Forty Martyrs <note>A group of Roman soldiers who were martyred for their Christian faith near Sebaste in Lesser Armenia in 320 AD.</note> who suffered at Sebaste under Licinius. Upon returning to his homeland, he adorned them, along with Basil, with a distinguished eulogy. Ephrem himself testifies to this in the praises he wrote for Basil: where, **B** after he had said how wonderfully he had profited from his mutual conversation with that most holy Bishop of the Caesareans, he said: *“Then,”* (on page 293 of this volume) *“my womb conceived wisdom, so that it might prepare the praises of the Forty Martyrs. For that excellent man placed into my ears the entire manner of their endurance: namely, how they chose to die for Christ, and how many dangers they despised **C** so that they might gain Him; then how many those Saints were in number, and he told the rest of the matters pertaining to their piety.”* Ephrem’s eulogy for Saint Basil the Great exists in Greek in the Vatican manuscript codices 1633, page 81, and Basilian 26, page 144; and in Slavonic on page 292. It was published in Greek in the Oxford edition, page 399; **D** in Greek and Latin by Cotelerius in Volume 3 of the *Monuments of the Greek Church*, page 54; and in our edition in this Volume 2, page 289. In Latin only by Vossius, Volume 3, page 225. Compare the *Acts of the Saints* for February, page 51, and Volume 2 for June, page 809; Baronius in the *Roman Martyrology* for June 14; and Combefis in the *Preaching Library*, Volume 7, page 41.

**E** Bollandus (Volume 1 for February, page 51) and Fabricius (*Greek Library*, Book 5) affirm that this eulogy was written by Ephrem while Basil was still alive. Tillemont denies this (Volume 8, note 14 on Saint Ephrem), where he shows from Ephrem’s own words that this holy Father speaks of Basil the Great as if he were dead. This is confirmed by the authority of the Syriac life of the same Ephrem, **F** which I published in Volume 1 of the *Oriental Library*. For there, on page 52, section 29, regarding the death of Saint Basil the Great and the praises composed by Ephrem, these words are found: *“But when the blessed Ephrem received the news of Saint Basil's death, it cannot be said with how much bitter sorrow he was affected, thinking that his sheep had been deprived of the sight of such a great Shepherd; although they were by no means forsaken by their Shepherd [Christ]...”*

<summary>This page discusses the chronological timeline of Saint Ephrem the Syrian's life and writings, specifically his interactions with Saint Anthony the Great and Saint Basil the Great. It argues, citing various historical sources, that Ephrem wrote his eulogy for Basil after the latter's death, despite some scholarly debate.</summary>

<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTORY REMARKS</header>

### INTRODUCTORY REMARKS

<margin>A</margin>
...it must be said that these were established when he assists all mortals with *his* prayers. Thus, by writing songs and hymns in his praise, he attempted to soothe the bitterness of his grief in whatever way he could. It is certain, therefore, that a <term>encomium</term><note>A formal speech or piece of writing praising someone.</note> of this kind existed not only in Greek and Slavonic, but also in Syriac: a point which Tillemont correctly concludes in volume 8, note 13, based on the character or style of writing, which is entirely different from that of Greek authors.

<margin>B</margin>
Therefore, after the death of Basil, which occurred in the year of Christ 378, that work of Ephraem was composed. To this, the same Author also added another regarding the holy Forty Martyrs of Sebaste <note>A group of Roman soldiers who became martyrs for their Christian faith in 320 AD.</note>, who suffered under Licinius; regarding this, see Surius and the *Acts of the Saints* for March 9th. Fabricius, in his *Greek Library*, book 5, asserts that this encomium is held in Combefis's *Preacher’s Library*, Volume 8, and that it is the very one mentioned by Lambecius in book 4, page 74. However, that learned man is mistaken: for Combefis and Lambecius, in the place cited, do not praise this sermon of Ephraem, which begins, *I wish to describe the image of the martyrs* <note>original: "εἰκόνα µαρτυρικὴν ἀναγράψαι βέλοµαι / Imaginem ſanctorum Martyrum deſcribere cupio"</note>; but rather another regarding *all the Martyrs of Christ who suffered throughout the whole world*, which begins: *The memory of the martyrs is always bright and lovely to Angels and men* <note>original: "φαιδρὰ καὶ ἐπέρατος ὑπάρχει ἁπαντὸς ἀγγέλοις καὶ ἀνθρώποις / Illuſtris & peramabilis eſt ſemper Angelis atque hominibus"</note>: regarding which see the Cologne edition by Vossius, Volume 3, page 740; the Roman edition, Volume 3, page 240; the Greek Oxford edition, page 411; and the Greek-Latin edition in this Volume 2, page 306. Moreover, that first one regarding the holy Forty Martyrs of Sebaste was first published in Latin by Vossius, Volume 3, page 230, and by us in Greek and Latin in this Volume, page 341, from the Vatican manuscript codex 679, page 34. That Ephraem is truly the author of this sermon becomes clear not only from those things which I noted above regarding the encomium for St. Basil, but also from the following words, in which the Syrian teacher expresses his own name and indicates that he is undertaking the praises of those martyrs which he had previously promised. *For I have not*, he says on page 341, *forgotten my promise, by which I promised you [an account] of the eminent victors*, 

<margin>A</margin>
when I was narrating those things which pertain to the holy and faithful Basil, the chosen man of God. And on page 355: *Intercede, O Triumphs of Christ, for the least and miserable Ephraem, that I may find mercy and be saved by the grace of Christ.*

<margin>B</margin>
<margin>Regarding the manuscripts of Ephraem in the note...</margin>
That style of writing—namely, weaving together encomiums for the Saints—was familiar to Ephraem. For in his life, besides the praises of Basil the Great and the Martyrs of Sebaste mentioned above, he is also said to have written about St. James of Nisibis; about the Martyrs of Nisibis; about the Martyrs of Edessa, Samona, Guria, and Abibus; about all the Martyrs of Christ; and finally, about the praises of the Edessenes who bravely resisted the Emperor Julian: Volume 1 of the *Eastern Library*, page 50.

<margin>C</margin>
Philoxenus, in his book *On the One of the Trinity Who Was Incarnate and Suffered*, cites a fragment from the encomium of St. Ephraem for *Julian Saba* <note>A 4th-century hermit in Mesopotamia.</note>, in the volume cited, page 56; and from the book on the Martyrs of Nisibis, in the same place. There exists, moreover, a sermon by Ephraem on the monk Julian in Greek, in the Otthobonian manuscript codex 1, page 381, and the Laurentian Medici codex 67 and 68. The beginning is:

<margin>D</margin>
*This is the best of the ascetics* <note>original: "οὗτος ὁ ἄριστος τῶν ἀσκητῶν"</note>: in Arabic, in Volume 1 of the *Eastern Library*, page 154, it begins: **I am the lowly and sinful [servant]...** <note>original Arabic: "أنا الحقـير الخـاطي المتـر..."</note>
Finally, it was issued in Latin by Vossius, Volume 3, page 264. The beginning: *This best and strongest among the champions of piety.*

<margin>E</margin>
There are also held in Greek several sermons of Ephraem regarding Abraham and his niece Mary: *Brothers, I wish to relate to you* <note>original: "ἀδελφοί µε βέλοµαι ὑµῖν διηγήσασθαι"</note>. On the Fathers who were perfected in their own times: *My heart grieves* <note>original: "τὴν καρδίαν µε ἀλγῶ"</note>. Another on the same: *On this first day* <note>original: "ἐν ταύτῃ τῇ ἡµέρᾳ τῇ πρώτῃ"</note>. On those who have fallen asleep in Christ: *What is this today?* <note>original: "τί τοῦτο σήµερον"</note>. On a deceased brother: *When an angel is sent* <note>original: "ὅταν ἀποσταλῇ ἄγγελος"</note>. On those who have said farewell: *As the divine law [dictates]* <note>original: "ὡς ὁ θεῖος νόµος"</note>.

<margin>F</margin>
On all the Martyrs of Christ: *Bright and lovely* <note>original: "φαιδρὰ κ᾿ ἐπέρατος"</note>. On the praises of the holy Martyrs: *Senseless and ignorant* <note>original: "ἀσύνετος ἀµαθής"</note>. On the sinful woman and the Pharisee: *Many being [present]* <note>original: "πολλῶν ὄντων"</note>. On the holy Good Friday <note>The text uses "Paraſceven," meaning the day of preparation/Friday.</note> and on the Thief: *Lately of Rebecca* <note>original: "πρώην ᾿Ρεβέκκας"</note>. On the Holy Apostles Peter and Paul: *Rejoice [in] Christ* <note>original: "χαίρετε Χριſτοῦ"</note>. Likewise on the Saints of the Old Testament, namely on Noah, and on continence: *But since some are caught* <note>original: "ἀλλ᾿ ἐπειδὴ ἁλίσκονταί τινες"</note>. On Lot, and on security: *Since the pleasure-seekers* <note>original: "ἐπειδὴ οἱ φιλήδονοι"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the catalog and critical analysis of the works of St. Ephrem the Syrian, specifically focusing on sermons attributed to him and their authenticity.</meta>

61

# ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHREM THE SYRIAN.

<margin>A</margin> In *Abraham & Isaac*, <note>original: "δοκιμάζει ὁ Θεός"</note> "God tests." In the praises of *Joseph the Patriarch*, <note>original: "ὁ Θεός τῷ Αβραάμ"</note> "God [spoke] to Abraham." In *Elijah the prophet*, and in the *widow of Zarephath*, <note>original: "ἡσυχίαν δότε"</note> "give silence" <note>This is a common liturgical call for the audience to pay attention.</note>. In *Daniel & the three youths*, <note>original: "φέρε δή"</note> "come now." Gerard Vossius published Ephrem’s sermons in Latin: I. In the *prophet Jonah*, and on the repentance of the Ninevites, Beginning: *Jonah the Hebrew*. II. On the praises of the most holy Virgin Mary, Mother of God, <margin>B</margin> *Inviolate, whole*. III. Lamentations of the same on the passion of the Lord: *Standing by the Cross*. Finally, they are held in Arabic: I. A sermon on the sinful woman, Beginning: —

-> <note>original Syriac: "ܬܘ ܚܙܘ ܐܚܝ܇ ܟܡܐ ܪܚܡܬ ܐܢܫܘܬܗ"</note> Come and see, my brothers, how great is the love for humanity <-
-> <note>original Syriac: "ܕܡܪܢ ܥܠ ܚܛܝܐ ܚܒܝܒܐ܂"</note> of our Lord for beloved sinners. <-

*Brothers, listen and see how great is the love of our Lord Jesus for beloved sinners* <margin>C</margin> II. On Elijah the prophet,

-> <note>original Syriac: "ܕܡܢ ܕܐܝܬ ܠܗ ܕܚܠܬ ܐܠܗܐ܇"</note> For whoever has the fear of God, <-
-> <note>original Syriac: "ܓܒܪܐ ܗܘ ܪܒܐ܂"</note> he is a great man. <-

*Great is he who possesses the fear of God.*

Tillemont <note>Sébastien le Nain de Tillemont (1637–1698), a famous French ecclesiastical historian known for his rigorous criticism.</note>, in his notes on the writings of Ephrem (Volume 8, page 757), considers whatever works bear his name concerning *Noah*, *Abraham*, *Lot*, *Joseph*, *Daniel*, <margin>D</margin> *Jonah*, and *Elijah* to be unworthy of the holy doctor and thus spurious. He passes the same judgment on the sermon regarding the *Transfiguration of the Lord*, which he instead attributes to Ephrem, Patriarch of Antioch; and on the sermons regarding the *Passion of the Lord*, the *Cross*, the *praises and laments of the Blessed Virgin Mary*, and the *sinful woman*. Indeed, he attributes the Eulogy of the Blessed Virgin Mother of God to a certain monk of Jerusalem, and asserts that all the others are entirely foreign to Ephrem’s style. Many words are not needed to show how undeservedly Tillemont rejects these praised sermons of Ephrem, which, as I have already noted, are found in the oldest Greek, Arabic, and Slavonic manuscripts. Compare the Second and Third Volumes containing the Syriac writings of Ephrem, and you will find many of those mentioned above <margin>F</margin> are represented in the Syriac text, though the Greek translator decided they should be condensed.

Casimir Oudin <note>A former monk turned bibliographer who cataloged ecclesiastical writers.</note> (Volume 1, *On Ecclesiastical Writers*), following Lambecius (Book 5 of the *Imperial Library of Vienna*, page 143), observes <margin>A</margin> that the Homily of St. Ephrem on the Sinful Woman, which was published by us in this Volume on page 297, is mutilated and defective. He says: "This homily also exists in the Greek Theological manuscript 116, number 9, as can be seen in Book 4 of these commentaries, page 69. But it is mutilated by almost two entire leaves at the beginning, and starts there <margin>B</margin> incorrectly with these words: <note>original: "πολλῶν ὄντων ὁσίων κ' ἀξίων τοῦ Θεοῦ"</note> 'Many being holy and worthy of God,' etc. This same oration also exists equally imperfectly among the works of St. Ephrem published in Latin by Gerard Vossius, Provost of Tongeren, in the Cologne edition by Quentel (Volume 3, page 711), where it begins in this way: *'Among many holy and worthy men of God, etc.'* In this <margin>C</margin> latter Imperial manuscript, however, it does not read simply 'many being,' etc., but 'therefore, many being' <note>original: "πολλῶν τοίνυν ὄντων"</note>. This clearly signifies, by the insertion of the particle 'therefore,' that those words are not the beginning of that homily, but rather the epilogue or the closing of the introduction, connecting it to the subsequent treatment of the proposed <margin>D</margin> theme." This same homily—as it appeared in Latin through Vossius, in Greek in the Oxford edition (page 405), and in Greek and Latin in our edition in this Volume 2 (page 297)—is found in the Greek Vatican manuscripts 438, 562, 564, 1216, 1633, 1641; Palatine 364; Basilian 13, 87; Barberini 248, 249; and Ambrosian 4. Therefore, <margin>E</margin> I willingly concede to Lambecius and Oudin that this homily is defective and mutilated, which will later be more clearly demonstrated by a comparison with the Syriac text. However, I firmly deny that it is mutilated by exactly two leaves at the beginning or that it starts incorrectly with the words 'Many being.' Whatever may be said of the Imperial manuscript, whose <margin>F</margin> beginning is 'Therefore, many being,' it is unlikely that all the Greek manuscripts found in Greece and preserved in Europe were transcribed from one and the same copy from which two entire leaves had fallen out, and that not even a single copy survived for us that escaped the sacrilegious hand of the person who tore them. Since, therefore, all the Greek copies match exactly, it is an indication...

<summary>This page discusses the authenticity of various sermons attributed to St. Ephrem the Syrian, defending them against the criticisms of Tillemont by citing ancient manuscripts in multiple languages. It specifically debates whether a famous homily on the "Sinful Woman" is missing its original introduction in the surviving Greek versions.</summary>
<keywords>Ephrem the Syrian, Gerard Vossius, Tillemont, Casimir Oudin, manuscript criticism, Syriac literature, Sinful Woman homily, Greek manuscripts, hagiography</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<page-num>LIV</page-num>
-># INTRODUCTION<-

<margin>A</margin>
...it is the case that the Greek Interpreter translated from the beginning exactly as it is read in our codices <note>Codices: ancient manuscript books</note>. However, that argument taken from the particle *consequently* <note>original Greek: *τοίνυν* (toinun)</note> inserted in the Caesarean codex is so slight that it ought not to undermine the reliability of all the other manuscript codices. These, of course, and similar additions of particles—and even entire sentences—should be attributed to the ignorance or carelessness of the scribes.

<margin>B</margin>
The complete Syriac homily <note>Homily: a religious discourse or sermon</note> of Ephrem regarding the sinful woman <note>original Latin: *mulierem peccatricem*; this refers to the woman who anointed Jesus’ feet in the Gospels</note> appears in Volume 3, page 387. The beginning: *Great physician, who came to heal the pains and diseases of the descendants of humble Adam, through the poured-out mercies of your grace.* <note>original Syriac: ܐܳܣܝܳܐ ܪܰܒܳܐ ܕܰܐܬܳܐ ܠܡܶܐܣܰܝ ܟܺܐܒܶܐ ܘܟܽܘܪ̈ܗܳܢܶܐ ܕܰܒܢܰܝ̈ ܐܳܕܳܡ ܡܶܛܽܠ ܚܽܘܒܳܟ</note> This is the fourth among the Exhortatory <term>Paraenetic: from the Greek 'parainetikos', referring to sermons intended to provide moral advice or encouragement</term> sermons of the holy Doctor, which are titled *On Penance*. It ends on page 412 with these words:

<margin>C</margin>
> Weep, therefore, just as she wept: and so you shall be healed, just as she was healed. Love Him as much as she loved: and the same pardon she received, you shall receive from that Good One, who does not wish for the destruction of sinners: namely Jesus, who is today, and tomorrow, and yesterday, and Himself unto the ages of ages; whose power is for eternity, and whose empire has no end: His door always stands open to all who knock with faith. Praise to His goodness and charity, whose benefits are immense—an unfailing sea of mercies and a most rich, ever-overflowing treasure. To Him, and to His Father, and to the Holy Spirit be everlasting glory: Triple Deity <note>original Syriac: ܐܰܠܳܗܳܐ ܬܠܺܝܬܳܝܳܐ (Alāhā tlithāyā)</note>, spare your servants according to your grace.

I have translated these things word-for-word so that it might be clear to anyone considering the Latin version placed alongside the Syriac text that it should not seem surprising if the Greek translation sometimes recedes further from Ephrem's original meaning. Indeed, where Ephrem calls Jesus Christ our Lord the **Triple of the Deity**, this is a Syriac idiom signifying the "mediator" between the Godhead and humanity, or between God and men: for a mediator approaches as a "third" <note>original Latin: *tertius*</note> between those for whom he interposes his mediation. Father Benedict <note>likely referring to Pedro Benedetti/Petrus Benedictus, a Maronite scholar who edited Ephrem's works in the 18th century</note> renders this passage thus: *I pray and beseech you, divine Trinity: command your suppliant servants to be free from fault:* referring it, of course, to the Trinity, whereas Ephrem speaks of our mediator Jesus Christ in a continuous discourse.

<margin>D</margin>
<margin>X <unclear>Regarding no Arabic monuments/codices of Ephrem providing mention...</unclear></margin>
The Arabic translator, working from the Syriac, it seems, and from the Greek language, presents us with another shorter version, the title of which in the Beroean manuscript codex 1, page 194 (concerning which see Volume 1 of the *Oriental Library*, page 585) is: **Sermon of Saint Mar Ephrem the Syrian concerning Mary the sinner.** <note>Note: The "sinful woman" is here identified as Mary, a common historical identification with Mary Magdalene in the Western and some Eastern traditions.</note> The beginning:

<margin>E</margin>
> Brothers, hear and see how great is the love for humanity of our Lord Jesus Christ toward sinners who return to Him with all their heart; how this sinful woman was healed from her sin.

Indeed, this can be observed in almost all the more recent Arabic translations: those who, in Syria and Egypt, translated some small work of the Holy Fathers from Greek or Syriac into the Arabic language...

<summary>This page discusses manuscript variations and translation challenges in the homilies of St. Ephrem the Syrian, specifically focusing on the story of the "sinful woman." The author critiques previous translations of Syriac idioms, arguing that certain terms refer to Christ's role as Mediator rather than to the Trinity.</summary>

### ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHREM THE SYRIAN.

undertake to translate, they do not everywhere stick to the words or the meanings; instead, they describe certain excerpts and, in a way, forge a new work themselves, yet they are not <margin>A</margin> afraid to sell it under the name of the ancient Author. Therefore, whatever writings of this kind come down to us, composed after the twelfth century, they are to be considered as having absolutely no <margin>B</margin> credibility unless, upon being compared to the original manuscript, it becomes clear that they agree with the Greek or Syriac text. Into this category, I place all the Arabic sermons (except for those fifty-two already mentioned, translated long ago from the Greek, concerning which see Volume 1 of the *Oriental Library*, from page 150 to page 156) which bear the name of Ephrem in our <margin>C</margin> Vatican codices.

In this number are rightly included the following sermons, which are represented in Beroean Codex 1: namely, first, the Sermon whose title on page 210 is: *A Discourse on Death, Sin, and Repentance, composed by the holy Mar Ephrem.* <note>original Syriac: "Mímró d-ʻal mawto wa-ḥṭíto wa-tyóbuto d-ʻabíd l-qadíšo mory Eprym"</note> It begins:
<margin>D</margin> *Sermo of Saint Ephrem on death, sin, and repentance. Beginning:* "Hear, my brothers: it is better to die than to live in sin. It is better to cut down a dry tree than for it to multiply. It is better to be sick and die than to be tormented by disease." <note>This Syriac excerpt is translated into Latin in the original text; I have rendered both into English here.</note>
> "Hear, my brothers: it is better to die than to live in sin. It is better that the
> <margin>E</margin> tree be cut down than that it remain dry. It is more desirable for the
> infirm to die than to be tormented by disease."

Here, as you see, there is nothing worthy of Ephrem; rather, the work seems to be a hodgepodge by some more recent Jacobite <note>A member of the West Syrian (Syriac Orthodox) Church; the author likely views them through a traditional Roman Catholic lens as "heterodox."</note> compiled from various poorly understood exhortations of the ancient Fathers. For while he directs the entire sermon in Arabic to the <margin>F</margin> people, he repeatedly uses the Syriac authorities of Scripture and the Syrian Doctors <note>Teachers or theologians of the church.</note>, which he immediately translates into the Arabic language. Thus, on page 211, he recites from a Psalm:
"One thing I have asked of the Lord, and this I seek: that I may dwell in his house all the days of my life." <note>original Syriac: "Ḥdo še'elt men Moryo..." — Psalm 27:4</note>
> "One thing I have asked of the Lord, and this I seek:
> that I may dwell in his house all the days of my
> life." And on page 212, from Jacob of Sarug <note>A prolific Syriac poet-theologian (c. 451–521 AD).</note>:
> "If anyone, while fasting from bread, sins with a word,
> what, I ask, does he gain? For he builds
> and then destroys, losing his reward. For while fasting,
> that righteous man is justified; but by speaking words of iniquity,
> he corrupts his righteousness. For the belly is empty;
> but the odor is foul and not accepted by God."
> In the same place, from the Syrian writer Balaeus <note>A 5th-century Syriac hymnographer.</note>, he brings forward this testimony:
> "We are not commanded to fast only from food,
> but let us fast from anger; and from fury let us cleanse our thoughts;
> and then our fast will be worthy of the kingdom of heaven."
> Finally, he closes this sermon by invoking the Saints, among whom the Jacobite author lists the heretical defenders of his own sect in these words:

Among the manuscripts of the esteemed Bodleian Library, there exists a sermon inscribed: *A sermon or homily of Mar Ephrem concerning Saint Pakidus, his wife, and two sons*, in Syriac, octavo format. I view this with suspicion, no differently than the other Arabic sermon concerning *Saints Peter and Paul*, of which I wrote in Volume 1 of the *Oriental Library*, page 156. For, besides the fact that no other Saint of this name occurs among the Greeks and Syrians except for Placidus (also known as Eustachius) with his wife Theopista and two sons, I have already noted in Volume 1, page 60, in the section *Furthermore, they are mistaken*, that whatever the more recent Syrians sing in the church in "Ephremic meter" <note>A specific 7-syllable poetic meter attributed to Ephrem</note> should not be ascribed to Ephrem himself unless it is established from elsewhere.

<page-num>LVII</page-num>

### ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHREM THE SYRIAN.

<margin>A</margin> "Come, brothers, and my beloved: a flock chosen in Christ our Lord, marked with the sign of holy baptism. Come, and hear the word that is salutary to your souls. Come, brothers, let us do business <note>The author uses the metaphor of "negotiating" or "trading" for spiritual gain.</note> as long as this celebration lasts, and let us acquire eternal life. Fill your eyes with tears, and stand before the Lord with weeping and lamentation. For if the eyes are filled with <margin>B</margin> tears, then the hearts will flee toward penance and repentance." This agrees with the Greek discourse which appeared in Volume I, page 28. It begins: *Come, beloved* <note>original Greek: δεῦτε ἀγαπητοί</note>. But the Arabic translation is more concise, and is extended only as far as those words which are held in the Greek text of the cited volume on page 32: *leaving behind therefore the works of the heathens, let us not turn back again and do the same things* <note>original Greek: καταλιπόντες οὖν τὰ τῶν ἐθνῶν ἔργα , μὴ τραφῶμεν πάλιν εἰς τὰ ὀπίσω , καὶ αὐτὰ πράττωμεν</note>.

<margin>C</margin> "Therefore, having left behind the works of the heathens, let us not turn back again and do the same things." The rest, which is read in Greek up to page 40, the Arabic translator omitted, and in its place composed an epilogue, the beginning of which is on page 188:

> Depart from sin by the mercy of God.
> From the midst of the world of darkness, to the light that shines.
> And to the kingdom that does not pass away, so that we may be delighted.
> In the time when God reveals... <note>original Syriac: ܐܺܝܙܶܠܘ ܡܶܢ ܚܰܛܺܝܬܳܐ...</note>

<margin>D</margin> "Flee the battle of luxury, and be not captured by the charms of pleasures. For luxury is the worst of all Satan’s nets. Luxury is the stumbling block of man." You see how much liberty those scribes take who copy things in Arabic: they either mutilate the discourses or make them up from their own brains.

Regarding the most glorious Virgin Mary, Mother of God, several of Ephrem’s praises exist in *Latin*; others only in *Greek*; and finally, many more in *Syriac*.

<margin>E</margin> The Latin versions are by an ancient anonymous translator, which Lipomanus, Surius, and Vossius <note>These were prominent 16th and 17th-century editors of hagiography and patristic texts.</note> later published: namely, I. *On the praises of the most holy Mother of God*: Beginning: *Entirely undefiled and pure, Virgin Mother of God.* <note>Lipom. Vol. 8, p. 10; Sur. Vol. 6, p. 1; Voss. Vol. 3, p. 207.</note> II. *A Prayer to the most holy Mother of God.* Beginning: *Immaculate, unpolluted, incorrupt, modest, Virgin Bride of God, our Lady.* <note>Lipom. Vol. 8, p. 11; Sur. Vol. 6, p. 2; Voss. Vol. 3, p. 210.</note> III. *Lamentation of the most holy Mother of God standing near the Cross.* Beginning: *Standing by the cross, the most pure Virgin, looking upon the Savior hanging on the wood.* <note>Lipom. Vol. 8, p. 14; Sur. Vol. 7, p. 205; Voss. Vol. 3, p. 198.</note> However, from which manuscripts—Greek or Syriac—these prayers were first translated into Latin is not clear to me; nor have I been able to find them in Syriac or Greek in the many great manuscripts which I reviewed in the first volume and in this second one.

The Book of Thocara (or Thecara) the monk contains *eight prayers to God and eleven to the Blessed Virgin*, which are said to be collected from the writings of St. Ephrem. These are represented in the manuscripts Coislin 312 from folio 310, and Vatican 1190 from folio 1117: they were reviewed by us in the Introduction to the first Volume, page 136 and following.

In the Syriac Nitrian Codex VII, which was written in the year 834 of the Greeks (523 AD), among 552 Ephremic hymns regarding the Church, three exist at numbers 35, 36, and 37, which contain a comparison between Eve and Mary. Beginning:

> I have come to you... I have come to you
> For two words... for two words
> When you see... or you hear
> In one hour... that in one hour <note>original Syriac: ܐܳܬܶܝܬ ܠܳܟܽܘܢ...</note>

"Both without guilt, both simple, Mary and Eve, let one be compared with the other: one was the cause of our death, the other of our life." These appeared together in Volume 2 of the Syriac-Latin edition, page 327.

<margin>F</margin> Nitrian Codex VIII, written in the year 863 of the Greeks (551 AD), contains as many songs of praise for the Blessed Virgin Mary as it does discourses on the birth of Christ. Beginning: Oh...

> Daughter of the flesh who is like...
> The child from you is flesh...
> Inside the womb which is like...
> Inside the belly which is like...
> That wonder which is like... <note>original Syriac: ܒܰܪܬܳܐ ܕ݀ܦܰܓܪܳܐ...</note>

"It happened most gratefully and by far most pleasantly..."

<page-num>58</page-num>
### PREFACE

> “...this day has dawned, Lord, for Kings, Priests, and Prophets: it has fulfilled their predictions in reality and confirmed them. Today the Virgin bore Emmanuel in the town of Bethlehem.” Published in Volume 2, Syriac-Latin, from page 396.

<margin>B</margin>
Regarding these Syriac sermons of Saint Ephrem on the Most Blessed Virgin, I believe no one will raise a doubt; for they all harmonize with Ephrem’s style, genius, and time. This is further confirmed by the venerable antiquity of the manuscript codices dating from the beginning of the sixth Christian century. Furthermore, these same sermons on the birth of Christ are cited by Philoxenus, Bishop of Hierapolis, who was writing around the year 512 AD. See Volume 1 of the *Oriental Library*, page 56.

<margin>C</margin>
The Syriac sermons on the *Blessed Virgin*, the *Holy Martyrs*, *Penance*, the *Deceased*, and various annual solemnities seem to be of the same authentic origin; these are prefaced in the Vatican Syriac Codex X, page 122, with this title:

<margin>D</margin>
The praises of the teaching-songs <note>original Syriac: *madrashe*, a form of instructional hymn</note> of Saint Ephrem, which are used at the altar or the consecration, and in the shrines of the saints; and on the feasts of our Lord, and on the days of fasting, and on the Passion of our Lord, and on His Resurrection, and on His Ascension, and on every occasion; and upon them are the “Scales” <note>original Syriac: *souloke*, referring to the melodic steps or rungs of the hymns</note> of the songs by the holy teacher Saint Ephrem.

<margin>E</margin>
> “The ‘Scales’ of hymns by the holy teacher Ephrem. Of the five hundred Scales he composed, no others are known to survive in our times except for these forty which we have at hand and which we are beginning to transcribe.”

The first, to the Tone: *This is the month* <note>original Syriac: *Shalel l'ammono*</note>, begins on page 122, on the reverse side: *A gift was given* <note>original Syriac: *Deshna ethyhib*</note> to *Blessed Maria*. The second, to the Tone: <margin>F</margin> *Oh, what a race* <note>original Syriac: *O mono haw gensa*</note>... *The Father wrote a Letter*, begins on page 125: *It was not because I begot you* <note>original Syriac: *Lo hwo denno awledtok*</note>. And so on for the rest. This codex was written in the year 1875 of the Greeks <note>The Seleucid calendar era; this corresponds to 1563 AD</note> (1563 AD) in Sadad, a village in the region of Damascus, at the Church of the Mother of God, on November 26th, as noted on pages 167 and 168. Regarding these forty Scales, therefore, there is no doubt among the Syrians that they are the genuine offspring of Ephrem—and quite rightly so. For Sozomen (Book 3, Chapter 16) and Theodoret (Book 4, Chapter 29) speak of these Scales or hymns when they say:

> “Since Harmonius, the son of Bardesanes <note>Bardesanes was a famous 2nd-century philosopher and poet often accused of heresy</note>, had formerly composed certain songs, and by mixing impiety with the sweetness of the melodies, he charmed the souls of the listeners and drew them toward destruction; Ephrem himself, borrowing the composition of the melodies from that source, mixed piety with the songs, and provided the listeners with a medicine that was at once pleasant and life-giving. And even now, these Songs make the feast days of the Martyrs more joyful and more splendid.”

The Greek writers are in agreement with the Syriac author of the *Life of Ephrem*, who seems to have had the “Scales” we mentioned above before his eyes when he wrote:

And behold, he saw that many did not know,
And were led by the melodies of the sky <note>meaning deceptive or worldly songs</note>.
The son of Bardesanes had set songs
To disturb the minds of the children of men.
And Ephrem came and set songs
For hearing and for reading. And in every kind
Of wisdom, he arranged the “Scales.”
And all those Scales which
Saint Ephrem made
Were about five hundred
Scales. And now they are found
In the land of Egypt, and in the holy city
Of Jerusalem, and in Tur Abdin,
And in all the monasteries.

> “Now indeed, the blessed Ephrem, having been sufficiently taught how much those men were captivated by music and song, and desiring to call them away from profane and dishonest games and the dances of youths, established choirs of Virgins. He taught them hymns, scales, and odes composed of sublime and spiritual thoughts, concerning Christ's birth, Baptism, Fasting, and the other mysteries of Christ’s dispensation, concerning the resur—” <note>The text breaks off here to be continued on the next page</note>

<meta>catchword: surre- (resurrection)</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the authenticity of Saint Ephrem the Syrian's hymns (Scales), explaining how he adapted popular musical melodies to promote orthodox Christian doctrine against the influence of Harmonius. It cites various historical sources and a 16th-century manuscript from Sadad to support the genuineness of these forty surviving works.</summary>

<page-num>LIX</page-num>

### ON THE WORKS OF ST. EPHRAEM THE SYRIAN.

<sig>Vol. II. h 2</sig>

<meta>Catchword: HIS-</meta>

„ ...the Resurrection, and of the Ascension: furthermore about the Martyrs, about Penance, and about the Departed; and he brought it about that those Virgins would gather at the Church on all the festivals of the Lord, the celebrations of the Martyrs, and on the Lord's Days <note>Latin: *diebus Dominicis*, referring to Sundays</note>. “ The testimony of Greek and Syrian writers proves that "Scalas" <note>Latin: *Scalas*, literally "Ladders," referring to rhythmic or tiered hymns</note> of this kind—the Ephraemian Hymns concerning the mysteries of the Lord, the Blessed Virgin, the Martyrs, Penance, and the Departed, such as those described in our Syriac Vatican Manuscript X—must be considered genuine works.

In the same Syriac Vatican Manuscript X on page 40, and in all the Prayer Books <note>Latin: *Horologiis*, liturgical books containing the services for the hours of the day</note> or weekday offices of the Syrian Jacobites <note>A term used here for the Syriac Orthodox tradition</note>, whether printed or in manuscript, *Ephraemian hymns of seven syllables* are presented on the same subjects as the "Scalas" mentioned above: namely, regarding the *Blessed Virgin, the Martyrs, and Penance,*

<margin>A</margin> and the Departed. The first of these on page 40 begins: *You who are hidden in the bosom of your Father.* <note>Original Syriac: ܐܰܝܟ݁ܰܢ ܕ݁ܰܟ݂ܣܶܐ ܐܰܢ݈ܬ݁ ܒ݁ܥܽܘܒ݁ܳܗ ܕ݁ܰܐܒ݂ܽܘܟ݂</note> There are eighteen of these hymns; they are preceded by 60 others authored by Jacob of Serugh <note>A prolific 5th-6th century Syrian poet-theologian known as the "Flute of the Holy Spirit"</note>, and followed by 7 others that bear the name of Balai <note>A 5th-century Syriac poet known for his liturgical hymns</note>.

<margin>B</margin> It would be a long task if I wished to begin a discussion here regarding all the songs or poems about the Blessed Virgin or the holy Martyrs that appear in the church books of the Syrians, or whether they should be classified as fake or genuine hymns. However, because we are publishing not only the Greek writings of Ephraem but also the Syriac ones, we shall discuss these individually in their proper place.

<margin>C</margin>

->From the Vatican Library, on the Ides of February<-
->In the Year of our Lord 1743.<-

->Regarding the second Greek and Latin Volume<-

## OF THE WORKS OF SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN
### A N  E P I S T L E.
<note>Saint Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373) was a prolific Syriac-language hymnwriter and theologian. This edition presented his works in Greek and Latin for Western audiences.</note>

---

If it should seem a wonder to anyone that three volumes of Ephrem's Sermons, published only in Latin, were produced by the distinguished Gerard Vossius <note>Gerardus Vossius (1547–1609) was a scholar who published a major Latin edition of Ephrem's works in the late 16th century.</note> over the space of ten years—for he brought the first to light in the year of Christ 1589, the second in 1593, and completed the third in 1597—it will perhaps seem no less a wonder that ten whole years have slipped by for me to make this second volume of the Greek-Latin edition available to the public. For just as many years have passed since, with your approval, most distinguished Cardinal, I published the first volume of the most holy Syrian Doctor and Prophet, translated from Greek into Latin and illustrated with notes and <term>Prolegomena</term> <note>original: "Prolegomenis"; introductory essays or scholarly prefaces describing the history and authenticity of the texts.</note>. 

But, such is your great kindness, I hope you will take it in good part if this volume has not come forth into the public light as swiftly as you desired. For me, the cause of the delay—or rather, the deliberate decision to postpone this edition until the present day—was twofold: One reason indeed was that while I was preparing the second and third volumes, I was ordered by the Supreme Pontiff Clement XII <note>Pope Clement XII (reigned 1730–1740) was the patron of much of Assemani's work.</note>, at the request of the entire Maronite nation <note>The Maronites are an Eastern Catholic Church in full communion with Rome, primarily based in modern-day Lebanon.</note>, to sail to Syria for certain ecclesiastical matters, as I had explained to you more fully by letter. Thus, I was forced to interrupt what I had begun; and even after my return to the City [Rome], I was still occupied in settling those matters—

<meta>catchword: return [regres-]</meta>

<summary>In this dedicatory letter, Joseph Simon Assemani explains to Cardinal Querini why the second volume of St. Ephrem the Syrian's works took ten years to publish, citing his diplomatic mission to Syria for the Maronite Church under Pope Clement XII.</summary>
<keywords>Joseph Simon Assemani, Cardinal Angelo Maria Querini, Saint Ephrem the Syrian, Gerard Vossius, Pope Clement XII, Maronites, Vatican Library, Syria, 18th-century scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory letter or preface to a multi-volume collection of the works of Saint Ephrem the Syrian. The previous pages likely established the project's patron and initial scope.</meta>

LXI
-> E P I S T O L E <-

I would have been occupied with my return, had not the wisdom and authority of the Most Holy Father **BENEDICT XIV** <note>Pope Benedict XIV (reigned 1740–1758), known as a great scholar and patron of the arts.</note> untied the knot: to whom I profess to owe a great deal, both for many other benefits bestowed upon me, and also because he recalled me from the unpleasant noise of legal disputes <note>original: "ingrato causarum strepitu"</note>, into which I had fallen whether I wanted to or not, back to that most pleasant **Vatican retreat**, where I might devote myself entirely to the unrolling of books according to my custom. 

The other reason [for the delay] was to allow space in the meantime for a man who was truly most learned and outstanding in every kind of doctrine and language, but of a very advanced age: **Peter Benedict**, a Syrian Maronite <term>Maronite: A member of an Eastern Catholic Church in full communion with Rome, primarily from Lebanon and Syria.</term> of the **Society of Jesus** <note>The Jesuits.</note>, who was cultivating the "little field" committed to him. He had undertaken the task of translating the Syriac works of **Saint Ephrem** into the Latin language while I was working on the Greek versions. Although he executed this duty, which You assigned to him, with the greatest possible diligence, he was nonetheless unable to bring it to a final conclusion. He published two volumes with the greatest elegance he could muster; but while he was seeing to the third and final volume, the excellent old man, worn out by labor and years, yielded to the fates <note>A poetic way of saying he died.</note>, leaving the work unfinished. 

This was also your intention, Most Eminent Prince <note>likely referring to a Cardinal</note>, and your plan: that the work should be urged forward from both sides, so that this edition, so splendid and clear as decreed by You, might more quickly see the light of day. Nor were there lacking incentives to execute this task strenuously and industriously. For that almost incredible diligence and vigilance of yours excited both of us to apply ourselves to the task You handed down. We saw that You did not cease, night and day, to write commentaries filled with varied learning, and to embrace each and every discipline in your mind and handle them with your pen in such a way that—unless most learned letters were sent out into the light—hardly any weeks passed by for You, whether You were in perfect health or even lying in bed due to illness. 

Therefore, spurred on by your examples, after that most famous man—the glory and ornament of my countrymen <note>referring to the deceased Peter Benedict</note>—ceased both to live and to write, I decided there should be no delay in having this work immediately succeed the volumes published by him.

I will now briefly indicate what has been achieved by me in this edition. I had already warned readers in my Letter to You prefixed to the first volume that we were maintaining the order of the **Oxford Edition** <note>referring to the Greek edition of Ephrem's works published in Oxford in 1709</note> for this reason: because out of the many Greek manuscripts existing in the Vatican and elsewhere, no copy has come to us which completely agrees with another, whether in the arrangement and number of the *Sermons*, or in the total conformity of titles, words, and sentences. We see this same thing even in the Latin versions of Ephrem's works, of which neither the order, nor the titles, nor the words for the most part agree. For this reason, since the first volume comprises the first thirty-two Sermons of the Oxford Edition, we decided that in this second volume, those which the same edition represents from the thirty-first Sermon to the last should be published in the same order. However, as for why we added some additional sermons or fragments here and there in our edition, this reason moved us: namely, because [there were] gaps...

The author explains his decision to follow the structure of the 1709 Oxford Edition due to inconsistencies among available Greek manuscripts.</summary>

### E P I S T O L A.
<note>Latin: "Epistle" or "Letter"</note>

...we have deemed it necessary to complete many [passages] that occur in the Oxford edition, and to add other excerpts that were omitted by Edward Thwaites <note>Edward Thwaites (1667–1711) was an English scholar and editor of the first Greek edition of Ephrem the Syrian's works (Oxford, 1709).</note>, the editor of that edition, and which seemed to pertain to the published short works, based on the authority of the oldest manuscripts.

Hence, after the Sermon whose beginning in the Oxford edition is on page 347, and in this volume on page 192—*my Christ-loving brothers* <note>original Greek: ἀδελφοί μου φιλόχριστοι</note>—we have subjoined another on page 209, which begins: *beloved, let us prefer nothing more than unfeigned love.* <note>original Greek: ἀγαπητοί, μηδὲν προτιμήσωμεθα πλέον τῆς ἀνυποκρίτου ἀγάπης.</note> Ambrogio Traversari <note>original Latin: Ambroſius Camaldulenſis (1386–1439), a theologian and translator of Greek patristic works.</note> had published this in Latin; however, it is found in Greek on page 455 in the same Oxford edition, and in the manuscript codices: Vatican 1774, Palatine 17 and 269, Imperial Vienna 31, 38, and 144, Basilian 17, Barberini 31, and in Slavonic as Sermon 103, page 234. Vossius <note>Gerardus Vossius (1577–1649) published a famous Latin edition of Ephrem's works.</note> published the same, but not in the same order, nor as a continuous discourse: for those things which are held as a single sequence in Ambrogio's version and in the cited manuscripts are read separately and in different volumes in the Vossius edition. Specifically: in Volume 1 of the Roman edition, page 11, near the middle, starting from these words: *It remains therefore, brothers, that we have nothing prior or more ancient than the possession of charity.* Volume 1, page 14: *And these things indeed, and more besides these, regarding charity.* Volume 3, page 201, in that Sermon whose beginning is *All celebration*, from these words: *we indeed, small and great alike, men and women, let us celebrate the Lord's feasts in the Christian manner.* Volume 3, page 202, in the same Sermon, from these words: *But what kind of things are to be revealed after it?* Volume 1, page 198: *The Christ-beloved listeners asked: recount to us also the rest, so that we may hear.* Finally, Volume 1, page 201: *Lest all be in one punishment, etc. We know from the Gospel that there are various places of torment.* Other Greek manuscript codices present almost the same "Questions and Answers" separately from that Sermon; we have therefore decided to publish them on page 377 from Vatican Code 438, as they are somewhat different from those published in this volume on pages 192, 212, and 253.

That sermon titled *On the disease of the tongue and evil affections*, which appeared in the Oxford edition on page 398, we have completed as it was defective and mutilated, using other Greek manuscript codices on page 279 of this volume. It begins: *All rests are prepared in all humanity.* <note>original Greek: ἡτοιμασμέναι εἰσὶν αἱ ἀναπαύσεις πᾶσαι ἐν πάσῃ ἀνθρωπότητι.</note> Diverse desires are found scattered among all men. Pietro Francesco Zini <note>A 16th-century Italian scholar known for translating Greek works into Latin.</note> had published it in **Latin** as Sermon 5 of the Venice edition of 1574, folio 21; as did Vossius in Volume 1, page 103. It exists, however, in Syriac, but far more richly, in the Nitrian <note>The Nitrian manuscripts come from the Monastery of St. Mary Deipara in the Nitrian Desert of Egypt; they are famous for preserving ancient Syriac texts.</note> Codex V, page 236, marked with this title: <term>ܡܠܬܐ ܕܠܘܚܡܐ ܕܠܐ ܢܟܣ܊ ܡܢ ܕܢܟܣ܊ ܠܚܒܪܗ ܘܗܘ ܢܕܝܕܐ ܘܡܠܝ ܟܠ ܡܘܢ̈</term> <note>original Syriac: "A word of correction for one who does not rebuke, from him who rebukes his companion, and he is vile and full of all [vices]."</note> **On Correction:** It begins: *He who rebukes ought to be free from blame himself.* Those things which we have translated from the Greek begin in the cited Nitrian Codex on page 239, from these words: <term>ܐܦܢ̈܃ ܕܝܢ ܒܟܠܗܘܢ ܒܢ̈ܝܢܫܐ ܟܝ̈ܫܝܢ ܪܓܝ̈ܓܬܐ܃ ܕܠܟܠܢܫ ܬܗܘܐ ܐܓܘܢܐ܃ ܕܒܗ ܢܕܪܘܫ ܬܪܥܝܬܗ.</term> <note>original Syriac: "For indeed, in all men desires are spread, so that for every man there might be a struggle in which he may exercise his mind."</note>
" Thus all desires are scattered among all men, so that

It specifically details the reconstruction of fragmented texts regarding Christian charity and the moral "disease of the tongue."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the editorial preface regarding the Greek and Syriac editions of St. Ephrem the Syrian’s works, specifically discussing the reconciliation of various manuscripts and previous editions like the Oxford edition by Edward Thwaites.</meta>

<page-num>LXIII</page-num>

-> # LETTER <-

This serves as proof that, even in the Greek manuscripts, not even half of this sermon is shown. It will, however, be published as soon as possible in Latin and Syriac in the third volume, which—after the death of the renowned Peter Benedict—Stephen Evodius, Archbishop of Apamea in Syria and my nephew by my sister, has undertaken to prepare at your command, Most Eminent Prince <note>This likely refers to a Cardinal or high-ranking ecclesiastical patron, possibly Cardinal Quirini or Pope Clement XII, who sponsored these scholarly editions.</note>.

The text titled *Concerning Lady Sala* <note>original: "De Domina Sala"</note>, which appears on page 419 of the Oxford edition, does not belong to Saint Ephrem at all, as everyone knows; rather, it is taken from the *Lausiac History* of Palladius <note>A 5th-century collection of biographies of the desert fathers.</note>, as Edward Thwaites himself observes in his notes. We have reprinted this on page 393 so that nothing from the Oxford edition is missing here, placing it after those *Questions and Responses* mentioned above.

The fragment of a sermon whose beginning appears on page 423 of the Oxford edition—*into the battle of the mighty* <note>original Greek: "εἰς μάχην δυνατῶν"</note>—actually exists within that sermon which begins: *last year some people came to me* <note>original Greek: "πέρυσι μοι προσῆλθόν τινες"</note>. Consequently, we are publishing the entire work on page 411, based on Vatican manuscripts 441 and 442, and Alexandrian manuscripts 16 and 42.

After the fragment *on the divine breath* <note>original Greek: "περὶ τοῦ ἐμφυσήματος"</note>, which appeared on page 423 of the Oxford edition, we have added several other pieces on the Book of Genesis from page 323 to page 326, sourced from Vatican manuscripts 1520 and 1657, and Alexandrian manuscript 7. Furthermore, we have added excerpts from Greek <term>catenae</term> <note>Literally "chains"; these are biblical commentaries made up of strings of quotes from various Church Fathers.</note> on the Scriptures—specifically on Job, Psalm 93, the Transfiguration of the Lord (or the Gospel of Matthew), and the Nativity of the Lord—from page 424 to page 427. These were supplied to us by Vatican manuscripts 251, 752, 1610, and 1642, and Palatine manuscript 230.

Edward Thwaites published the *Sermon on the Eight Thoughts* on page 422, though it was incomplete in the <term>Cromwellian Codex</term> <note>A manuscript collection formerly owned by the family of Oliver Cromwell and later donated to the Bodleian Library.</note>. We have filled its gaps in this volume on page 428 using Vatican manuscript 375 and Palatine manuscript 91. At the same time, we demonstrate that the greater part of this same sermon is contained within the one titled *On the Perfection of a Monk*, which begins on page 411 with the words *Last year*.

The *Testament of Ephrem*, which appeared in Greek on page 365 of the Oxford edition, we have compared with the Syriac text on page 395, and we have placed the variant readings on page 433.

Finally, after the sermon titled *On Faith* <note>original Greek: "περὶ πίστεως"</note>, which begins *The Lord in the Gospel* <note>original Greek: "ὁ Κύριος ἐν τῷ εὐαγγελίῳ"</note> and occupies the last place in the Oxford edition, we have added several sermons starting from page 341 so that this volume might grow to its proper size. These additional sermons are related in some way to those published by Edward Thwaites. Among them is the *Eulogy for the Forty Holy Martyrs*, which Ephrem himself had promised to his listeners while composing the praises of Saint Basil the Great on page 289 of this volume. Furthermore, the sermon titled *On the Perversity of Morals*, which holds the forty-eighth place among the fifty praised by Photius <note>A 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople known for his "Bibliotheca," a collection of book reviews.</note> on page 183, is edited here separately on page 368, based on the authority of the manuscripts, just as it was in the edition by Vossius <note>Gerardus Vossius, a 17th-century scholar who also edited Ephrem's works.</note>.

# OF OUR HOLY FATHER
# EPHREM THE SYRIAN

## ON THE LIFE OF THE BLESSED
## ABRAHAM, AND HIS NIECE
## MARY.

---

### ENGLISH TRANSLATION OF THE GREEK TEXT

<margin>A</margin>
**M**y brothers, I wish to relate to you the beautiful and perfect <term>way of life</term> <note>original: πολιτεία (politeia) — This term refers to the specialized "conduct" or "citizenship" of a monk or saintly person.</note> of a wonderful man; a life which he began and completed in a way that brought him glory. Yet I hesitate to record the wonderful and bright witness of the image of his virtue, so beloved by God.

<margin>B</margin>
For behold, while the man’s life and conduct were beautiful and perfect, I am weak and <note>original: ἰδιώτης (idiōtēs) — unlearned or lacking formal training.</note> unlearned. While the image of his virtue is bright and wonderful, the "colors" of my words are thick and fearful. Nevertheless, even though I am weak and unlearned, I dare to speak; even if I cannot worthily grasp his perfection, I will relate what I am able, lest I fail to tell the story of this "second Abraham."

<margin>C</margin>
This man’s holiness occurred in our own times, and he lived a life on earth that was angelic and heavenly. He possessed a patience like <term>adamant</term> <note>original: ἀδάμας (adamas) — a legendary stone of impenetrable hardness, representing unshakable faith.</note>, and he was deemed worthy of heavenly grace. Since his youth, he purified himself to become a temple of the Holy Spirit, and he prepared himself as a holy vessel, so that the God who called him might dwell within him.

<margin>D</margin>
This blessed man had parents who were very wealthy; they loved him beyond the measure of human nature. They betrothed him to a girl from the time he was a child, expecting to lead him into a position of high rank. But he did not wish for this; rather, from a young age...

---

### ENGLISH TRANSLATION OF THE LATIN TEXT

<margin>A</margin>
**M**y brothers, I wish to tell you the beautiful and perfect account of the life of an admirable man: which he himself so began and perfected that he earned great glory. Furthermore, I

<margin>B</margin>
fear to weave the admirable and illustrious testimony of the image of his virtue, which was acceptable to God. For look, the life and conduct of the man is indeed good and perfect, but I am weak and unskilled. The image of his virtue is brilliant and wonderful, but the "colors" <note>A metaphor for the author's rhetorical skills compared to the "painting" of the saint's life.</note> are sad and dreadful. Nevertheless, although I am feeble and unskilled in speech, and unable to worthily comprehend his perfection, I will still attempt to relate it in part, according to my ability, and to discourse,

<margin>C</margin>
albeit insufficiently, regarding this "other Abraham" who existed in our times. He led an angelic and heavenly life on earth, and possessed a patience like that of adamant, and deserved to attain heavenly grace. And because from his youth he showed himself to be chaste and modest, he was made a temple of the Holy Spirit and a holy vessel, so that God, who had called him, might keep His home and habitation within him.

<margin>D</margin>
This blessed man, therefore, had parents who were quite rich; since they loved him beyond the measure of human nature, they betrothed a girl to him while he was still a boy, expecting that he would be promoted to some high dignity. But he, feeling far differently, soon from his very youthful age...

<summary>This page introduces the biography of St. Abraham, a wealthy young man who rejects his family's plans for marriage and status to pursue a life of extreme asceticism and "angelic" virtue. The author uses a painting metaphor, humbly worrying that his own poor writing ("colors") will fail to do justice to the saint's brilliant "image."</summary>
<keywords>St. Ephrem the Syrian, St. Abraham of Qiduna, hagiography, asceticism, monasticism, "Second Abraham," arranged marriage, Holy Spirit</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of St. Ephrem the Syrian, transitioning from his early upbringing to his radical decision to embrace a monastic life. It details his flight from a forced marriage and his initial years of seclusion.</meta>

-># ST. EPHREM THE SYRIAN<-

# ST. EPHREM THE SYRIAN

<margin>A</margin> From his youth, he began to frequent the churches: and whenever he heard the sacred Scriptures being read, he would gladly lend his ears, and studiously practice and meditate upon them. However, his parents were compelling him to be bound by the bonds of marriage: although he had resisted them long and hard, at last, overcome by a sense of modesty <note>original: "pudoris verecundia"; a feeling of respect or shame toward his parents' wishes</note>, he was forced to acquiesce and give his consent.

<margin>B</margin> When the wedding had been celebrated for seven days <note>Ancient weddings often lasted a full week</note>, and while he was already sitting in the bridal chamber <note>original: "thalamo"</note> with his bride, suddenly, like a flash of light, divine grace shone in his heart. Having found and followed this grace as a guide, he immediately leaped out of the house and departed from the city. Two miles <note>original: "milliaribus"; Roman miles are slightly shorter than modern miles</note> away, he found a certain empty cell, which he entered and lived in with great joy, glorifying God with a happy heart.

<margin>C</margin> Both his parents and his relatives and neighbors were seized with great amazement at his deed; they went out and sought that blessed man everywhere. When they found him after seventeen days in that cell, pouring out prayers to God, they were utterly stunned at the sight of him. But the blessed man said to them: "Why do you wonder? Rather, praise the most kind God, who snatched me from the filth <note>original: "coeno"</note> of my iniquities; and pray for me, so that I may be able to endure until the end of my life this yoke which the Lord willed me, though unworthy, to undergo, and that I may establish my life in a way well-pleasing to His will."

<margin>D</margin> When they had answered him, "Amen," he urged and entreated them not to trouble him too often under the pretext of visiting. Then he blocked up the door of the cell and shut himself inside, leaving only a very small window through which he might receive food. His mind was illuminated by divine grace, by which he made progress day by day in the best way of life; he possessed great self-control <note>original: "continentiam"</note> and devoted his time to vigils, weeping, humility, and charity.

<margin>E</margin> As the fame of him spread everywhere, all who heard of it flocked from every side to see him and to be helped by him. For the word of wisdom and understanding had been given to him by God: and his reputation and fame among his parents <note>The Greek text implies his fame was a "shining light" alongside the memory of his parents</note> shone like a most brilliant light.

<margin>F</margin> It happened ten years after the time of his renunciation that his parents departed from this life, leaving him a great amount of gold and resources. But he asked a certain faithful friend to distribute everything that had fallen to him among the poor and orphans, so that he would not be called away or hindered from his prayers by these things. When this was done, he remained with a tranquil mind and a soul free from cares. For it was the study of the blessed man to keep his mind unencumbered by any earthly things: and therefore he possessed nothing on earth, except for a single cloak <note>original: "sagum"; a coarse woolen cloak worn by the poor or monks</note>,

one <note>catchword: unum</note>

---

<margin>A</margin> He devoted himself to the church, and gladly heard the divine Scriptures and cheerfully meditated upon them. But his parents forced him to enter into marriage. He himself did not wish it; but being frequently troubled by them, he was led into it out of great modesty.

<margin>B</margin> And when the wedding had taken place, on the seventh day, as they had set him in the bridal chamber <note>original: "παστῷ" (pastos)</note> with the bride, suddenly, like a light, grace shone into his heart. Leaping up, he went out of the house. That light became a kind of guide for him. Following it, he went out of the city, and two miles away he found a vacant cell in a field; entering it, he dwelt there with great joy and gladness of heart, glorifying God.

<margin>C</margin> Amazement seized his parents and their relatives regarding what had happened. And going out everywhere, they sought the blessed man. After seventeen days, they found him in the cell praying to God, and seeing him, they wondered. But the blessed man said to them: "Why do you wonder? Glorify God instead, the Lover of Mankind, who rescued me from the mire <note>original: "βορβόρου" (borborou)</note> of my lawlessness, and pray for me, so that until the end I may bear the yoke which the Lord deemed me, the unworthy one, fit to undergo, living according to what is well-pleasing to Him, and that He may work His will in me."

<margin>D</margin> They answered and said, "Amen." And he urged them not to disturb him continually. And having blocked up the door, he closed himself in, leaving only a small window through which he received nourishment. His mind was enlightened by grace, and he was progressing in his most excellent way of life; he acquired great self-control, as well as wakefulness and weeping, humility and love.

<margin>E</margin> Fame ran everywhere concerning him, and all who heard came to him to behold him and to be benefited. For a word of wisdom and understanding was given to him, and the report and fame concerning him was like a shining light <note>original: "φωστήρ" (phoster); a heavenly body or luminary</note> among his kindred.

<margin>F</margin> His parents died ten years after his renunciation, leaving him much money and gold. But he urged a certain true friend of his to distribute these to the poor and orphans so that he would not be hindered from prayer. And having done this, he remained free from care. For this was the zeal of the blessed man: that his mind should not be bound by any earthly thing, and because of this he possessed nothing on earth except one cloak <note>original: "σαγὶν" (sagin)</note> and

and <note>catchword: καὶ</note>

<summary>This page narrates St. Ephrem's dramatic conversion, where he fled his own wedding feast following a divine vision to live as a hermit. He successfully resisted family pressure, eventually giving away a large inheritance to maintain his life of prayer and extreme poverty.</summary>
<keywords>St. Ephrem the Syrian, monasticism, asceticism, bridal chamber, divine light, renunciation of wealth, hermit cell, Syrian Christianity</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Saint Abraham Kidunaia (Abraamius), focusing on his extreme asceticism and the Bishop's decision to send him on a mission to a pagan village.</meta>

# ON THE LIFE OF THE BLESSED ABRAHAM

<margin>A</margin> He possessed only one hair shirt <note>original: *cilicinam*, a garment made of coarse animal hair worn as a form of penance</note> which he wore, and a single small bowl from which he was accustomed to eat and drink. Along with these few things, he possessed a humility beyond measure and a love that honored all people equally. He did not favor the rich over the poor, nor a ruler over a subject; instead, he honored everyone alike without partiality. He never rebuked anyone harshly, but his speech was "seasoned with salt" <note>A biblical metaphor (Colossians 4:6) meaning wise and wholesome speech</note> through love and...

<margin>B</margin> ...gentleness. For who, hearing his beautiful replies, could ever be sated by the sweetness of his words? Or who, looking upon his venerable and angelic face, was not moved to see him again and again? Throughout the entire time of his religious practice, he never changed his rule of life, practicing for fifty years with all eagerness. Because of his infinite devotion and the love he held for Christ, that entire span of time seemed to him like only a few days, and his way of life never felt like a burden to him.

<margin>C</margin> Now, not far from the city, there was a very large and wide village in which everyone, from the smallest to the greatest, were pagans <note>The Greek text uses *Hellenes* (Greeks), a common term in the early church for non-Christians or heathens; the Latin uses *pagani*</note>. No one had the power to convert them, even though many Priests <term>Presbyters|from the Greek 'presbyteros', meaning an elder or priest in the early church</term> and Deacons had been appointed there by the local Bishop. Yet, no one was able to turn them away from their madness for idols; everyone who tried returned without success, unable to endure the misery and persecution the villagers brought upon them.

<margin>D</margin> Even crowds of monks had attempted to reach them once or twice, but they achieved nothing toward their conversion. One day, while the Bishop was sitting with his clergy, he remembered this blessed man and said to them: "In all my years, I have never known a man so perfected,

<margin>E</margin> adorned in every good work and in all the virtues that God loves, as the Master Abraham." The clergy replied, affirming that he was truly a servant of Christ and a perfect ascetic. The Bishop then said to them, "I wish to ordain him as a Priest for that village of pagans. Indeed, through his patience and love, he will be able to turn them back to God."

<margin>F</margin> Rising up immediately, the Bishop went with his clergy to Abraham's cell. When they arrived and had greeted him, the Bishop began to talk to him about the village, urging and pleading with him to go there. When Abraham heard this, he became deeply grieved and said to the Bishop: "Forgive me, Father, I beg of you; allow me instead to stay here and weep for my sins."

<summary>This page describes Saint Abraham's fifty years of humble ascetic life and his angelic reputation, which leads the local Bishop to choose him for a difficult mission: converting a notoriously stubborn pagan village that had driven away all previous missionaries.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Abraham Kidunaia, asceticism, humility, pagan conversion, missionary work, early church hierarchy, hair shirt, monasticism</keywords>
