<meta>This page continues the life of Saint Abraham (Abraham of Kidunja), following his arrival in a pagan village to build a church. The text is presented in parallel columns of Greek and Latin; this translation synthesizes both into a single English narrative to ensure all nuances from both versions are captured.</meta>

# THE LIFE OF THE BLESSED ABRAHAM

---

<margin>A</margin>
He himself, having returned, went back to his own place. And going into the church, with weeping and mourning he entreated God on their behalf so that they might be saved. When morning came, the villagers <note>The Latin text specifically uses "pagani" (pagans), referring to the non-Christian inhabitants of the region.</note> arrived and found him praying; they were struck with such amazement that they stood speechless. From then on, they began coming to the church every day—not for the sake of prayer, but to gaze upon the beauty of the building and its fine ornamentation. Seeing them, he began to urge them to recognize the true God.

---

<margin>B</margin>
But they, taking up sticks, beat him as if he were a lifeless stone. Thinking he was dead, they left him there nearly dead and withdrew. At midnight, he regained consciousness. Groaning, he wept bitterly and said: "Why, O Master, have you overlooked my humble state? Why do you turn your face away from me? Why do you push away my soul and ignore the works of your hands?"

---

<margin>C</margin>
"And now, O Master who loves mankind, look upon your servants and grant them the knowledge of you, for there is no God besides you." After this prayer, he rose and went back into the village. Entering the church, he stood inside singing psalms.

---

<margin>D</margin>
When day broke, the people arrived and saw him there again. Struck with wonder that turned into a violent madness, these cruel and heartless men—devoid of any mercy—tortured him mercilessly. Binding him again with ropes, they dragged him outside the village just as they had done before. He suffered these things for a period of three years, enduring like a diamond <note>original: "ὡς ἀδάμας" (Greek) / "adamantis instar" (Latin). A common hagiographic metaphor for a saint whose resolve cannot be broken by physical pain.</note> through these great tribulations and hardships. He remained steadfast while being beaten, insulted, dragged about, and oppressed by hunger and thirst.

---

<margin>E</margin>
Yet, despite everything that happened to him, he never turned away from them nor became indignant. Instead, through his endurance, his love for them grew even more. Like a man extinguishing a wildfire, he quenched their anger with his gentleness. He encouraged them, admonished them, and spoke with kind words. He entreated the elders as if they were his fathers, the younger men as brothers, and the children as his own offspring—even while they mocked and sneered at him.

---

<margin>F</margin>
One day, all the inhabitants of the village, from the smallest to the greatest, gathered together. Marveling at him, they began to say to one another: "Look at this man's patience and his unspeakable affection <term>affection: "φιλοστοργία" (philostorgia), a specific Greek term for deep, natural, family-like love</term> toward us. Even after the many tribulations and evils we have inflicted upon him, he has not left this place, nor has he spoken an evil word to any of us, nor has he turned his back on us..."

<summary>This page recounts the extreme physical persecution Saint Abraham endured for three years while attempting to convert a village, highlighting his "diamond-like" endurance and refusal to respond with anger.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Abraham, Kidunja, persecution, hagiography, patience, church building, spiritual endurance, philostorgia</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the life of Saint Abraham of Edessa (c. 4th century). On the previous page, Abraham likely began his ministry in a stubborn pagan village. This page describes the success of his teaching, his sudden departure to maintain his hermit-like devotion, and the community's distress at losing their "shepherd."</meta>

# ON THE LIFE OF THE BLESSED ABRAHAM

### Greek Version
<page-num>7</page-num>

<sig>A</sig>
The people, for their part, received his words with great eagerness, listening joyfully to his teaching. To them, he was like an angel of God. Just as a building's foundation is made secure and beautiful by its structural bonds, so too was every soul bound to him by love and longing. Their minds were enlightened by the comfort of the faith and his instruction.

<sig>B</sig>
The blessed man lived among them for an entire year after they came to believe, teaching them the word of God without ceasing, both day and night. Eventually, witnessing their piety toward God, the warmth of their faith, and the love, diligence, honor, and glory they showed him, he became afraid. He feared that he might be forced by their devotion to relax his <term>rule of asceticism</term> <note>original: "kanona tes askeseos" — the strict set of spiritual disciplines, like fasting and solitary prayer, that a hermit follows</note> and that his mind might somehow become entangled in the worries of earthly affairs.

<sig>C</sig>
Rising in the night, he prayed to God, saying: "O only sinless one, only holy one, who rests among the saints; O only lover of mankind and compassionate Master, you who called this your people out of darkness and established them in the marvelous light of your knowledge. O Master, you who released them from the bonds of the adversary <note>referring to the Devil</note> and turned them back from error, giving them faith in you: preserve them until the end,

<sig>D</sig>
O Lord, and help this flock of yours, which you have acquired through your love for mankind. Wall them about with your all-powerful grace and enlighten their hearts forever, so that by fulfilling what is pleasing to you, they may be found worthy of eternal life. And help me, your weak servant; let not this <note>original: "phragma" — literally a fence or barrier, here referring to his sudden separation from the people</note> be counted as a judgment against us, for I long for you and I pursue you."

<sig>E</sig>
Having finished his prayer, he went forth and blessed the village with the sign of the cross three times, then withdrew secretly to another place.

<sig>F</sig>
When morning came, according to their custom, the people came and searched for him. When they did not find him, they were stunned. Like wandering sheep, they went about looking for their shepherd, calling his name with fear and weeping. When they had searched everywhere and could not find him, they were deeply grieved. They went immediately to the Bishop and reported what had happened. When the Bishop heard this, he also became very sorrowful. He quickly sent out many people to search for the blessed man, especially moved by the tears and the pleading of Abraham’s flock. He was sought everywhere like a precious stone, and...

### Latin Version

<sig>A</sig>
They received his words with great alacrity, most willingly hearing his doctrine, and from it they brought forth the most abundant fruit. In their sight, he seemed like an Angel of God, and like the binding of a firm and beautiful building. Indeed, every soul was bound to him by the link of charity and love, and their minds were illuminated by the consolation of faith and his teaching.

<sig>B</sig>
Furthermore, the blessed man spent a whole year among them after they had received the faith, teaching them the word of God without ceasing, day and night. But when he perceived their zeal toward God and the firmness of their faith—as well as their great love, care, honor, and reverence toward himself—he feared that because of them he would be compelled to relax and dissolve the discipline of his religious life, and that in some way his mind would be bound by the cares of earthly things.

<sig>C</sig>
Rising at night, he prayed to God thus: "You who alone are without sin, who alone are holy, who rest in the saints, who alone are the clement and benign Lord: you who called back this people from the darkness and established them, O Lord, in the admirable light of your knowledge. You who released them from the bonds of the adversary and converted them from error, granting them faith in you: preserve them unto the end,

<sig>D</sig>
O Lord, and succur this your flock, which you have possessed by your benignity. Fortify them with your omnipotent grace, and always enlighten their hearts, so that by performing those things which are pleasing and acceptable to you, they may merit to attain eternal life. And to me also, a weak man, impart your help; and let not this matter be reckoned against me in judgment, because I desire you and seek to follow you."

<sig>E</sig>
And having completed his prayer, he departed, signing the village three times with the sign of the cross, and retired secretly to another place.

<sig>F</sig>
When morning came, they gathered as was their custom and sought him. But because they did not find him, they were struck with amazement. Wherefore, like wandering sheep, they went to various places seeking their shepherd, calling out his name with great fear and lamentation. When they could not find him despite searching everywhere, they were exceedingly saddened and went straight to the Bishop to announce what had happened to them. When he heard this, he too was cast down with sorrow and immediately sent many out with diligence to seek the holy man, especially on account of the tears and the consolation of his flock. And while he was sought everywhere like some precious stone, and...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Saint Abraham’s mission to a village he converted. # SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN

| English (from Latin) | English (from Greek) |
|:--- |:--- |
| Since he could not be found, and those who had been sent to seek him out had already returned with their task unaccomplished; the Bishop went to the village with the entire Clergy, and delivered a comforting sermon of life to them. From among them, he appointed **Priests**, **Deacons**, and **Readers**: <note>original: "Lectores/anagnostai" — a minor clerical rank responsible for reading scriptures during liturgy.</note> for they were all now strengthened in the faith and love of Christ. When the blessed man heard of the growth and progress of the Clergy, and of their condition, he rejoiced greatly; and glorifying God, he said: "What shall I return to you, most kind Lord, for all that you have given to me? <note>A reference to Psalm 116:12.</note> I adore and glorify your saving **dispensation**." <note>original: "dispensatio/oikonomia" — God's plan for the salvation of humanity.</note> And so, praying and exulting, he returned to his former cell. | <margin>A</margin> Since he was not found, and those sent to find him had returned without success, the bishop, coming to the village with all the clergy, encouraged them through the word of life, and from among them he appointed priests, deacons, and readers. For they were all recognized in the faith and love of Christ. But when the blessed man heard of the introduction of the clergy and of their establishment, <margin>B</margin> he rejoiced greatly, and glorifying God, said: "What shall I give back to you, my good Master, for all that you have given back to me? I worship and glorify your saving plan;" and having prayed thus and rejoicing, he arrived at his former cell. |
| Moreover, he built another small cell on the outside, and with great joy and gladness of heart, he shut himself in the inner one, blocking up the doors. When they realized this, the inhabitants of that village came to the blessed Abraham, rejoicing with great joy that they had obtained him as a true guide of life. They approached him as if to a father, to be taught and enlightened; and because they were edified beyond words by his way of life, they considered it the greatest grace if they could at least see him and hear the words of salvation from him. O miracle, beloved! Truly this man is full of praise and glory: for in such great tribulations as he endured in that village, he never broke the rule of his religious life, nor did he turn from it to the right or to the left. Glory and magnificence be to the Lord our God, who granted him such patience. | He made a small cell outside the first one, and shut himself into the inner one <margin>C</margin> with great joy and gladness of his heart, blocking up the door. When those of the village heard of it, they came to the blessed Abraham and rejoiced with great joy, having found him to be truly a guide of life. They came to him as to a father to be taught and enlightened, and they acknowledged the greatest thanks for his beyond-words **ascetic conduct** <note>original: "politeia" — a monk's way of life or spiritual citizenship.</note> in seeing him and hearing words of salvation from him. O, the wonder <margin>D</margin> of it, beloved! This man is truly full of praise and glory, for in such great afflictions as he had in the village, he did not change the rule of his asceticism, nor did he turn from it to the right or to the left. Glory and majesty to the Lord our God, who gave him such endurance. |
| Therefore Satan, who from the beginning has been the enemy of the virtue and salvation of men, saw that in such great afflictions—which he had stirred up against him in that village—he had not been able to harm him, nor to draw him toward a release of his spirit, nor to lead his mind away from his purpose. Instead, just like gold in a furnace, he shone all the more, and progressed further as an example to many in patience, and in love and eagerness for God. The enemy of virtue, being vehemently embittered against the blessed Abraham, came to him with a great **apparition**, <note>original: "phantasmate" — a deceptive vision or ghostly appearance.</note> so that by striking terror into him, he might be able to deceive him. Thus, while he was standing and singing psalms in the middle of the night, suddenly a light shone brighter than the sun, and a voice like that of a multitude was heard, saying: "Blessed are you, lord Abraham; truly, I say, you are blessed: because no one among all has been found in righteous deeds as you have; no one..." | Therefore, when Satan saw—he who from the beginning is the <margin>E</margin> **Hater of Good** <note>original: "misokalos" — a common Greek title for the Devil, literally "one who hates the beautiful/good."</note> and the hater of mankind—that in such great afflictions which he raised against him in the village, he was not strong enough to drive him away, nor to cast him into laziness, nor to turn his mind away from his goal; but that like gold in a melting pot he was exceedingly more brightened and glorified, and progressed as a model of salvation for many in endurance and great love and <margin>F</margin> eagerness toward God; the Hater of Good was greatly embittered against the blessed Abraham. And he arrived with a great apparition to him, intending to deceive him by terrifying him. As he stood singing psalms at midnight, suddenly a light shone purer than the sun, and a voice like a multitude said: "Blessed are you, Lord Abraham, truly you are blessed, for no one has been found like you in all..." |

<summary>Following the conversion of the village, Saint Abraham returns to his cell to live as a recluse, but his fame brings the villagers to him for guidance. Satan, frustrated by Abraham's virtue, attempts to deceive him with a supernatural light and flattering voices at midnight.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Abraham of Edessa, asceticism, monastic cell, ordination of clergy, spiritual warfare, demonic deception, hater of good, light of the sun</keywords>

| "...your achievements, and in all things he has done all my wills just as you have; for this reason, you are blessed." **A** But the blessed man immediately recognized the deceit of the evil one, and raising his voice, he said: "May your darkness be with you unto your destruction! For you are full of deceit and fraud. I am but a sinful man; yet, having the grace of my God, and my hope and help in Him, I do not fear you, nor do your many apparitions terrify me. **B** For a fortified wall to me is the name of my Lord and Savior Jesus Christ, whom I have loved; and in His name, I rebuke you, unclean and thrice-miserable <note>original: "τρισάθλιε" — a common intensive Greek epithet meaning extremely wretched or pitiable.</note> dog!" And as he said these things, immediately, like smoke, the figure became invisible. | **A** "...in this way he has fulfilled all my desires, just as you have; and therefore you are blessed." But suddenly the blessed man, recognizing the trickery of the malignant one, said with a loud voice: "May your darkness be with you to your ruin: for you are full of trickery and deceit; I am truly a sinful man. However, because I have the love of my God, and hope in Him, and His help, I do not fear you in the least, nor do your many apparitions and ghosts strike terror into me. **B** For indeed, the name of my Lord and Savior Jesus Christ, whom I have loved, is like a most fortified wall to me; in that name I rebuke you, most foul and most wretched dog." While he was saying these things, the figure vanished from his sight instantly like smoke. |
| But the blessed man, with much eagerness and a calm spirit, blessed God as if he had seen no apparition at all. **C** Again, after a few days, when the blessed night was passing, Satan, holding an axe <note>original: "ἀξίνην" — can refer to an axe or a pickax.</note>, began to destroy his cell. Appearing to have pierced through it, he cried out with a great voice, saying: "Hurry, my friends, hurry! Enter quickly and suffocate him!" But the blessed man said to him: "All the nations surrounded me, and in the name of the Lord I warded them off." <note>A quotation from Psalm 118:10.</note> Immediately the figure vanished, and the cell was completely intact. | The blessed man, with much alacrity and a tranquil mind, gave thanks to God, as one who had seen no ghost at all. **C** Truly, after a few more days, while he was praying at night, Satan, holding an axe, began to overturn his cell; and when he seemed to have already pierced it, he cried out with a loud voice, saying: "Hurry quickly, O my friends, hurry, and entering swiftly, suffocate him." But the blessed man said against him: <margin>Psalm 118.</margin> *All the nations surrounded me, and in the name of the Lord I have been avenged upon them.* He then vanished instantly, while the cell remained whole and unharmed. |
| **D** And again, after a few more days, while he was chanting <term>psalms</term><note>The practice of "psalmodies," or singing the Biblical Psalms as a form of prayer.</note> at midnight, he saw his mat <note>original: "ψιάθιον" — a woven reed mat used by monks for standing and sleeping.</note> upon which he stood to chant, burning with a most violent flame. Stepping upon the flame, he said: "I will tread upon the asp and the basilisk, and I will trample the lion and the dragon, and over all the power of the enemy through the name of our Lord Jesus Christ who helps me." **E** Burning with rage, Satan cried out and said: "I will defeat you, you wicked man, looking for arts to counter your contempt of me." | **D** And again, after a few other days had passed, while he was occupied with the Psalmody in the middle of the night, seeing the mat on which he stood while chanting burning with a vehement flame, he trampled the fire, saying: <margin>Psalm 91.</margin> *I will walk upon the asp and the basilisk, and I will trample the lion and the dragon*; and I shall overcome all the power of the enemy in the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, who assists me. Fleeing away, Satan cried out and said: **E** "I will conquer you, wicked one, and I will find arts by which I may crush you, who now hold me in contempt." |
| On one of those days, as the blessed man was taking food according to his custom, the enemy entered his cell in the form and habit of a young man and approached to overturn his bowl <note>original: "καυκίον" — a small vessel or drinking cup.</note>. But he, recognizing the trick, held it fast and ate, not caring for the deceit. **F** Jumping up, the figure stood by him, and placing a lampstand with a lamp burning upon it, he began to chant in a great voice, saying: "Blessed are the undefiled in the way, who walk in the law of the Lord." When he had said many words from the psalm, Abraham did not answer him until he had finished eating. After eating, he sealed himself with the sign of the cross and said to him: "Unclean and thrice-miserable dog, depart, you coward! If you know that those are blessed..." | On a certain day, therefore, when the blessed man was taking food as was his custom, the enemy entered his cell in the form and clothing of a youth, and coming closer, he strove to overturn his dish. But he, recognizing him, held it with his hand, and caring nothing for his snares, continued to eat. **F** And when the figure had leaped up, he approached him, and setting a lampstand before him and placing a burning candle upon it, he began to chant with a loud voice, saying: <margin>Psalm 119.</margin> *Blessed are the undefiled in the way, who walk in the law of the Lord.* And when he had chanted many words from that Psalm, Abraham answered him nothing until he had taken his food. When he had now eaten, fortifying himself with the sign of the cross, he said to him: "Unclean dog, and thrice-pitiable, most foolish and most timid; if you know that..." |

<meta>This page continues the hagiographical account of Saint Ephraim the Syrian’s spiritual combat. <page-num>10</page-num>
### SAINT EPHRAEM THE SYRIAN ###

<margin>A</margin> "If they are blessed, why then do you trouble and disturb them? For truly all those who love God with their whole heart are blessed." Answering him, the Devil said: "I bring troubles upon them for this reason: so that I may overcome them and hinder them from every good work." But the blessed man said to him: "May it not go well for you, you accursed and spoken-against creature, that you should have the power to hinder anyone who fears God—unless perhaps those who are like you, and who by their own will depart from God. These you easily conquer and deceive because God is not within them. <margin>B</margin> But from those who love God, you fail and vanish like smoke caught by the wind. Indeed, a single prayer of theirs, mixed with tears, pursues you just as dust is scattered when driven by a whirlwind. My God lives, who is blessed forever, He is my glory; therefore I do not fear you, even if you stood here for your whole life, and I count you as nothing, you impure dog. Indeed, I despise you just as a crushed little puppy would be scorned by anyone." <margin>C</margin> When the blessed man said these things, the demon suddenly vanished.

Again, after some days, when he had completed his Psalmody <note>The practice of chanting or reciting the Book of Psalms as a form of daily liturgical prayer</note>, the enemy came to him with a phantasm <term>phantasm (a deceptive vision or supernatural illusion created by demons)</term> of a great multitude of men, who seemed to be pulling at his cell <note>A small, simple hut or room used by a monk</note> with ropes they had cast around it, crying out to one another, "Throw him into the pit!" <margin>D</margin> Seeing them, the blessed man said: <margin>Psalm 118</margin> *“They surrounded me like bees to a honeycomb, and they flared up like fire among thorns; and in the name of the Lord I have been avenged upon them.”* <note>Original: "Circumdederunt me, sicut apes..." This is Psalm 118:12 (or 117 in the Septuagint/Vulgate numbering).</note> Then Satan, crying out, said: "Woe is me, woe is me! I do not know what I should do to you next. For behold, you have conquered and overcome me in everything, and having despised all my strengths, you have trampled me underfoot. Nevertheless, I will not depart from you even so, until I have overcome you and made you humble and subject to me." <margin>E</margin> The blessed man said to him: "Anathema <term>Anathema (a formal curse or ban, often used to repel evil spirits)</term> be to you and to all your powers, you most wicked and impure creature! Glory and adoration be to our Lord, the only holy God, who gave you over to be trampled by us, his lovers. Therefore realize, you most unhappy and weak creature, that we fear neither you nor your illusions."

<margin>F</margin> For a long time, though he had attacked the blessed man with various temptations, imaginings, and engines of madness, he was by no means able to strike any fear into his mind or thoughts; rather, it excited the man to greater eagerness and love toward God. For since he loved God with his whole heart and soul, and established his life and conduct according to His will, he abundantly merited divine grace; and therefore the Devil had no power to harm him in anything. For he had knocked patiently...

---

<margin>A</margin> "Why do you even bother them? For truly all those who love God from their whole heart are blessed." And the Devil, answering, said to him: "So that I may conquer them, I hinder them from every good work." But the blessed man said to him: "May it not be good for you, accursed one, that you should hinder any of those who fear God; you conquer those like yourself who have fallen away from God by their own choice. These you conquer and lead astray because God is not in them. <margin>B</margin> But from those who love God, you vanish just like smoke from the wind. For one prayer of theirs, with tears, pursues you just as chaff is pursued by a storm. My God lives, who is blessed forever, who is my boast; I do not fear you, even if you stand here your whole time, nor do I pay any attention to you, unclean dog. I truly despise you as if one were despising a crushed little dog." <margin>C</margin> When the blessed man said these things, that one immediately became invisible.

And again after some days, as he was finishing his psalmody, the enemy arrived with a phantasm of a crowd, and they seemed to be throwing ropes into the cell, and dragging it they cried out to one another, saying: "Throw him into the pit." <margin>D</margin> Looking around at them, the blessed man said: "They surrounded me like bees to a honeycomb, and they flared up like fire in thorns, and in the name of the Lord I defended myself against them." And Satan, crying out, said: "Woe is me, woe is me, I do not know what I shall do to you. For behold, you have conquered me in all things, and you have despised all my power, you have trampled me. But even so, I will certainly not depart until I have conquered and humbled you." <margin>E</margin> But the blessed man said to him, "Anathema to you and to all your power, most foul one! Glory and worship to our Master, the only holy God, who made you to be trampled by us who love Him. Know therefore, miserable and weakest one, that we do not fear you, nor your phantasms."

<margin>F</margin> Having warred against the blessed man for a time with various temptations and phantasms and madnesses, he was not able to cast his mind into even a moment of cowardice, but rather it stirred him up to readiness and love toward God. For having loved Him from his whole soul, he struggled <note>original: "ἠγωνίσατο" - to struggle or compete, as an athlete does in a contest</note> to live his life according to His will, and he was made worthy of His grace. Because of this, the Devil did not have the strength to wrong him. And he knocked with endurance...

Ephraim triumphs through his unwavering love for God and by reciting the Psalms, specifically Psalm 118.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the hagiography (biography of a saint) of St. Abraham of Edessa, specifically focusing on his extraordinary ascetic virtues and physical resilience. The text is presented in parallel Greek and Latin columns, both of which are translated here into a single English narrative.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>ON THE LIFE OF THE BLESSED ABRAHAM.</header>

## ON THE LIFE OF THE BLESSED ABRAHAM.

---

<margin>A</margin> ...so that the treasure of God’s grace might be opened to him. When the entrance to it was opened for him, he went in and chose three precious stones: **Faith, Hope, and Love** <note>original: Πίστιν, Ἐλπίδα, Ἀγάπην / fides, spes, caritas</note>. With these he adorned his other virtues, and weaving a most precious crown, he offered it to Christ, the King of Kings. For who else loved God so much from their whole heart, and their neighbor as themselves? Or who was so sympathetic and showed such depths of mercy to those in labor?

<margin>B</margin> Which monk, hearing of his good conduct, did he not pray for—begging that he might be kept safe from the snares of the Devil and finish the course of his life blamelessly? Or which sinner or ungodly person, upon hearing of them, did he not immediately beg God with tears for their salvation? Throughout the entire time of his religious life, he never turned aside from his rule of discipline; in all those years, not a day passed him by without tears.

<margin>C</margin> He did not allow his lips to break into laughter, nor even into a smile. He never applied oil to his body, nor did he ever wash his face or his feet with water. He lived as if he were dying daily in his purposeful struggle. Oh, what a wondrous miracle! Despite his amazing self-control, his constant vigils, his floods of tears, his practice of sleeping on the ground <term>humicubatio: the ascetic practice of sleeping on the bare earth</term>, and the humbling of his body, he—

<margin>D</margin> —never grew faint or weak, nor was he ever exhausted by sluggishness or weariness. Instead, like someone hungry or thirsty, his mind—nourished by the power of divine grace—could never be satisfied with the sweetness of his ascetic labor. His appearance was like a blooming rose, and his body did not look exhausted from such hardship; rather, he remained healthy and strong, as if sustained by the grace of God.

<margin>E</margin> Even at the hour of his falling asleep <note>original: κοιμήσεως / obdormitionis; a common Christian euphemism for death</note>, his face appeared so bright that we recognized his soul was being escorted by a company of angels. There was another wonderful grace of God seen in him: in the fifty years of his ascetic life, the hair garments <term>cilicium: a rough garment made of goat's hair worn against the skin as a penance</term> he wore never wore out. They served him so well until the end of his life that others were even deemed worthy to receive them after they had grown old with him.

<margin>F</margin> I wish, beloved, to tell you of another marvelous thing which he did in his old age. To those who are truly wise and spiritual, it is wonderful and full of benefit and contrition. The matter is this: The blessed man had an only brother; when he died, his daughter was left an orphan.

<summary>This page describes the intense ascetic life of St. Abraham of Edessa, emphasizing his supernatural physical endurance, his constant prayer for others, and his miraculous "hair shirt" that never wore out over fifty years.</summary>
<keywords>St. Abraham of Edessa, asceticism, monasticism, Faith Hope and Charity, hair shirt, miracles, compassion, orphan niece</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of an ascetic's life, likely from a collection of hagiographies (lives of saints) attributed to or compiled by Ephrem the Syrian. This specific section recounts the early life of a young girl—historically identified as Mary, the niece of Abraham of Kidunaya—who is raised in a cell adjacent to her hermit uncle.</meta>

# SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN

<margin>A</margin>
Her acquaintances and friends brought her to her uncle when she was seven years old. Because he lived enclosed in his inner cell, he ordered her to be placed in an outer one. There was a very small window between the two cells through which he taught her the Psalter <note>The Book of Psalms</note> and the rest of the Scriptures. She kept vigil with him in divine praises and sang psalms; just as he practiced self-denial, so did she. Eagerly progressing in the path of piety she had undertaken, she strove to perfect all virtues.

<margin>B</margin>
For the blessed man frequently prayed to God for her, that she might keep her mind fixed on Him and not be entangled in the cares of earthly things, because her father, departing from this life, had left her a great amount of money. The man of God immediately commanded that these funds be distributed to the poor.

<margin>C</margin>
She herself also continually entreated her uncle, saying: "Father, I ask and beseech your holiness <note>original: *sanctitatem tuam* / *τὴν σὴν ἁγιωσύνην*</note> to pray to the Lord for me, that I may be freed from absurd and wicked thoughts, and be rescued from all the Enemy’s schemes and the various snares of the Devil." And so, exercising herself eagerly, she kept the rule of her ascetic <term>ascetic: a lifestyle of rigorous self-discipline and abstinence from worldly pleasures for religious reasons</term> life. The blessed man rejoiced to see her progressing so well in the best way of life, with such eagerness, gentleness, and love toward God.

<margin>D</margin>
For twenty years she lived with him in self-denial, dwelling like a fine lamb and an immaculate dove.

<margin>E</margin>
But when the twenty-year period was complete, that deceitful and cunning serpent <note>original: *ὄφις* (ophis)</note>, seeing her excel in the virtues of the solitary life and contemplating only heavenly things, began to waste away with the fierce fire of envy. He laid traps to ensnare her, so that he might at least strike the blessed man with sorrow and anxiety, and turn his mind away from God even slightly because of the distress felt for her. Just as with our first parents <note>Adam and Eve</note>, the wicked and cunning beast—burning with jealousy—found the serpent as a fit instrument for deception, so that he might cause those who lived in happiness to instead inhabit a thorny and laborious land. So too here, searching for a time, he found for himself a vessel prepared for destruction.

<margin>F</margin>
There was a certain man who was a monk in name only, who used to visit the blessed man quite studiously under the pretext of conversation. But catching sight of that blessed girl through the small window, the wretch, blinded in his mind, desired to be with her. He laid traps for her for a long time, for nearly a year, until at last, having found an opportunity, he rendered her completely estranged from the blessed life of the true paradise.

<summary>This page describes the twenty-year spiritual formation of a young girl under the tutelage of her hermit uncle, ending with the introduction of a deceptive "monk" who intends to lead her astray.</summary>
<keywords>asceticism, cell, Psalter, spiritual warfare, envy of the devil, Mary of Kidunaya, Abraham of Kidunaya, monastic life</keywords>

->### ON THE LIFE OF BLESSED ABRAHAM<-
->13<-

<margin>A</margin> For as if she had been deceived by the serpent, she opened the door of her cell and went out; for by the deceit of the wicked dragon, she fell from the height of her God-loving and pure virginity. Just as the first-formed parents <note>Referring to Adam and Eve.</note> tasted of the fruit and their eyes were opened, and they knew that they were naked; so she also, after committing the sin, was distraught in her mind and fell into despair. She tore her hair-cloth tunic <note>original: "χιτῶνα αὐτῆς τὸν τείχινον" / "cilicinam vestem". A 'cilicium' was a rough garment made of goat's hair worn for penance.</note> 
<margin>B</margin> and struck her face with her hands, wanting to suffocate herself. For she said within herself, wailing: "Now I am dead; I have lost my days, the fruit of my asceticism and self-control, and the labor of my tears. I have provoked God; I have destroyed myself, and I have cast my holy uncle into the most bitter grief, and I have become a laughingstock to the Devil. 
<margin>C</margin> Why do I, a wretched and unhappy woman, still live? Alas, what have I done? Alas, what have I suffered? Alas, from where have I fallen? How was my mind darkened? How was I robbed by the Evil One? How I fell, I did not understand; how I slipped, I did not know; how I was defiled, I do not know. What kind of cloud has covered my heart, so that I cannot consider what I have done? Where shall I hide? Where shall I go? Into what pit shall I throw myself? Where is the advice of 
<margin>D</margin> my holy uncle? Where is the teaching of his companion Ephraem <note>St. Ephrem the Syrian, a famous theologian and friend of Abraham.</note>, when he used to say to me: 'Watch yourself, and keep your soul spotless for your incorruptible and immortal Bridegroom; for your Bridegroom is holy and jealous.' I no longer dare to look up to heaven, for I have died to God and to men; I am no longer able to look steadily toward that window. 
<margin>E</margin> For how shall I, a sinner, attempt to speak again with that holy man? And if I do dare it, will not a fire coming out through the window consume me? It would be better for me to go away instead to a place where there is no one who knows me; since I have died once and for all, and there is no longer any hope of salvation left for me."

Rising up immediately, she went away to another city, and having changed her appearance <note>or "habit," implying she put off her monastic clothing.</note>, 
<margin>F</margin> she established herself in an inn <note>original: "πανδοχείῳ" / "diversorio". In this context, it suggests she has entered a life of prostitution.</note>. While this ruin was happening to the woman, the holy man saw a vision in a dream. He beheld a great dragon, terrible in appearance, hissing with great strength, which came out of its place and went as far as his cell; finding a dove there, it swallowed it and then returned to its own place. Awakening, the blessed man was greatly saddened and wept bitterly, saying: "Could it be that Satan is raising a persecution..."

<meta>This page continues the life of St. Abraham Kidunaia (Abraham of Edessa) as recorded by St. Ephraim the Syrian. The previous page likely concluded a description of a symbolic dream involving a dragon and a dove. The catchwords 'gnus' and 'σιον' refer to the Latin *agnus* (lamb) and the Greek *plesion* (neighbor/near), transitioning into the themes of the lost lamb and the proximity of the enemy.</meta>

# ST. EPHRAEM THE SYRIAN

->Page 14<-

Satan would stir up trouble against the holy church and turn many away from the faith, or lest some schism or heresy should arise in the church of God. Therefore he prayed to God, saying: "O most Kind One, who knows all things before they happen <note>original Latin: *praescius*; Greek: *prognosta*</note>, you alone know what this great vision means." 

<margin>Α</margin> Two days later, he saw the same dragon coming out of its place and entering his cell to him. He saw it place its head under his feet, where it was torn apart; but that dove was found still alive and without injury. Immediately waking from sleep, he called Mary once and then again, saying: "Wake up! Why are you lazy and listless? Today is the second day that you have not opened your mouth to praise God." 

<margin>Β</margin> But when he received no answer from her, and for two whole days no longer heard her singing psalms in her usual way, he understood that the vision he had seen concerned her. 

<margin>Γ</margin> Therefore, sighing, he wept bitterly, saying: "Woe is me! A cruel wolf has snatched away my lamb, and my daughter has been made a captive." And raising his voice, he said: "O Christ, Savior of the world, return your lamb Mary to the fold of life, so that my old age does not descend to the grave <note>original Latin: *ad inferos*; Greek: *eis hadou* (Hades)</note> with sorrow. Do not despise my prayer, Lord: but send out your grace more swiftly, to tear her unharmed from the mouth of the dragon." 

<margin>D</margin> And so those two days in which he saw the vision signified a period of two years in which his niece <note>The Greek *adelphide* specifically denotes a niece. Mary of Edessa had been raised by Abraham in an adjoining cell as an ascetic.</note> lived a life outside. But he did not cease to plead with God for her day and night. After two years had passed, when he learned where she was and how she was conducting herself, he asked a certain friend and acquaintance of his to send someone there to find out everything accurately about her, and to note and observe her location and her life. 

<margin>Ε</margin> When the messenger arrived, he learned everything for certain and reported back how he had seen her, what kind of life she was leading, and pointed out the location. Once Abraham was certain about her and fully informed that it was truly her, he arranged for a military uniform <term>military habit|The *habitum militarem* was a secular disguise used by the monk to enter the city or tavern without being recognized as a man of the cloth.</term> and a horse to be brought to him. Opening the door of his cell, he went out, dressed in the military attire, and placed a tall, deep hat <term>galerum / kamelaukion|A type of cap or cowl; here Abraham uses a deep version to obscure his face and clerical tonsure.</term> on his head to veil his face; taking a single coin with him and mounting the horse, he departed. 

<margin>F</margin> Just as a spy <note>original Latin: *explorator*; Greek: *kataskopos*</note> wishing to scout a city or a country assumes the dress of that place so as not to be easily recognized by the inhabitants, so also the blessed Abraham traveled in a secular disguise <note>original: *habitu alieno* / *schēmati dēmosiō*</note> so that he might put the enemy to flight. Truly, therefore, he is worthy of admiration...

<summary>Abraham interprets a prophetic vision of a dragon as the spiritual fall of his niece Mary, who has fled her ascetic life. After two years of prayer, he discovers she is living in a city and disguises himself as a soldier to go and rescue her.</summary>

### ON THE LIFE OF BLESSED ABRAHAM

This man is worthy to be called a second Abraham. <margin>Genesis 14</margin> **A** For just as the first Abraham went out to the war of the kings and, after striking them down, brought back his nephew Lot; so too this second Abraham went out to war against the Devil, and having conquered him, brought back his niece. After he had arrived at the place and entered the inn to stay, he looked around here and there, striving to see her. Then, after **B** much time had passed and the opportunity to see her had not been given, he said to the innkeeper with a slight smile: "Friend, I have heard that you have a beautiful girl here, whom I would gladly see." The innkeeper, seeing the man’s advanced age and his gray hair, was surprised by him <note>The Latin 'coarguit' suggests the innkeeper might have been judging the old man's interest in a young woman</note>; then he replied, "There is a girl here, and she is beautiful beyond measure." Indeed, this Mary was lovely almost beyond what nature allows.

When the blessed man asked what the girl was called, the innkeeper replied that she was named Mary. Then, with a cheerful face, **C** Abraham said: "I beg you, call her for me, so that I may feast and be merry with her today; for I have been captivated by love for her just from hearing about her." When she was called and arrived, and he saw her in that provocative finery and prostitute’s clothing, his whole body and his entire being almost dissolved into tears. But through **D** wise and strong resolve, he repressed his emotions—as if locking them in a secret sanctuary—lest the woman recognize him and immediately choose to flee. 

As they sat and drank together, the blessed man began to speak with her as if he were a lover burning with an unquenchable fire of love. In this way, the saintly man strongly opposed the Devil, taking back the captive woman to restore her to the bridal chamber of Christ <note>The "bridal chamber of Christ" is a common metaphor in early Christian writing for the soul's return to a state of grace or the monastic life</note>. While he was talking with her, she stood up and, embracing **E** his neck, began to receive him with kisses. But as she was kissing him, she caught the scent of his body—fragrant with the sweet smell of his angelic way of life <term>angelic way of life: a common historical term for the ascetic or monastic life, which was believed to make a person more like an angel</term>—and she was suddenly reminded of her own former life of prayer. She groaned deeply, saying: "Woe to me, miserable and alone!" 

The innkeeper was amazed and said to her: "Lady Mary, you have been here for two years, and I have never heard such sighs or words from you. What has happened to you now?" She replied: "I would be blessed **F** if I had died three years ago." Immediately, the blessed man, so that he would not appear too stern <note>He remains in character as a traveler to avoid scaring her away before he can reveal his identity</note>, said to her: "Are you just now, in my presence, starting to remember your sins?" Did the girl not say in her heart: "How much this man's face resembles the face of my uncle?" Yet, God—who alone is most kind and wise—arranged things so that she would not recognize him yet, fearing she might flee in terror. He then immediately took out a coin—

### Page 18

...placed her head at his feet and wept throughout the night, saying: "What can I give back to you, Lord, for all these things?" When dawn had already broken, the blessed old man said to her: "Rise, daughter, and let us leave this place." She answered him, saying: "I have a little gold here and a few clothes; what do you command me to do with these?" The blessed man said to her: "Leave those things here, for all of them are the portion of the Evil One <note>original: "maligni" (Latin) / "Πονηροῦ" (Greek). Referring to the devil; Abraham insists she abandon the wealth gained through her life as a prostitute.</note>." 

Rising up, they left immediately. He placed her upon a horse and led it, preceding her with joy. Just as a shepherd who finds a lost sheep carries it on his shoulders, so this blessed man traveled with joy in his heart. When they reached his own place, he enclosed her in an inner cell, while he remained in the outer one. There, dressed in sackcloth <note>A coarse garment made of goat hair, worn as a symbol of mourning and penance.</note>, she remained in the greatest humility of spirit and with many tears. She wore herself out with constant vigils and the strictest labors of self-control, eagerly pursuing the goal of repentance and boldly falling down before God in prayer. 

Truly, this was sincere repentance! Truly, it was the healing and restoration of the soul. Through such a struggle, everyone ought to confess and give thanks to God. For who is so stony and hard-hearted that, hearing her tearful and lamenting voice, would not also be moved to praise God? Indeed, our own remorse, compared to her repentance, is merely a shadow and a phantom. With such patience, zeal, and tireless concern, she turned back to God, asking for a sign to be given that her repentance had been accepted. Therefore, the most kind and merciful God granted her the gift of healings <note>A traditional hagiographic motif where a penitent is granted miraculous powers as proof of divine forgiveness.</note> as a certainty that her repentance was accepted.

The blessed man lived for another ten years. Seeing her sincere repentance and remarkable eagerness of spirit, he glorified and magnified God. Thus, in a good old age, the truly holy servant of God went to his rest. He completed the course of his life at seventy years of age. Of those, he spent fifty in the <term>ascetic: a life of rigorous self-discipline and abstinence from worldly pleasures for religious purposes</term> way of life with great eagerness and an admirable struggle. He lived in humility and love, without being a "respecter of persons" <note>An idiom meaning he did not show favoritism or partiality based on social status.</note>, as is common among many who honor one person while despising another. In such a long span of time, he never grew sluggish in his religious practice, nor did he ever change the rule of his excellent life; instead, he was always so prepared as if he were to die daily.

This was the life <note>Latin: "conversatio"; Greek: "ἀγωγή". Refers to one's conduct or spiritual way of life.</note> of the blessed Abraham:

---

<margin>A</margin> ...at her feet, she wept all night, saying: "What shall I give back to you for all these things, Master?" When dawn came, the blessed man said to her: "My child, rise, let us go." She replied to him: "I have a little gold and clothing here, what do you command concerning these?" The blessed man said to her: "Leave them here; for all these <margin>B</margin> are the portion of the Evil One." And rising, they left immediately. He set her upon the horse, and he himself led it before her, rejoicing. And just as a shepherd, when he finds a lost sheep, lifts it upon his shoulders; so the blessed man walked, rejoicing in his heart. And when they came to the place, he shut her into the inner cell, and stayed himself in the outer <margin>C</margin> one. She, with sackcloth, humility, many tears, vigils, and self-control, accurately and eagerly fulfilled the goal of repentance, openly praying and falling down before God. Truly, such is true repentance. Truly, it is the healing and renewal of the soul. Through such a struggle, everyone should confess to God. For who is so stony or hard of heart that, hearing the voice of her weeping, <margin>D</margin> would not be moved to glorify God? For compared to her repentance, ours is but a shadow and a phantom. For with such patience, zeal, and agony, she unceasingly approached God, asking for a sign if her repentance had been accepted. Therefore, the good and man-loving God granted her the gift of healings for the full assurance of her accepted repentance.

<margin>E</margin> The blessed man lived another ten years, seeing her sincere repentance and her exceptional eagerness, and glorifying and magnifying God. Thus, the truly holy servant of God rested in a good old age. He was perfected at seventy years old, having practiced the ascetic life for fifty years. With great eagerness and a wonderful struggle, <margin>F</margin> he lived in humility and love, not favoring any man's person—which is natural for many to do, preferring one and despising another. In so much time of ascetic practice, he never became slothful at all, nor changed the rule of his best life, but was so disposed as if dying every day.

This was the way of life of the blessed Abraham,

### ON THE LIFE OF THE BLESSED ABRAHAM.

<margin>A</margin> This way of life was most pleasing to God, as were his struggles of patience and endurance. Like a gazelle escaping from snares, he departed from the corruptible bridal chamber <note>Abraham famously fled his own wedding feast to live as a hermit, leaving his bride behind to preserve his virginity for God</note>. He never allowed his thoughts or his mind, once firmly established in his holy purpose, to be turned backward. He did not lose heart during the tribulations stirred up against him by the Enemy <note>The Devil</note> in the village, nor was he ever terrified during the constant battle against the illusions of the demons.

<margin>B</margin> He brought his struggle to a triumphant conclusion in the matter of that blessed woman <note>His niece, Mary</note>. Through spiritual wisdom and an inexpressible prudence—which was mistaken for ignorance but was in fact a spiritual labor—he trampled the Dragon <note>Satan</note> under his feet. He snatched his most beloved dove from between the Dragon's teeth and led her back to the true Bridegroom, Jesus Christ. <note>This refers to Abraham disguising himself as a soldier to enter a brothel and rescue Mary, who had fallen into sin after being seduced</note>

<margin>C</margin> These were the struggles and labors of the blessed Abraham. We have recorded them here for the consolation and devotion of those who wish to attain eternal life, and for the praise and glory of God, who abundantly provides all that is necessary and useful to everyone.

<margin>D</margin> We have described his other virtues elsewhere. At the hour when he departed to the Lord, nearly the entire city and the neighboring villages gathered together. Each person eagerly approached his venerable and holy body, snatching a blessing from his garments <note>A common practice in late antiquity where the clothes of a saint were treated as holy relics capable of healing</note>. If any sickness touched a piece of the clothing that had been taken, the person was immediately healed.

<margin>E</margin> That blessed woman <note>Mary</note> lived for another five years, during which she practiced the religious life with such intensity that she never ceased to pray to God with tears, day or night. Indeed, those who frequently passed by that place and heard the sound of her wailing were moved to compassion; they would stop and join their weeping with hers, and lamenting their own sins, they would glorify God. When her final day came, her face appeared radiant with grace to all who saw her, so that we understood a choir of holy angels was present there. We gave glory to God, who through his inexpressible mercy saves those who hope in him, in Christ Jesus our Lord.

<margin>F</margin> Woe is me, my beloved! For while these souls have reached a beautiful end and have been confidently pulled away from earthly things to be bound to the love of God, I remain unprepared and sluggish in my resolve. Behold, the endless winter <note>A metaphor for death or the final judgment</note> has overtaken me, and I am naked and unready—I marvel— <note>The text breaks off here as it moves to the next page</note>

<summary>This page concludes the hagiography of Saint Abraham and his niece Mary, describing their holy deaths, the miracles associated with Abraham's clothing, and Mary's deep penance. It ends with the author’s personal lament over his own spiritual unreadiness compared to the saints.</summary>

| I wonder at myself, how I sin daily, and daily I perform penance. For an hour I build up, and in an hour I overthrow what was constructed. In the evening I say: tomorrow I will repent; but when morning comes, I am seized by <term>spiritual apathy</term> <note>original: "accedia"; a state of listlessness or spiritual "sloth" often discussed by early monks</note>, and with a proud mind, I let the day pass by. Again at midday I say: this coming night I will keep watch, and with tears I will entreat God to be merciful to my sins; but when the night arrives, I am instead filled with sleep. | **A** | I am amazed at my own state, how day by day I sin, and day by day I repent; in one hour I build up, and in another I tear down. In the evening I say, tomorrow I will repent; but when morning arrives, <term>indifference</term> <note>original: "rhathymia"; a Greek term for carelessness or laziness in spiritual duties</note> takes over, and I pass the day in distraction. Again at noon, I say: in the coming night I will be sober, and with tears I will call upon God, so that He might be merciful toward my sins. |
| <margin>Luke 19.</margin> Those who received the money with me <note>A reference to the Parable of the Pounds/Minas in the Gospel of Luke, where a master gives money to his servants to invest</note> strive to do business day and night, so that they may obtain the praise of the proclamation and be placed over ten cities: but I, because of my laziness, have hidden it in the earth. My Lord has drawn near, and behold, my heart trembles, and I weep for the days of my negligence, having no excuses to offer. | **B** | But when night falls, I am carried away by sleep. Those who received the silver with me struggle day and night, trading so that they may rule over ten cities with glory. But I, through my own hesitation, have hidden it in the earth, and my Lord has drawn near to come; and behold, my heart trembles, and I weep for the days of my indifference, having nothing to say to Him in my defense. |
| Have mercy on me, You who alone are without sin: and save me, You who alone are kind and merciful: because besides You, the blessed Father, and Your only-begotten Son, who became incarnate for our sake, and Your Holy Spirit, who gives life to all things, I know no other, nor have I believed in any other. And now remember me, Lord, and lead me out from the prison of my iniquities: for both things are Yours, O Lord—both when You willed me to enter into this world, and when You shall command me to depart from it. | **C** | O only sinless One, have pity; O only Lover of Mankind, save me. For besides You, the blessed Father, and Your only-begotten Son who took flesh for us, and Your Holy Spirit who gives life to all, I know no other, nor have I believed in any other. And now remember me, Master, and lead me out of this prison of my lawlessness. For both are Yours, O Master: the time for me to enter into this age, and the time to depart from it. |
| Remember me, who lacks any defense; and save me, a sinner. May Your grace, which is my help, refuge, and glory in this world; may that grace itself protect me under its wings in 그 day that is dreadful and terrible. | **D** | Remember me, the one who has no excuse, and save me, the sinner. May Your grace, which has become my support and my refuge, my boast and my praise in this age, itself shelter me under its wings in that fearful and frightening day. |
| For You know, You who are the searcher of the heart and the reins <note>An archaic term for the kidneys, historically believed to be the seat of deep emotions and hidden thoughts</note>, that I have turned away from many paths of wickedness and scandals (I mean the crooked paths of heretical dogmas, and the vain explanations made by them). And this was not of myself, but from Your grace, by which my mind was enlightened. | **E** | For You know, You who examine hearts and minds, that I have turned away from many crooked paths and a multitude of stumbling blocks (and by crooked paths I mean the opinions of the heretics and their meddlesome interpretation); and this was not from me, but from Your grace. For it enlightened my understanding. |
| I pray to You, holy Lord, save my soul in Your kingdom, and deem me worthy to bless You with all those who have pleased You: because glory, worship, and magnificence belong to You—the Father, and the Son, and the Holy Spirit—now, and always, and forever and ever. Amen. | **F** | I beseech You, holy Master, save my soul in Your kingdom, and make me worthy to bless You with all those who have been well-pleasing to You; for to You belongs glory, worship, and majesty—to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen. |

<summary>This page contains a bilingual (Latin and Greek) penitential prayer by St. Ephraem the Syrian, focusing on the struggle against spiritual procrastination and the avoidance of heretical teachings. The author uses the Parable of the Pounds to illustrate his guilt and concludes with a Trinitarian doxology.</summary>

# BY THE SAME BLESSED AND MOST HOLY FATHER OF OURS AND UNIVERSAL TEACHER, EPHRAEM THE SYRIAN
<note>original: ΤΟΥ ΑΥΤΟΥ ΜΑΚΑΡΙΟΥ ΚΑΙ ΟΣΙΩΤΑΤΟΥ ΠΑΤΡΟΣ ΗΜΩΝ ΚΑΙ ΟΙΚΟΥΜΕΝΙΚΟΥ ΔΙΔΑΣΚΑΛΟΥ ΕΦΡΑΙΜ ΤΟΥ ΣΥΡΟΥ</note>

---

## A DISCOURSE ON THE ALL-BEAUTIFUL JOSEPH.
<note>In Eastern Christian tradition, Joseph is often called "The All-Beautiful" (Pankalos) not just for his physical appearance, but for his virtuous character and his role as a "type" or foreshadowing of Christ.</note>

<margin>A</margin>
<margin>B</margin>
O God of Abraham, God of Isaac, God of Jacob—blessed God, who chose the holy seed of your servants who loved you: grant to me in your goodness that the streams of your grace may flow within me in great abundance. May I be enabled to describe the bright and magnificent spectacle of the all-beautiful Joseph, who was always a venerable support for the heavy old age of the Patriarch Jacob.

For this boy, from his earliest years, prefigured the two comings of Christ: the first, which took place from the Virgin Mary; and the future coming, when he shall appear again to the whole world. 

<margin>C</margin>
Therefore now, beloved, you who are loved by Christ, let us be of good courage and joyful in soul, so that we may listen and behold these great deeds of this most handsome youth without distraction.

<margin>D</margin>
And I say, my brothers, that this youth was not only most handsome, but also wonderful—a fountain of self-control <term>pudicitiæ/σωφροσύνης: a term implying both modesty and sexual purity</term>, a most excellent victor, and a marvelous trophy. For this reason, he was especially a <term>type: a person or event in the Old Testament that foreshadows someone or something in the New Testament</term> of that future coming of the Lord. Let each one, therefore, cast out from his own soul every concern for earthly things, and receive with longing these melodic songs; for they are spiritual things that gladden the soul.

<margin>E</margin>
For just as the Lord was sent to us from the Father’s bosom to save us all, so also the boy Joseph was sent from the bosom of his father Jacob to visit his own brothers. And just as the cruel brothers of Joseph, as soon as they saw him approaching, began to plot evil against him—even though he brought them a message of peace from their father—

<margin>Matth. 21.</margin>
<margin>F</margin>
so also the Jews, always hard of heart, as soon as they saw the Savior, said: "This is truly the heir; come, let us kill him, and everything will be ours." <note>This is a reference to the Parable of the Wicked Tenants found in Matthew 21:38.</note> And just as the brothers of Joseph said, "Let us make away with him and be rid of his dreams," in the same way the Jews said, "Come, let us kill him and seize his inheritance."

<summary>This page begins a sermon by Ephraem the Syrian using the story of the biblical Joseph as a symbolic "type" of Jesus Christ. It establishes parallels between Joseph being sent to his brothers and Christ being sent to the world, noting that both were met with murderous plots by their own people.</summary>
<keywords>Ephraem the Syrian, Joseph the All-Beautiful, Typology, Patriarch Jacob, Christology, Parables, Biblical Parallelism</keywords>

# SAINT EPHREM THE SYRIAN

<margin>A</margin> While eating together, they sold him—the one whom they had already slaughtered in their purpose and will. In the same way, the wretched Judeans, while eating the Passover, killed the Savior. Joseph’s descent into Egypt signifies our Savior’s descent to the earth. And just as Joseph, shut within the inner chamber, <note>original Latin: "thalamum"; likely referring to the bedroom of Potiphar's wife where he was tempted.</note> trampled upon all the power of sin and carried away the noble crowns of victory 
<margin>B</margin> against his Egyptian mistress, so also the Lord and Savior of our souls, descending into the underworld, <note>original: "infernum"</note> overthrew and destroyed by His right hand all the power of the most cruel and unconquered tyrant <note>A reference to the Devil or Death.</note> there. Because Joseph had overcome sin, he was kept confined in prison until the time arrived for him to receive his crown; so also our Lord, 
<margin>C</margin> in order to take away all the sins of the world, was placed in a tomb. Joseph spent a period of two years in prison, living there with great tranquility and security of mind; but our Lord remained for three days in the underworld as one who is powerful and subject to no corruption. By the command of Pharaoh, Joseph was calmly led out of prison as a true <term>type: a person or event in the Old Testament believed to foreshadow something in the life of Christ.</term> and as an easy and faithful interpreter of dreams, and a prophet of the future abundance of grain. But 
<margin>D</margin> our Lord Jesus Christ rose from the dead by His own power, and having snatched away the spoils of the underworld, offered our reconciliation to His Father, preaching resurrection and eternal life. Joseph, having received power over all Egypt, sat in the chariot of Pharaoh; but our Savior and King before the ages, ascending into heaven in a bright cloud, sits at the right hand of the Father with majesty and glory 
<margin>E</margin> above the Cherubim as the only-begotten Son. Now, while Joseph was already reigning in Egypt, having received authority and power over all the land, his brothers were brought voluntarily before the tribunal and throne of the one they had condemned to death; and they entered with fear and trembling to worship in the presence of him who had been sold into death by them. They fell down trembling before Joseph, whom they had not wanted to reign 
<margin>F</margin> over them. Once Joseph recognized his brothers, he showed them to be murderers with a single word. When they realized this, they stood astonished and struck with fear, plainly confused, not daring to speak, nor having anything at all to offer in excuse; instead, they openly recognized their sin committed in that hour when they sold him. But he who was thought by them to have been delivered to death in the underworld...

<margin>A</margin> to him. The brothers of Joseph, while eating together, sold him, having slaughtered him in their intention. In this same way, the detestable Judeans, while eating the Passover, slaughtered the Savior. The descent of Joseph into Egypt signifies the descent of our Savior upon the earth. And in the way that Joseph, inside the chamber, trampled all the strength of sin, putting on the brilliant prizes of victory over his Egyptian 
<margin>B</margin> mistress; so also our Lord, the Savior of our souls, by His own right hand, having descended into Hades, <note>original Greek: "ᾅδην" (Hades)</note> there dissolved all the power of the most terrible and hard-to-capture Tyrant. Since Joseph conquered sin, he was placed in custody until the hour of his crowning; so also our Lord, so that He might lift the sin of the world, 
<margin>C</margin> was placed in the tomb. Joseph spent a two-year time in custody, passing the time there in great safety; but our Lord remained three days in Hades as one who is powerful, not undergoing corruption. Joseph was brought out from prison by the command of Pharaoh cheerfully, as a true type interpreting easily the solution of dreams, 
<margin>D</margin> signifying the abundance that was to come. But our Lord Jesus Christ was raised from the dead by His own power, having despoiled Hades, offering to His Father our reconciliation, preaching resurrection and eternal life. Joseph sat in the chariot of Pharaoh, having received authority over all Egypt; but our Savior, 
<margin>E</margin> the King before the ages, having gone up into the heavens in a cloud of light, sits at the right hand of the Father with glory, above the Cherubim, as the only-begotten Son. While Joseph was reigning in Egypt, having received authority over his enemies, his brothers were led voluntarily before the judgment seat of the one who had died <note>i.e., died in their eyes/intentions</note> by their hand; they were led to worship with fear and trembling before the one sold 
<margin>F</margin> by them into death. And in fear they worshiped in the presence of Joseph, whom they did not wish to reign over them. Joseph, having recognized his brothers, revealed them as murderers in a single word of his. Having learned this, they stood speechless in great agony, not daring to utter a sound, having absolutely nothing to say in defense, knowing accurately their own sin in that hour in which they sold him. But he who seemed among them to have been destroyed in

### ON THE PRAISES OF BLESSED JOSEPH

<margin>A</margin> ...suddenly, he was found to be reigning over them. So too on that fearsome day, when the Lord shall come in the clouds of the air, sitting upon the throne of his kingdom, all his enemies—as many as did not wish him to reign over them—shall be led as prisoners by fearsome angels before his judgment seat. For the lawless <note>Greek: ἄνομοι (anomoi); Latin: impii. Used here to describe those who rejected Jesus's divinity.</note> Jews thought then that if he were crucified, he would die like a mere man; those wretched ones were not persuaded that <margin>B</margin> he is in fact God, having come for salvation and to save our souls. Just as Joseph spoke with boldness to his brothers, terrifying them and making them tremble, saying: "I am Joseph, whom you sold into slavery, but now I reign over you, even though you did not wish it"; so also the Lord shows the cross in a radiant form to those who crucified him, and they will recognize the <margin>C</margin> cross itself, and the Son of God who was crucified by them. Do you see how accurately Joseph became a true **type** <note>In theology, a "type" is a person or event in the Old Testament seen as a foreshadowing of Christ.</note> of the Lord’s own sovereignty?

Since his virtue blossomed from a young age by his own free will, we shall now begin to explain the virtues of this holy youth. This blessed boy spent seventeen years in his father’s house, <margin>D</margin> progressing every day in the fear of God, in a good way of life, and in honor of his parents. But seeing the misconduct of his brothers, he reported to his father a small portion of their many deeds. For truly, virtue cannot be in harmony with injustice; they are incompatible. It was for this reason that they hated Joseph, because he was a stranger to their wickedness. Being a virtuous boy, he had dreams and saw those things that were to happen <margin>E</margin> to him by the divine <term>economy</term> <note>From the Greek 'oikonomia,' meaning God's plan or administration of human history for the purpose of salvation.</note> of God Most High. But his father Jacob, not knowing the secret hatred held against Joseph, loved Joseph with a simple heart because of the flower of virtue that always shone in him from his youth. Now, while they were grazing the sheep in Shechem, it happened that Joseph was with his father; and his father Jacob, as a tender-hearted father, was concerned for those in Shechem. And <margin>F</margin> he said to Joseph: "Come, child, go to your brothers and inquire accurately after their health, as well as the flocks; then return quickly." Joseph, having received his father's command, departed with joy toward his brothers, bringing a message of peace from his father, as well as the concern his father had for them. As he was going...

<summary>This page presents Joseph as a "type" of Christ, comparing his unexpected rule over his brothers to Christ’s future reign at the Last Judgment. It also narrates Joseph's early life, highlighting how his virtuous nature and his report of his brothers' misconduct led to their secret hatred of him.</summary>

## ENGLISH TRANSLATION (From the Latin)

<margin>A</margin>
...he was wandering on the way, having not found his brothers or their flocks. While he was walking, sad and sighing on their account, a man happened upon him and showed him the way that led to his brothers. When Joseph saw them from a distance, he hurried toward them with joy, desiring to embrace them all with great love. But they, as soon as they saw him approaching, began to deliberate about killing him, like fierce and monstrous wild beasts.

<margin>B</margin>
Joseph proceeded, therefore, like an innocent lamb about to be torn apart by the hands of most cruel wolves. When he finally reached them, he greeted them charmingly and kindly, offering them peace on behalf of their father. But they immediately, like wild animals, rose up and stripped him of the <term>variegated tunic</term><note>The famous "coat of many colors" (Latin: tunica variegata), a symbol of Jacob's favoritism toward Joseph.</note> that he was wearing; each of them gnashed their teeth at him, desiring to swallow him alive. Fierce and merciless, to satisfy their hatred, they struck that chaste and holy boy with various blows in their madness.

<margin>C</margin>
Then Joseph, seeing himself placed in such great evils (for there was clearly no one who would take pity on him), turned himself to prayers, tears, and sighs; raising his voice, he questioned them, saying: "Why are you so angry?

<margin>D</margin>
I implore you all, bear with me a little while I pray and entreat you, my brothers. My mother is dead, and our father Jacob mourns her every day until now; do you wish to bring another sorrow to our father while the first still lasts and has not yet ceased? I implore you all, bear with me a little, lest I be separated from father Jacob, and lest his old age descend with sorrow to <term>the grave</term><note>Latin: "ad infernum," referring here to the Hebrew concept of Sheol, the place of the dead, rather than a place of punishment.</note>."

<margin>E</margin>
<margin>Gen. 12.</margin>
"I adjure you all by the God of our fathers, Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob; who from the beginning called Abraham and said to him: *Go out from your land, and from your kindred, and from your father’s house; and come into the land which I will show and give to you; and I will multiply your seed like the stars of heaven, and like the innumerable sand which is on the shore of the sea.*"

## ENGLISH TRANSLATION (From the Greek)

<margin>A</margin>
...him, seeing him wandering, not finding the brothers with their shepherds. And while he was grieving and sighing for them, a man found him and showed him the way. When Joseph saw them from afar, he went with joy, longing to greet them all. But as he approached, they saw him and, like wild beasts, they wanted to destroy Joseph.

<margin>B</margin>
He went like an innocent lamb to fall into the hands of the most bitter wolves. When he drew near, he greeted them as friends, bringing peace from the presence of their father. But they, rising up immediately like wild beasts, stripped off the <term>tunic of many colors</term><note>Greek: χιτῶνα ποικίλον (chiton poikilon), the diverse or embroidered garment.</note> which he had put on, and each one gnashed their teeth to swallow him alive. Cruel and unpitying in their hatred, they outlandishly mistreated that venerable and holy youth in their own madness.

<margin>C</margin>
But Joseph, seeing himself to be in the midst of evils, and there being absolutely no one who had compassion, resorted finally to petition, and put forward tears and sighs, and lifting up his voice he begged them, saying: "Why are you angry? I entreat you all, endure me a little while, so that I may pray, my brothers."

<margin>D</margin>
"My mother has fallen asleep; Jacob mourns her until now every day. Do you wish to bring another mourning to our father, while the former remains and has not yet ceased? I entreat you all to endure me a little, so that I may not be separated from Jacob, and his old age might not go down with pain to <term>Hades</term><note>Greek: ᾅδου (Haidou), the underworld or grave.</note>."

<margin>E</margin>
"Therefore I adjure you all by the God of our fathers, Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, who from the beginning called Abraham and said: *Go out from your land, and from your kindred, and from the house of your father, and come to the land which I shall show you. And I will grant it, and I will multiply your seed as the stars of heaven, and as the sand by the lip of the sea, which is innumerable.*"

<margin>F</margin>
"By God the Most High, who gave endurance to Abraham to offer his only-begotten son Isaac eagerly for sacrifice, so that the endurance might be reckoned to Abraham for a boast. By the God who went down with Jacob in Haran, into Mesopotamia, from where Abraham went out. By the God who rescued Jacob from tribul-"

For no longer will you see my face, nor hear my voice, nor will your old age lean upon me any longer; nor will I in turn look upon your holy white hair: since my condition now is no better than that of a dead man who has been buried. Lament, father, for your son, and let the son in turn weep for you: for thus as a boy have I been separated from your face." |

My tears and groans have failed, father, and my very voice has plainly grown faint, nor is there anyone to help. O Earth, O Earth, you who cried out to the holy God for righteous Abel who was unjustly slain, as we have received from the tradition of our elders and fathers—that the earth indeed cried out to God because of the blood of the righteous; now you also cry out to my father Jacob, clearly and openly indicating to him what has happened to me from my brothers." <margin>Gen. 4</margin> |

And just as a victor is accustomed to rejoice over conquered and fleeing enemies: |
